1 How the SGT paper behaves, and how to prep it

Before any content, get the shape of the thing right, because half of prepping an entrance is knowing what you are walking into. SGT University runs its own offline entrance for the MA Clinical Psychology (RCI) programme at its Gurugram campus, in two stages: a written test followed by a personal interview. Unlike RML's CET-166, there is no standalone syllabus document, no published breakdown of number of questions, marks per question, duration, or negative marking. SGT's own pages say only that the syllabus follows UGC guidelines. That is not a gap in your prep; it is the design. A UGC-guideline general-psychology aptitude test means the paper samples broadly and shallowly across the whole PG-psychology base rather than drilling deep into one niche.

Key. No published SGT syllabus is not a reason to panic; it is a reason to prep breadth over depth. The paper rewards the person who can recognise a term, a theorist, or a test from any corner of psychology, not the person who has memorised one topic to the bone.

The seven areas the written test draws on

Mapping the "UGC-guideline general psychology" wording onto how these general aptitude papers actually behave, the written test pulls from seven areas. This book has one section per area, in this order:

#AreaWhat it samples
2Biopsychology / physiologicalNeuron, action potential, neurotransmitters, brain structures, endocrine, sensation & perception
3DevelopmentalJean Piaget, Erik Erikson, Lev Vygotsky, attachment, moral & lifespan development
4Abnormal / clinicalDSM-5-TR & ICD-11, the major disorders, etiology, case vignettes
5Assessment / testingReliability & validity, intelligence & personality tests, projectives, Indian adaptations
6Psychotherapy & counsellingPsychoanalytic, behavioural, CBT, humanistic; who developed which therapy
7Statistics & research methodologyDescriptive & inferential stats, designs, sampling, ethics
8General awareness / aptitudeHistory & schools of psychology, learning, memory, social psychology, key theorists
Why this order. Biopsychology and developmental are the highest-density "recognise the fact" areas in any general paper, so they lead. Assessment and therapy are where the "who made this" questions cluster, so full names are non-negotiable there. General awareness is last because it is the catch-all that ties the schools and theorists together once you have met them in the earlier sections.
Trap. Do not confuse SGT's general aptitude test with a deep clinical M.Phil paper. A general-psych entrance will ask "who proposed the hierarchy of needs" far more often than it asks a fine-grained ICD-11 sub-code. Prep the recognisable landmarks of every area, not the deep cuts of one.

How to spend the days you have

  1. Breadth pass first. Read all fourteen chapters once, front to back, so no area is a blank. That alone clears most of a general aptitude paper.
  2. Second pass on the founder boxes. The teal Who → what boxes are the single most exam-efficient thing here; a general paper is dense with "match the theorist / test / therapy to its author."
  3. Use your existing mocks as the drill. Everything on the site (the PYQ mocks, the common-section work, the bio and diagnostic sets) is the same base SGT samples. Sitting any of them is valid SGT practice.
  4. Prep the interview in parallel (Section 9): the second stage is a real gate, and it is the cheapest marks to secure because it rewards clarity about why you want clinical psychology, not more recall.

2 Biopsychology & Physiological Psychology

Biopsychology (also called physiological psychology, biological psychology, or behavioural neuroscience) asks one question: how does the physical machinery of the body, above all the nervous system, produce behaviour, thought, emotion, and experience? To answer an entrance paper on this topic you must be able to build the brain from its smallest working part upward. We therefore start with the single cell, the neuron, and end with whole-brain imaging. Nothing here needs prior biology; every term is defined as it appears.

2.1 The Neuron: The Unit of the Nervous System

The nervous system is built from billions of specialised cells called neurons (nerve cells) that receive, integrate, and transmit information as electrical and chemical signals. A neuron is not like an ordinary body cell; it is shaped for communication, with parts that collect signals at one end and pass them on at the other.

Parts of a neuron

  • Soma (cell body): the metabolic centre. It holds the nucleus and keeps the cell alive. Incoming signals are summed here.
  • Dendrites: short, branching fibres that spread out from the soma like the branches of a tree (the word means "little tree"). They are the receiving surface; they collect signals from other neurons.
  • Axon: a single long fibre that carries the outgoing signal away from the soma toward other cells. One neuron has many dendrites but usually just one axon (which may branch at its end).
  • Myelin sheath: a fatty white insulating layer wrapped in segments around many axons. It speeds up signal conduction and stops the signal leaking. Myelin is why "white matter" is white.
  • Nodes of Ranvier: the small unmyelinated gaps between segments of myelin. The signal jumps from node to node (see saltatory conduction).
  • Terminal buttons (axon terminals, end feet): the swollen tips at the end of the axon branches. They store and release chemical messengers onto the next cell.
Why. The neuron's shape is a receive-integrate-transmit pipeline: dendrites in, soma decides, axon out, terminals hand off. Every fact about signalling maps onto this direction of flow, so memorise the direction, not just the parts.

Glial cells (neuroglia): the support staff

Neurons are outnumbered by glial cells ("glia" means glue), which support, feed, insulate, and protect neurons. They do not fire signals themselves but are essential.

  • Astrocytes: star-shaped cells that hold neurons in place, supply nutrients, mop up excess chemicals, and help form the blood-brain barrier.
  • Oligodendrocytes: make the myelin sheath in the central nervous system (brain and spinal cord). One oligodendrocyte myelinates several axons.
  • Schwann cells: make the myelin sheath in the peripheral nervous system (nerves outside the brain and cord). One Schwann cell myelinates one segment of one axon, and Schwann cells help peripheral nerves regenerate.
  • Microglia: the immune cells of the nervous system; they clean up dead cells and fight infection.
Trap. Papers love to swap myelin-makers: oligodendrocytes myelinate the CNS, Schwann cells myelinate the PNS. If a question mentions the brain or spinal cord, the answer is oligodendrocyte; if it mentions a peripheral nerve, the answer is Schwann.

The neuron doctrine: Golgi versus Cajal

In the late 1800s scientists argued whether the nervous system was one continuous web (the "reticular theory") or a set of separate cells. Camillo Golgi, an Italian physician, invented a silver-staining method (the "black reaction") that made single neurons visible, yet he believed the network was continuous. The Spaniard Santiago Ramon y Cajal used Golgi's own stain to show that neurons are separate, individual cells that merely communicate across tiny gaps. Cajal's view, the neuron doctrine, won.

Who → what. Camillo Golgi → the silver stain that revealed neurons (but he backed the continuous-network theory). Santiago Ramon y Cajal → the neuron doctrine (neurons are discrete cells). They shared the 1906 Nobel Prize despite disagreeing.

2.2 Resting and Action Potentials: How a Neuron Fires

The resting potential

A neuron at rest is electrically charged, like a tiny battery. The inside of the axon is about −70 millivolts (mV) relative to the outside; this is the resting membrane potential. It exists because charged particles (ions) are unevenly spread across the membrane: sodium (Na+) is concentrated outside, potassium (K+) inside, and the inside carries a net negative charge.

Key. Resting potential = about −70 mV, inside negative. The neuron is "polarised" and ready to fire.

This imbalance is maintained by the sodium-potassium pump, a protein in the membrane that constantly pushes 3 sodium ions out for every 2 potassium ions it pulls in, using energy. Because it moves more positive charge out than in, it helps keep the inside negative.

The action potential (the nerve impulse)

When enough incoming signals push the membrane past a threshold (about −55 mV), the neuron fires an action potential, a rapid electrical spike that travels down the axon. It has clear phases:

  1. Depolarisation: sodium channels open, Na+ rushes in, and the inside shoots up toward +30 to +40 mV (briefly positive).
  2. Repolarisation: sodium channels close, potassium channels open, K+ flows out, and the charge drops back toward negative.
  3. Hyperpolarisation (undershoot): the charge briefly overshoots below −70 mV before the pump restores the resting state.
Key. The all-or-none law: a neuron either fires a full action potential or none at all. The strength of a stimulus is coded by how often the neuron fires (frequency), not by making a bigger spike. Every spike is the same size.

Refractory periods

  • Absolute refractory period: immediately after firing, the neuron cannot fire again no matter how strong the stimulus (sodium channels are inactivated).
  • Relative refractory period: shortly after, the neuron can fire again but only with a stronger-than-usual stimulus.
Why. The refractory period forces the impulse to travel in one direction (down the axon, not backward) and sets an upper limit on firing rate.

Saltatory conduction

In myelinated axons the impulse does not crawl smoothly; it jumps from one node of Ranvier to the next (Latin saltare, to leap). This saltatory conduction is much faster and more energy-efficient than conduction along an unmyelinated fibre.

Trap. When myelin is destroyed (as in multiple sclerosis), conduction slows and fails; saltatory conduction cannot occur without intact myelin and nodes. Do not confuse the nodes (bare gaps) with the myelin (insulation): the impulse jumps to the nodes because the myelinated stretches are skipped.

2.3 Synaptic Transmission: How Neurons Talk to Each Other

Neurons do not touch. Between the terminal button of one neuron and the dendrite of the next lies a microscopic gap, the synapse (synaptic cleft). The signal crosses this gap chemically.

  1. The action potential reaches the terminal button of the presynaptic (sending) neuron.
  2. Calcium enters and causes tiny sacs called synaptic vesicles to move to the membrane.
  3. By exocytosis the vesicles fuse with the membrane and dump their neurotransmitter (chemical messenger) into the cleft.
  4. The neurotransmitter drifts across and binds to receptors on the postsynaptic (receiving) neuron, like a key in a lock.
  5. This opens channels and changes the postsynaptic cell's charge.
  6. The signal is then cleared by reuptake (the presynaptic neuron sucks the transmitter back in for reuse) or by enzymes that break it down.

Excitation and inhibition

  • EPSP (excitatory postsynaptic potential): the transmitter makes the postsynaptic cell more likely to fire (depolarises it toward threshold).
  • IPSP (inhibitory postsynaptic potential): the transmitter makes it less likely to fire (hyperpolarises it away from threshold).
Why. A neuron is a voting machine: it constantly adds up EPSPs and IPSPs arriving on its dendrites and soma (this is "summation"). Only if excitation outweighs inhibition past threshold does it fire. Behaviour is the net result of this balance across billions of cells.

The discovery of neurotransmission

Who → what. Otto Loewi → first proof of chemical neurotransmission. In a famous 1921 frog-heart experiment (reportedly designed in a dream) he stimulated the vagus nerve of one heart, collected its fluid, applied it to a second heart, and slowed that heart too. He named the substance "Vagusstoff" ("vagus substance"), later identified as acetylcholine, the first known neurotransmitter. Charles Sherrington → coined the term "synapse" and described reflex integration.

2.4 Major Neurotransmitters

Different chemical messengers do different jobs, and many disorders and drugs work by changing one of them. Learn the function and the clinical link for each; entrance papers almost always test the link.

NeurotransmitterMain functionDisorder / drug link
Acetylcholine (ACh)Muscle movement, learning, memory, arousalLoss of ACh neurons in Alzheimer’s disease; blocked at the junction in myasthenia gravis
DopamineReward, motivation, voluntary movement, pleasureToo much (overactivity) linked to schizophrenia; too little (loss of cells) causes Parkinson’s disease
SerotoninMood, sleep, appetite, impulse controlLow levels linked to depression and anxiety; SSRIs raise it
Norepinephrine (noradrenaline)Alertness, arousal, fight-or-flightInvolved in mood disorders and stress; targeted by some antidepressants
GABA (gamma-aminobutyric acid)The main inhibitory transmitter; calms neural activityLow activity linked to anxiety and seizures; boosted by benzodiazepines and alcohol
GlutamateThe main excitatory transmitter; learning and memoryExcess causes excitotoxicity (cell death from over-firing), implicated in stroke and seizures
EndorphinsNatural painkillers; pleasure and "runner’s high"Mimicked by opiate drugs (morphine, heroin), which bind endorphin receptors
Key. Memorise the four highest-yield pairings: dopamine → schizophrenia (too much) and Parkinson’s (too little); serotonin → depression; GABA → anxiety (the calming brake); acetylcholine → Alzheimer’s and memory.
Trap. GABA is inhibitory (the brain’s brake) and glutamate is excitatory (the accelerator). Papers reverse these constantly. Also note dopamine is the classic "two-disease" transmitter: excess → schizophrenia, deficit → Parkinson’s, which is why anti-schizophrenia drugs can cause Parkinson-like side effects.

2.5 Organisation of the Nervous System

The whole nervous system splits into two great divisions:

  • Central nervous system (CNS): the brain and the spinal cord. This is the control centre and integrator.
  • Peripheral nervous system (PNS): all the nerves outside the CNS that connect it to the body and outside world.

The PNS divides again:

  • Somatic nervous system: carries sensory information in and controls voluntary skeletal muscles (moving your arm).
  • Autonomic nervous system (ANS): controls involuntary functions of glands and internal organs (heartbeat, digestion). It has two opposing branches.
SituationSympathetic branchParasympathetic branch
Role"Fight or flight" (arouses, spends energy)"Rest and digest" (calms, conserves energy)
Heart rateSpeeds upSlows down
PupilsDilate (widen)Constrict (narrow)
DigestionInhibitedStimulated
Trap. Sympathetic and parasympathetic are opposites, but both belong to the autonomic system. A common wrong answer treats the sympathetic system as "voluntary"; it is involuntary. Voluntary muscle control is the somatic system.

The spinal cord and the reflex arc

The spinal cord relays messages between brain and body, but it can also act on its own in a reflex arc, a fast, automatic response that bypasses the brain. When you touch a hot stove: a sensory (afferent) neuron carries the pain signal to the spinal cord, an interneuron passes it across, and a motor (efferent) neuron orders the muscle to pull back, all before the brain even registers the pain.

Why. Routing simple protective reflexes through the spinal cord alone saves the time it would take to consult the brain; the brain is informed a fraction of a second later. This is why you yank your hand away before you feel the burn.

2.6 The Brain

The brain is traditionally divided into hindbrain, midbrain, and forebrain, roughly from bottom-and-back (older, survival functions) to top-and-front (newer, higher functions).

Hindbrain

  • Medulla (medulla oblongata): controls vital automatic functions, heartbeat, breathing, blood pressure. Damage is fatal.
  • Pons: a bridge relaying signals between higher and lower brain; involved in sleep, arousal, and facial movement.
  • Cerebellum ("little brain"): coordinates balance, posture, and smooth, well-timed voluntary movement; also involved in some learning. Damage causes clumsy, jerky movement.

Midbrain

A relay hub for vision and hearing reflexes and for movement. It contains parts of the reticular formation, a network running through the brainstem that controls arousal, alertness, and the sleep-wake cycle.

Forebrain and the limbic system

The forebrain contains the most advanced structures. Beneath the cortex sits the limbic system, the emotional and motivational core:

  • Amygdala: the fear and emotion centre; tags experiences as threatening; central to aggression and emotional learning.
  • Hippocampus: forms new long-term memories and supports spatial navigation. Damage causes an inability to form new memories.
  • Hypothalamus: the body’s regulator; controls hunger, thirst, temperature, sex drive, and it commands the pituitary gland (the link between brain and hormones). Remember the "four Fs": feeding, fighting, fleeing, and sexual behaviour.
  • Thalamus: the great relay station; nearly all sensory information (except smell) passes through the thalamus on its way to the cortex.
Trap. Do not confuse hippocampus (memory formation) with hypothalamus (drives and hormones), or thalamus (sensory relay) with hypothalamus (regulation and pituitary control). Amygdala = emotion/fear; hippocampus = memory. These four are the most-tested structures in the whole section.

The cerebral cortex: four lobes

The wrinkled outer layer of the forebrain, the cerebral cortex, is where perception, thought, and language happen. Each hemisphere has four lobes:

LobeLocationMain function
FrontalFrontPlanning, decision-making, personality, voluntary movement (motor cortex), speech production
ParietalTop-backTouch, temperature, pain, body position (somatosensory cortex)
TemporalSides (near temples)Hearing, language comprehension, memory
OccipitalBackVision (visual cortex)

Language areas and hemispheric specialisation

Who → what. Paul Broca → Broca’s area (in the left frontal lobe), for speech production; damage causes Broca’s aphasia (halting, effortful speech, comprehension intact). Carl Wernicke → Wernicke’s area (in the left temporal lobe), for language comprehension; damage causes Wernicke’s aphasia (fluent but meaningless speech, poor comprehension). Roger Sperry → split-brain research on hemispheric specialisation (Nobel Prize 1981).

The two hemispheres are somewhat specialised (lateralisation): for most people the left hemisphere handles language, logic, and analysis, while the right hemisphere handles spatial ability, faces, and holistic processing. They are joined by the corpus callosum, a thick band of fibres. Roger Sperry studied "split-brain" patients whose corpus callosum was cut to treat epilepsy; his experiments showed the two halves could then work independently, revealing what each side does on its own.

Trap. Broca = broken, halting speech (production); Wernicke = wordy, fluent nonsense (comprehension). Both areas are in the left hemisphere for most people. Also: each hemisphere controls the opposite side of the body (contralateral control).

2.7 The Endocrine System

Alongside the fast electrical nervous system runs a slower chemical one: the endocrine system, a set of glands that release hormones directly into the bloodstream to act on distant organs.

  • Pituitary gland: the "master gland", sitting under the hypothalamus. It releases hormones that control the other endocrine glands (and it is itself controlled by the hypothalamus).
  • Adrenal glands: release adrenaline (epinephrine) for fight-or-flight and cortisol for stress.
  • Thyroid gland: releases thyroxine, which sets the body’s metabolic rate.
  • Pineal gland: releases melatonin, which regulates sleep and the day-night (circadian) cycle.

The HPA axis and key hormones

The stress response runs on the HPA axis: the Hypothalamus signals the Pituitary, which signals the Adrenal glands to release cortisol. This is the body’s slow, sustained stress system.

  • Cortisol: the main stress hormone; mobilises energy but harms health if chronically high.
  • Adrenaline (epinephrine): the fast surge that races the heart in an emergency.
  • Oxytocin: the "bonding" hormone; released in childbirth, breastfeeding, and social attachment.
  • Thyroxine: controls metabolism and energy use.
  • Melatonin: promotes sleep and tracks the light-dark cycle.
Why. Nervous versus endocrine is a speed-versus-duration trade-off. The nervous system sends fast electrochemical signals down fixed wires for immediate, brief effects; the endocrine system sends slower hormones through the blood for widespread, longer-lasting effects. The hypothalamus is the bridge that lets the brain command the hormones.
Trap. The pituitary is the "master gland", but it takes orders from the hypothalamus (a brain structure, not a gland in the usual sense). So the true top of the chain is the hypothalamus, which is why the same structure keeps appearing in both the brain and endocrine sections.

2.8 Sensation and Perception: The Basics

Sensation is the raw detection of a stimulus by a sense organ; perception is the brain’s interpretation of that sensation into a meaningful experience. Sense organs first perform transduction: converting physical energy (light, sound, pressure) into the neural signals the brain can read.

Thresholds and psychophysics

  • Absolute threshold: the smallest amount of a stimulus a person can detect 50% of the time (e.g. a candle flame seen far away on a dark night).
  • Difference threshold (just noticeable difference, JND): the smallest change in a stimulus that can be detected.
Who → what. Ernst Weber → Weber’s law: the JND is a constant proportion of the original stimulus, not a fixed amount (adding one candle to ten is noticeable; adding one to a hundred is not). Gustav Fechner → founded psychophysics and extended Weber’s work into a law relating physical stimulus strength to perceived sensation.

The visual pathway

Light enters the eye and lands on the retina, which holds two kinds of receptor cells:

  • Rods: many, at the edges; work in dim light; see in black and white; give night vision.
  • Cones: concentrated in the centre (fovea); work in bright light; give colour and sharp detail.

Signals leave the eye by the optic nerve and travel to the visual cortex in the occipital lobe. Two classic theories explain colour vision:

Who → what. Thomas Young and Hermann von Helmholtz → the trichromatic (three-colour) theory: the retina has three cone types tuned to red, green, and blue, and all colours arise from their combined activity. Ewald Hering → the opponent-process theory: colour is coded by opposing pairs (red-green, blue-yellow, black-white); one member of a pair is excited while the other is inhibited.
Trap. The two colour theories are not rivals; both are correct at different stages. Trichromatic (Young-Helmholtz) operates at the cones (retina); opponent-process (Hering) operates later, in the retinal ganglion cells and brain. Opponent-process best explains afterimages (stare at green, look away, see red).

Auditory basics

Sound waves enter the ear, vibrate the eardrum, pass through three tiny bones, and reach the fluid-filled cochlea in the inner ear. Hair cells in the cochlea transduce the vibrations into neural signals sent along the auditory nerve to the temporal lobe. Pitch corresponds to the frequency of the wave and loudness to its amplitude (measured in decibels).

2.9 Research Methods in Biopsychology

To study a brain we cannot open, biopsychologists use recording, imaging, and damage-based methods, plus genetics to separate nature from nurture.

Recording and imaging techniques

MethodWhat it doesShows structure or function?
EEG (electroencephalogram)Records electrical brain waves from scalp electrodes; excellent timingFunction (activity over time)
ERP (event-related potential)Averages EEG responses time-locked to a specific stimulus to isolate the brain’s reaction to itFunction (precise timing)
CT (computed tomography)X-ray scan giving a still picture of brain anatomyStructure
PET (positron emission tomography)Tracks a radioactive tracer to show which areas use most glucose/bloodFunction (metabolic activity)
fMRI (functional MRI)Tracks blood-oxygen flow to map active regions; good spatial detailBoth, but prized for function
Lesion studiesObserve behaviour after a brain area is damaged or removed to infer its functionFunction (by loss)
Trap. Sort methods by their strength: EEG and ERP have superb temporal resolution (when) but poor spatial detail (where); fMRI and PET have good spatial resolution (where) but slower timing. CT gives structure only; fMRI and PET show function. Lesion evidence shows what a region is needed for, not everything it does.

Behaviour genetics

Behaviour genetics studies how much of a trait comes from genes versus environment.

  • Twin studies: compare identical (monozygotic, 100% shared genes) with fraternal (dizygotic, ~50% shared) twins. If identical twins are far more alike on a trait, genes matter more.
  • Adoption studies: compare adopted children with both their biological and their adoptive parents to separate genetic from environmental influence.
  • Heritability: the proportion of the variation in a trait across a population that is due to genetic differences. It ranges from 0 to 1.
Trap. Heritability is about variation within a population, not about a single individual. A heritability of 0.5 does not mean "half of your intelligence comes from genes"; it means half of the differences among people in that group trace to genetic differences. This distinction is a favourite trick question.
Recap. Neuron = dendrites (in) → soma (integrate) → axon/myelin (out) → terminals; oligodendrocytes myelinate the CNS, Schwann cells the PNS; Cajal’s neuron doctrine beat Golgi’s network (they shared the 1906 Nobel). Resting potential −70 mV kept by the sodium-potassium pump; the action potential is all-or-none (depolarise, repolarise), jumps node to node (saltatory). Otto Loewi proved chemical transmission (acetylcholine, "Vagusstoff"). Key transmitter links: dopamine → schizophrenia (excess) and Parkinson’s (deficit), serotonin → depression, GABA (inhibitory) → anxiety, glutamate (excitatory) → excitotoxicity, ACh → Alzheimer’s. CNS vs PNS; somatic (voluntary) vs autonomic (sympathetic fight-or-flight vs parasympathetic rest-and-digest); the spinal reflex arc bypasses the brain. Brain: medulla (vital signs), cerebellum (coordination); limbic amygdala (fear), hippocampus (memory), hypothalamus (drives + pituitary), thalamus (sensory relay); four lobes (frontal, parietal, temporal, occipital); Broca (speech production) vs Wernicke (comprehension), both left; Sperry’s split-brain work. Pituitary is master gland under the hypothalamus; HPA axis releases cortisol. Weber-Fechner thresholds; rods (dim, night) vs cones (colour, detail); Young-Helmholtz trichromatic (cones) plus Hering opponent-process (later, explains afterimages). EEG/ERP = timing, fMRI/PET = location, CT = structure; twin and adoption studies define heritability as population-level variation, not an individual’s fixed fraction.

2.10 Sleep, consciousness and biological rhythms

Consciousness is the awareness a person has of their own thoughts, feelings and surroundings at any given moment. It is not a single fixed state but a continuum that shifts across the day and night, from alert wakefulness through drowsiness into the various stages of sleep. Sleep is not simply the switching off of the brain. It is an active, organised, rhythmic process that the body drives on a roughly 24-hour schedule, and studying it reveals how deeply behaviour is governed by biology.

Biological rhythms and the circadian clock

A biological rhythm is any cyclical change in a bodily process that repeats over a regular period. The most important for behaviour is the circadian rhythm, from the Latin circa (about) and dies (a day), meaning a cycle that lasts about 24 hours. The sleep-wake cycle, body temperature, and the release of many hormones all follow a circadian pattern. Rhythms shorter than a day are called ultradian (for example the stages of a single sleep cycle repeating through the night), and rhythms longer than a day are called infradian (for example the roughly monthly menstrual cycle).

The internal clock that keeps the circadian rhythm running is a tiny cluster of cells in the hypothalamus called the suprachiasmatic nucleus, usually shortened to the SCN. It sits just above the point where the optic nerves cross, which is why it can receive information about light directly from the eyes. Even with no external cues the SCN keeps ticking on a cycle close to, but not exactly, 24 hours. External cues that reset the clock to the true day are called zeitgebers, a German word meaning time-givers. Light is the most powerful zeitgeber.

Who → what. The SCN was identified as the master circadian pacemaker through animal lesion studies in the 1970s: destroying it abolished the regular sleep-wake and hormonal rhythms, showing that this one small structure sets the body's daily timing.

When light falls on the retina, signals travel to the SCN, which then instructs the pineal gland, a small structure deep in the brain, to adjust its output of the hormone melatonin. Melatonin promotes sleepiness. In darkness the pineal gland releases more melatonin, and in bright light it releases less. This is the chemical bridge between the light of the outside world and the felt urge to sleep.

Key. Light → eye → suprachiasmatic nucleus (the clock) → pineal gland → melatonin (the sleep signal). Darkness raises melatonin; light lowers it.

The sleep cycle: NREM and REM

Sleep is measured chiefly with the electroencephalograph, or EEG, a device that records the electrical activity of the brain through electrodes on the scalp. As a person falls asleep and moves through the night, the EEG traces out characteristic patterns of brain waves that let researchers divide sleep into distinct stages. Sleep falls into two broad kinds: NREM (non-rapid-eye-movement) sleep and REM (rapid-eye-movement) sleep.

Under the modern scheme, NREM sleep is divided into three stages, N1 to N3, followed by a period of REM. One full pass through these stages takes roughly 90 minutes, and the cycle repeats four to six times a night (an ultradian rhythm). As the night goes on, the deep NREM stages shorten and the REM periods lengthen.

  1. Stage N1 (light sleep): the brief transition from wakefulness into sleep. Brain waves slow from alpha toward theta. The person is easily woken and may experience sudden jerks.
  2. Stage N2: a deeper light sleep marked on the EEG by short bursts of activity called sleep spindles and single large waves called K-complexes. Most of a night is spent here.
  3. Stage N3 (slow-wave or deep sleep): the deepest sleep, dominated by large slow delta waves. It is hardest to wake someone from this stage, and it is thought to be the most physically restorative. Sleepwalking and night terrors, when they occur, arise here.
  4. REM sleep: after the deep stages the brain returns to a fast, active pattern resembling waking. The eyes dart rapidly beneath closed lids, and the body's voluntary muscles are effectively paralysed. Most vivid dreaming happens here.

EEG wave patterns

Each state of consciousness has a signature brain-wave pattern, described by its frequency (how many cycles per second, measured in hertz) and amplitude (how tall the waves are). As a rough rule, the more alert and active the brain, the faster and smaller the waves; the deeper the rest, the slower and larger the waves.

WaveFrequencyTypical state
BetaFast (about 13 to 30 Hz)Alert, awake, actively thinking or concentrating
AlphaModerate (about 8 to 12 Hz)Awake but relaxed, calm, eyes often closed; drowsy onset
ThetaSlow (about 4 to 7 Hz)Light sleep, stages N1 and N2
DeltaVery slow (below about 4 Hz), high amplitudeDeep slow-wave sleep, stage N3
Trap. Do not confuse fast small beta waves (busy waking mind) with slow tall delta waves (deepest sleep). A common exam catch is that REM sleep shows a fast, waking-like EEG even though the person is deeply asleep and hard to wake; this mismatch is why REM is called paradoxical sleep.

REM, paradoxical sleep and dreaming

REM sleep is called paradoxical sleep because the brain looks awake on the EEG while the body is at its most still, the large muscles being temporarily paralysed (a state called atonia that stops us acting out dreams). Rapid eye movements, irregular breathing and heart rate, and vivid narrative dreams all belong to REM. People woken from REM report dreaming far more often and in more detail than those woken from NREM. Newborns spend a very large proportion of sleep in REM, which has led to the idea that REM supports brain development and the consolidation of memory.

Theories of why we sleep

There is no single agreed answer to why sleep evolved, but two broad families of theory dominate.

  • Restorative theory: sleep serves to repair and restore the body and brain. During sleep, especially deep NREM, the body releases growth hormone, repairs tissue, clears metabolic waste, and the brain consolidates memories. On this view we sleep because sleep does necessary maintenance.
  • Evolutionary or adaptive theory: sleep evolved to keep an animal safe and to conserve energy during the hours when it cannot forage or defend itself effectively. On this view sleep is an adaptation that reduced risk and saved calories, and different species sleep in patterns suited to their ecological niche.

Common sleep disorders in brief

  • Insomnia: persistent difficulty falling asleep, staying asleep, or getting restful sleep, despite adequate opportunity, causing daytime distress or impairment.
  • Narcolepsy: a neurological disorder marked by overwhelming daytime sleepiness and sudden uncontrollable sleep attacks, sometimes with sudden loss of muscle tone (cataplexy) triggered by emotion, reflecting intrusions of REM-like states into wakefulness.
  • Sleep apnoea: repeated pauses in breathing during sleep, often because the airway collapses, which briefly wakes the person again and again and leaves them unrefreshed and sleepy by day.

2.11 Behavioural genetics and evolution

Behavioural genetics is the field that studies how much of the variation in behaviour and psychological traits across people can be traced to inherited biological factors versus the environment. It is the modern, evidence-based way of examining the old nature-versus-nurture question.

Genes, chromosomes and DNA

Inside the nucleus of almost every cell in the body are chromosomes, thread-like structures made of a molecule called DNA (deoxyribonucleic acid). Humans normally have 46 chromosomes arranged in 23 pairs, one of each pair inherited from each parent. DNA carries the coded instructions for building and running the body, and a gene is a segment of DNA that codes for a particular product or trait. Genes are the basic units of heredity passed from parents to offspring.

Key. DNA is the molecule; a gene is a stretch of DNA that codes for something; chromosomes are the long packaged strands of DNA. Genes sit on chromosomes; chromosomes are made of DNA.

Genotype versus phenotype

The genotype is the full set of genes an individual carries, the inherited genetic blueprint. The phenotype is the set of observable characteristics that actually appear, such as eye colour, height, or a behavioural tendency. The phenotype results from the genotype interacting with the environment. Two people can share a genotype for a trait yet differ in phenotype because of different experiences, nutrition, or circumstances.

Dominant and recessive

Genes often come in alternative versions called alleles. An allele is dominant if a single copy is enough to express its effect in the phenotype, and recessive if its effect appears only when two copies are present (one from each parent). This is why a recessive trait can skip generations, hidden in carriers who possess one copy but do not show it.

Heritability

Heritability is a statistic, expressed from 0 to 1 (or as a percentage), that estimates how much of the variation in a trait within a population is due to genetic differences among its members. It is one of the most misunderstood ideas in psychology.

Trap. Heritability is about variation across a group, not about a single person. A heritability of 0.5 for a trait does NOT mean half of one individual's trait is caused by genes. It means about half of the differences between people in that population are associated with genetic differences. Heritability also depends on the environment studied and can change if conditions change.

Twin and adoption studies

Because we cannot ethically control people's genes or upbringing, behavioural geneticists use natural experiments. The two main designs are twin studies and adoption studies.

  • Monozygotic twins (identical twins) develop from a single fertilised egg that splits, so they share essentially 100 percent of their genes.
  • Dizygotic twins (fraternal twins) develop from two separate eggs fertilised at the same time, so they share on average 50 percent of their genes, like ordinary siblings.

If monozygotic twins are more alike on a trait than dizygotic twins raised in similar conditions, that greater similarity points to a genetic contribution. Adoption studies compare adopted children with both their biological and their adoptive parents: resemblance to the biological parents suggests a genetic influence, while resemblance to the adoptive family suggests an environmental one. Studies of monozygotic twins reared apart are especially powerful because they combine shared genes with different environments.

Trap. Monozygotic = MONO = one egg = identical = 100 percent shared genes. Dizygotic = DI = two eggs = fraternal = about 50 percent shared genes. Do not swap these. "Identical" always means monozygotic.

The diathesis-stress idea

Genes rarely act alone. The diathesis-stress model proposes that a person may inherit a diathesis, a predisposition or vulnerability to a disorder, which only develops into the actual disorder when combined with enough environmental stress. A vulnerability to depression, for example, might stay dormant in a supportive life but emerge under severe adversity. This model neatly captures how nature and nurture work together rather than in opposition.

Evolutionary psychology

Evolutionary psychology explains behaviour and mental processes as products of evolution, shaped over countless generations because they helped ancestors survive and reproduce. Its foundation is the theory of natural selection.

Who → what. Charles Darwin (1809 to 1882) set out the theory of evolution by natural selection in his 1859 book On the Origin of Species. The core idea: individuals vary; some variations improve survival and reproduction in a given environment; those individuals leave more offspring, so the helpful traits become more common over generations.

An adaptation is any inherited feature, physical or behavioural, that arose through natural selection because it improved survival or reproduction. Traits such as fear of dangerous animals or the human infant's readiness to bond with caregivers can be viewed as adaptations. A refinement of the theory is inclusive fitness, the idea that an organism can pass on its genes not only by reproducing itself but also by helping close relatives, who share many of the same genes, to survive and reproduce. Inclusive fitness helps explain altruism toward kin, which pure individual survival cannot easily account for.

2.12 Psychophysics and perceptual organisation

Psychophysics is the scientific study of the relationship between physical stimuli (such as the intensity of a light or the loudness of a tone) and the psychological sensations they produce. It was one of the very first areas of experimental psychology and gave the young science a rigorous, measurable subject matter.

Thresholds and the just noticeable difference

The absolute threshold is the smallest amount of a stimulus that a person can detect at least half the time. A classic example is the faint glow of a single candle seen from a great distance on a dark clear night. The difference threshold, also called the just noticeable difference (JND), is the smallest change in a stimulus that a person can reliably notice as different from the original.

Who → what. Ernst Weber (1795 to 1878), a German physiologist, discovered that the just noticeable difference is a constant proportion of the original stimulus, not a fixed amount. This is Weber's law. If you can just tell 100 grams apart from 110 grams (a 10 percent increase), then to notice a change from 200 grams you need about 220 grams, again a 10 percent increase.
Who → what. Gustav Fechner (1801 to 1887), also German, built on Weber's finding to relate the strength of the physical stimulus to the intensity of the resulting sensation, proposing that sensation grows with the logarithm of stimulus intensity (Fechner's law). His 1860 book Elements of Psychophysics effectively founded psychophysics as a discipline and coined the term.
Trap. Weber's law is about the just noticeable difference between two stimuli and states it is a constant ratio of the starting stimulus. Fechner's law goes further and links physical intensity to the felt magnitude of the sensation using a logarithm. Weber = the JND is a constant proportion; Fechner = sensation is a log function of intensity. Do not merge the two names.

Signal detection theory

Classical threshold thinking assumed a fixed cut-off below which a stimulus is simply not sensed. Signal detection theory challenged this by arguing that detecting a faint stimulus depends not only on the stimulus but also on the observer's decision-making, including their expectations, motivation, and willingness to say "yes". Detection is treated as a judgement made against a background of noise, and two people with identical senses may report differently because they set their decision criterion in different places. This introduced the idea that perception involves an element of decision, not just raw sensitivity.

Gestalt principles of perceptual organisation

Once sensations reach the brain they are organised into meaningful wholes. The Gestalt school of psychology (Gestalt is German for form or whole) held that the mind naturally organises parts into structured wholes, summed up in the phrase that the whole is more than the sum of its parts.

Who → what. Max Wertheimer (1880 to 1943) is regarded as a founder of Gestalt psychology. His study of apparent motion (the illusion of movement created by lights flashed in sequence, the phi phenomenon) helped launch the school, which set out the principles by which we group visual elements.
  • Figure and ground: we automatically separate a scene into a figure (the object of attention) that stands out against a background (the ground).
  • Proximity: elements that are close together are perceived as belonging to the same group.
  • Similarity: elements that look alike (in colour, shape, or size) are grouped together.
  • Closure: we mentally fill in gaps to perceive a complete, whole figure even when parts are missing.
  • Continuity (good continuation): we tend to perceive smooth, continuous lines and patterns rather than abrupt, broken ones.
  • Common fate: elements that move together in the same direction are seen as a single group.

Depth perception

Depth perception is the ability to see the world in three dimensions and to judge distance, even though the image on each retina is flat and two-dimensional. The brain uses cues, some needing both eyes and some available to a single eye.

Binocular cues depend on having two eyes:

  • Retinal disparity: because the two eyes are set a little apart, each receives a slightly different image; the brain compares the difference to judge distance. The nearer the object, the larger the disparity.
  • Convergence: as an object comes closer, the eyes turn inward; the brain reads the degree of inward turning as a signal of nearness.

Monocular cues (also called pictorial cues, because artists use them to suggest depth on a flat canvas) need only one eye. They include relative size (nearer objects appear larger), interposition or overlap (a nearer object partly blocks a farther one), linear perspective (parallel lines seem to converge in the distance), texture gradient (detail becomes finer with distance), and aerial perspective (distant objects look hazier).

Perceptual constancies

Perceptual constancy is the tendency to perceive an object as unchanging even when the image it casts on the retina changes. This stability is what lets the world seem coherent rather than chaotic.

  • Size constancy: a person walking away is seen as the same size, though their retinal image shrinks.
  • Shape constancy: a door is seen as rectangular whether shut or swung open, though its retinal image becomes a trapezoid.
  • Colour constancy: a familiar object keeps its perceived colour under changing lighting.

Visual illusions

An illusion is a consistent misperception of a real stimulus, and illusions are valuable because they expose the rules the brain normally uses to build perception.

  • Muller-Lyer illusion: two lines of equal length appear different because of the arrowheads at their ends. The line with outward-pointing fins (like a corner going away) looks longer than the one with inward-pointing fins.
  • Ponzo illusion: two identical horizontal lines placed across converging lines (like receding railway tracks) look unequal, the upper one appearing longer because depth cues make the brain judge it as farther away and therefore larger.
Why. Illusions such as Muller-Lyer and Ponzo are thought to arise because the brain applies depth and size cues that usually serve us well in the real three-dimensional world, but which misfire when applied to flat drawings. They show that perception is an active construction, not a passive copy of the retinal image.
Recap. Sleep is an active circadian process run by the suprachiasmatic nucleus, pineal gland and melatonin, cycling through NREM stages N1 to N3 (theta then delta waves) and paradoxical REM, with restorative and evolutionary theories explaining its purpose. Behavioural genetics uses genes, chromosomes and DNA, the genotype-phenotype distinction, dominant and recessive alleles, heritability (variation in a population, not a single person), and monozygotic-versus-dizygotic twin and adoption studies, alongside the diathesis-stress model and Darwin's evolutionary account of adaptation and inclusive fitness. Psychophysics, founded by Weber and Fechner, measures absolute and difference thresholds and the JND (Weber = constant ratio; Fechner = logarithmic sensation), extended by signal detection theory; and perception is organised by Wertheimer's Gestalt principles, binocular and monocular depth cues, perceptual constancies, and revealing illusions like Muller-Lyer and Ponzo.

2.13 The complete map: brain, body & behaviour, and the people who mapped them

This is the full roster of the landmark discoveries, systems, and models in biopsychology that an aptitude paper can name and expect you to place with the right person. It runs across three families: the neuron and how signals cross it, how the brain is organised and localised, and the physiology of emotion, stress, sleep, and motivation. Learn the pairing (finding → person → one-line idea) and no named result can ambush you.

Key. The "#" runs continuously across all three families so every entry has a unique number. "Who" gives the full name the first time; a discovery is filed under the person(s) an exam most often credits. Read each core-idea line as the single sentence you would write if asked "what is this?"

Family A: Neuron & neurotransmission (how does one cell signal the next?)

#Discovery / system / modelWhoCore idea in one line
1Neuron doctrineSantiago Ramon y CajalThe nervous system is built from discrete, individual cells (neurons), not one continuous web.
2Reticular theoryCamillo GolgiThe rival (and wrong) view that the nervous system is a single continuous network; his silver stain, however, made neurons visible.
3The synapse & integrative actionCharles SherringtonNamed the "synapse" and showed the nervous system integrates excitation and inhibition to coordinate reflexes.
4Membrane / ionic theory of excitationJulius BernsteinThe resting membrane is polarised by ion distribution, and excitation is a breakdown of that polarisation.
5Action potential & ionic mechanismAlan Hodgkin & Andrew HuxleyExplained the nerve impulse via sodium and potassium ion flow across the axon membrane (squid giant axon).
6All-or-none lawEdgar AdrianA neuron fires at full strength or not at all; stimulus intensity is coded by firing frequency, not spike size.
7Chemical synaptic transmissionOtto LoewiProved neurons communicate chemically, isolating "Vagusstoff" (acetylcholine) from a frog heart.
8Acetylcholine (ACh)Otto Loewi & Henry DaleFirst transmitter identified; runs muscle action and memory; its loss is central to Alzheimer's disease.
9DopamineArvid CarlssonEstablished dopamine as a transmitter; its deficit underlies Parkinson's, its excess is linked to schizophrenia.
10Serotonin (5-HT)Betty Twarog & Irvine PageMood, sleep, and appetite modulator; low function is tied to depression (the target of SSRIs).
11GABAEugene RobertsThe brain's chief inhibitory transmitter; calms neural firing (the site of benzodiazepine action).
12GlutamateJeffrey WatkinsThe brain's chief excitatory transmitter; central to learning via NMDA-receptor plasticity.
13Noradrenaline (norepinephrine)Ulf von EulerTransmitter of arousal and the sympathetic "fight-or-flight" response; implicated in mood and attention.
14Hebbian learningDonald Hebb"Cells that fire together wire together": repeated co-activation strengthens the synapse between two neurons.

Family B: Brain organisation & localisation (which part does what?)

#Discovery / system / modelWhoCore idea in one line
15PhrenologyFranz Joseph GallThe historical (discredited) idea that faculties sit in brain "organs" read from skull bumps; the first localisation claim.
16Broca's areaPaul BrocaLeft frontal lobe region for speech production; damage causes non-fluent (expressive) aphasia.
17Wernicke's areaCarl WernickeLeft temporal region for language comprehension; damage causes fluent but meaningless (receptive) aphasia.
18Cortical homunculus & motor mappingWilder PenfieldElectrically mapped the motor and sensory cortex, showing a distorted body map scaled by sensitivity.
19Equipotentiality & mass actionKarl LashleyMemory is distributed: learning loss depends on how much cortex is destroyed, not exactly where.
20Split-brain & hemispheric lateralisationRoger SperryCutting the corpus callosum revealed each hemisphere has specialised, separable functions.
21Visual cortex feature detectorsDavid Hubel & Torsten WieselCells in the visual cortex respond to specific edges, lines, and orientations; vision is built from features.
22Reward / pleasure centresJames Olds & Peter MilnerRats self-stimulate a septal/hypothalamic "pleasure" circuit relentlessly, defining the brain's reward pathway.
23Triune brainPaul MacLeanA layered model: reptilian (survival), limbic (emotion), and neocortex (reason) evolved in sequence.

Family C: Physiology of emotion, stress, sleep & motivation (how does the body drive feeling and state?)

#Discovery / system / modelWhoCore idea in one line
24Papez circuitJames PapezEmotion is processed by a loop of limbic structures (hippocampus, cingulate, thalamus, hypothalamus).
25Kluver-Bucy syndromeHeinrich Kluver & Paul BucyBilateral temporal-lobe (amygdala) damage causes loss of fear, hyperorality, and hypersexuality.
26Amygdala & fear conditioningJoseph LeDouxMapped the amygdala as the hub of the fear response, including a fast "low road" bypassing the cortex.
27Milieu interieur (internal environment)Claude BernardLife depends on keeping the body's internal environment stable and constant; the seed of homeostasis.
28Homeostasis & fight-or-flightWalter CannonCoined "homeostasis" for self-regulated balance and described the sympathetic emergency response.
29James-Lange theory of emotionWilliam James & Carl LangeEmotion is the perception of bodily changes: we feel afraid because we tremble, not the reverse.
30Cannon-Bard theory of emotionWalter Cannon & Philip BardBodily arousal and the felt emotion occur simultaneously via the thalamus, not one causing the other.
31General adaptation syndrome (stress)Hans SelyeThe body meets any prolonged stressor in three stages: alarm, resistance, then exhaustion.
32HPA axisHans SelyeThe hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal chain that releases cortisol to run the slow, sustained stress response.
33REM sleep discoveryEugene Aserinsky & Nathaniel KleitmanIdentified rapid-eye-movement sleep, the active brain state strongly associated with dreaming.
34Sleep-stage & dream researchWilliam DementCharted the cyclic architecture of sleep stages and founded the scientific study of dreaming.
35Circadian rhythm & the biological clockFranz HalbergBody functions follow a roughly 24-hour internal cycle set by the suprachiasmatic nucleus and light.
36Trichromatic (Young-Helmholtz) theoryThomas Young & Hermann von HelmholtzColour vision starts from three cone types tuned to red, green, and blue wavelengths.
37Opponent-process colour theoryEwald HeringColour is coded by opposing pairs (red–green, blue–yellow, black–white), explaining afterimages.
Trap. Do not swap the aphasia pair: Broca is broken speech production (frontal), Wernicke is broken comprehension (temporal). Likewise, Cajal won on the neuron doctrine while Golgi championed the wrong reticular theory even though his stain made the work possible. And keep the emotion theories straight: James-Lange says body first (arousal → emotion), Cannon-Bard says both at once.
Recap. Three families cover the biopsychology map: Family A builds the signal (Cajal's neuron, Sherrington's synapse, Hodgkin-Huxley's spike, Loewi's chemical transmission, Hebb's plasticity, and the transmitter-to-disorder links); Family B localises function (Broca, Wernicke, Penfield, Lashley, Sperry, Hubel-Wiesel, Olds-Milner); Family C runs emotion, stress, sleep, and the senses (Papez, LeDoux, Cannon, Selye, Aserinsky-Kleitman, and the James-Lange versus Cannon-Bard debate). Fix the finding-to-person pairing and the whole "who discovered this?" column of the paper is safe.

3 Developmental Psychology

Developmental psychology studies how humans change and stay the same across the entire lifespan, from conception to death. It asks three linked questions: what changes (body, thinking, emotion, personality, social ties), when it changes (the timing and order of milestones), and why it changes (the mechanisms behind growth). This section builds every major theory from zero, then flags the exact contrasts and confusions that entrance papers love to test.

3.1 The Big Debates & Research Designs

Before any single theory, developmental science is organised around a handful of recurring arguments. Every theorist you meet later takes a side (often implicitly) on each of these, so learn the debate first and the theorists become easy to sort.

Nature vs nurture

This is the oldest question in the field: does development come mainly from nature (genes, biological maturation, inherited temperament) or from nurture (environment, learning, culture, parenting)? The modern answer is both, interacting. Genes set a range of possibilities (a reaction range); environment decides where in that range a person lands. Related ideas: epigenetics (experience switches genes on or off without changing the DNA code) and gene–environment correlation (a child's genes shape the environments they are exposed to, e.g. a musically gifted child seeks out instruments).

Key. Almost no serious theory is purely nature or purely nurture. The exam-correct framing is interactionism: genes and environment continuously shape each other.

Continuity vs discontinuity

Is development a smooth, gradual, quantitative build-up (continuity, like a ramp), or a series of distinct qualitative jumps into new stages (discontinuity, like a staircase)? Learning theorists (Skinner) lean continuity; stage theorists (Piaget, Erikson, Freud, Kohlberg) lean discontinuity.

Trap. "Stage theory" is a strong marker of the discontinuity side. If a question names Piaget, Erikson, Freud, or Kohlberg and asks about the nature of change, the answer is discontinuous / qualitative / stage-like, not gradual.

Critical vs sensitive periods

A critical period is a rigid, narrow window during which a specific experience must occur for normal development, or the ability is lost for good (Konrad Lorenz's imprinting in geese; some aspects of vision). A sensitive period is softer: a window when the organism is especially receptive and learning is easiest, but development can still happen (harder, less complete) outside it (first-language and accent acquisition).

Key. Critical = rigid, all-or-none, permanent loss. Sensitive = optimal-but-flexible. Human language is usually called a sensitive period, not a strict critical one.

Stability vs change

Do early traits (temperament, attachment style, intelligence) persist across life (stability), or can people fundamentally transform (change)? Most evidence shows moderate stability with real room for change, especially with new relationships and environments.

Research designs in developmental psychology

Because the subject is change over time, the study design is itself an exam favourite.

DesignHow it worksStrengthWeakness
Cross-sectionalDifferent age groups tested at the same time (e.g. 5-, 10-, 15-year-olds today)Fast, cheap, no dropoutCohort effects: age differences may reflect the era each group grew up in, not development
LongitudinalThe same people followed and re-tested across yearsShows true individual change & developmentSlow, costly, attrition (dropout), practice effects
Sequential (cross-sequential)Several age cohorts each followed over time (a blend of the two)Separates age effects from cohort effectsComplex, expensive to run
Trap. Cohort effect is the signature weakness of cross-sectional designs; attrition (participant dropout) is the signature weakness of longitudinal designs. Papers swap these to test you.

3.2 Prenatal Development & Teratogens

Development begins at conception. The roughly 38-40 weeks of pregnancy are divided into three stages, and knowing their order and hallmark events is standard exam content.

StageTime spanHallmark events
GerminalConception → ~2 weeksRapid cell division of the zygote; the ball of cells (blastocyst) travels down and implants in the uterine wall
Embryonic~2 – 8 weeksThe embryo forms all major organs and body systems (organogenesis); heart begins to beat; most vulnerable stage to damage
Fetal~9 weeks → birthThe fetus grows in size; organs mature and become functional; movement, sleep–wake cycles; age of viability (~22–24 weeks) when survival outside the womb becomes possible

Teratogens

A teratogen is any environmental agent (drug, virus, chemical, radiation) that causes harm to the developing embryo or fetus. Key principles: harm depends on timing (the embryonic period is most sensitive because organs are forming), dose, and the genetic vulnerability of mother and child. Common teratogens: alcohol (causes Fetal Alcohol Syndrome, marked by facial abnormalities and intellectual disability), nicotine, cocaine and other drugs, the rubella virus, and high-dose radiation.

Key. The embryonic stage is the period of maximum teratogen vulnerability because that is when organs are being built. Same insult in the fetal stage usually does less structural damage.

3.3 Jean Piaget: Cognitive Development

Who → what. Jean Piaget, a Swiss psychologist, proposed that children are active "little scientists" who construct knowledge by acting on the world, passing through four fixed, universal, qualitatively distinct stages of thinking (cognitive constructivism).

The engine: how thinking grows

  • Schema (plural schemas / schemata): a mental framework or "file" for organising information (a child's "dog" schema = four legs, fur, barks).
  • Assimilation: fitting new information into an existing schema (seeing a cat and calling it "dog").
  • Accommodation: changing the schema to fit new information (learning cats are a separate category and updating the schema).
  • Equilibration: the drive to balance assimilation and accommodation; disequilibrium (when old schemas fail) pushes the child to a higher level of understanding.
Trap. Do not swap these two. Assimilation = the world bends to fit your existing schema (schema stays the same). Accommodation = your schema bends to fit the world (schema changes).

The four stages

StageAge rangeKey features & the achievement
SensorimotorBirth – ~2 yrsKnows the world through senses and movement. Key gain: object permanence (objects still exist when out of sight; before this, "out of sight, out of mind"). Beginnings of symbolic thought at the end.
Preoperational~2 – 7 yrsSymbolic thinking, language, pretend play. But thinking is limited by egocentrism (cannot take another's viewpoint), centration (focusing on one feature), and lack of conservation. No true logical operations yet. The "three-mountains task" shows egocentrism.
Concrete operational~7 – 11 yrsLogical thinking about concrete objects. Masters conservation (quantity stays the same despite a change in shape), reversibility (mentally undoing an action), classification, and seriation (ordering). Still struggles with abstract, hypothetical ideas.
Formal operational~11 yrs → adulthoodAbstract reasoning, hypothetical-deductive logic, systematic problem solving, thinking about "what if" and ideals. Adolescent egocentrism may reappear (imaginary audience, personal fable).
Why. The classic conservation test: pour water from a short wide glass into a tall thin glass. A preoperational child says the tall glass "has more" (fooled by height, i.e. centration). A concrete-operational child knows the amount is unchanged, because they grasp reversibility. This single demonstration is the boundary between the two stages and is heavily tested.

Critiques of Piaget

  • He underestimated young children; with simpler methods, object permanence and some abilities appear earlier than he claimed.
  • Development is more continuous and domain-specific than his neat universal stages suggest; not everyone reaches formal operations in every area.
  • He neglected culture and social interaction as engines of cognitive growth (the gap that Lev Vygotsky fills).

3.4 Lev Vygotsky: Sociocultural Theory

Who → what. Lev Vygotsky, a Russian psychologist, argued that cognitive development is driven by social interaction and culture. Children learn through guided participation with more skilled partners; thinking is first social ("between people") and then becomes internal ("within the person").
  • Zone of Proximal Development (ZPD): the gap between what a child can do alone and what they can do with help from a skilled partner. Learning happens best inside this zone.
  • Scaffolding: the temporary, adjustable support a teacher or peer gives, gradually withdrawn as the child becomes competent (like scaffolding removed once a building stands). Note: the term "scaffolding" was coined by Jerome Bruner and colleagues, building on Vygotsky's ZPD.
  • More Knowledgeable Other (MKO): anyone with more skill or knowledge on the task (a parent, teacher, older peer, even a tool).
  • Private speech: children talk out loud to themselves to guide their own behaviour; this later goes underground as silent inner speech that regulates thought. Vygotsky saw this as helpful self-guidance; Piaget dismissed similar talk as merely "egocentric" and non-social.

Piaget vs Vygotsky (a classic exam pairing)

DimensionJean PiagetLev Vygotsky
Engine of growthIndividual exploration; the "lone scientist"Social interaction & culture; the "apprentice"
Role of languageReflects thought that already developedDrives and shapes thought (inner speech)
Learning & developmentDevelopment leads / limits learning (must be ready)Learning leads development (within the ZPD)
StagesUniversal, fixed stagesNo fixed universal stages; culturally variable
Private speechEgocentric, immature, fadesAdaptive self-guidance, becomes inner speech
Trap. "Learning leads development," "ZPD," "scaffolding," and "MKO" are all Vygotsky. "Object permanence," "conservation," "assimilation/accommodation," and the four stages are all Piaget. Do not attribute one's concept to the other.

3.5 Erik Erikson: Psychosocial Development

Who → what. Erik Erikson, a German-born ego psychologist, proposed eight psychosocial stages spanning the whole lifespan. Each stage centres on a crisis (a tension between two poles); resolving it well yields a lasting virtue (basic strength). Development is lifelong and social, not just childhood and sexual.
StageApprox. ageCrisisVirtue gained
10 – 1 yr (infancy)Trust vs MistrustHope
21 – 3 yrs (toddler)Autonomy vs Shame & DoubtWill
33 – 6 yrs (preschool)Initiative vs GuiltPurpose
46 – 12 yrs (school age)Industry vs InferiorityCompetence
512 – 18 yrs (adolescence)Identity vs Role ConfusionFidelity
618 – 40 yrs (young adult)Intimacy vs IsolationLove
740 – 65 yrs (middle adult)Generativity vs StagnationCare
865+ yrs (late adult)Integrity vs DespairWisdom
Key. Memorise stage 5 above all: Identity vs Role Confusion in adolescence, virtue Fidelity. It is Erikson's most cited stage and the launch point for James Marcia's identity statuses (below).

3.6 Sigmund Freud: Psychosexual Development

Who → what. Sigmund Freud, founder of psychoanalysis, proposed five psychosexual stages. At each stage libidinal (sexual) energy centres on a different erogenous zone; how conflicts there are handled shapes adult personality.
StageAgeFocus & conflict
Oral0 – 1 yrMouth (sucking, biting); weaning
Anal1 – 3 yrsBowel/bladder control; toilet training
Phallic3 – 6 yrsGenitals; Oedipus / Electra complex, identification with same-sex parent, superego forms
Latency6 yrs – pubertySexual urges dormant; energy into school, friendships, skills
GenitalPuberty onwardMature adult sexuality; capacity for love and work

Fixation occurs when a conflict at a stage is unresolved (through too little or too much gratification), leaving libido "stuck" there and shaping adult traits. Oral fixation → smoking, overeating, dependency; anal-retentive → stubborn, orderly, stingy.

Erikson vs Freud (contrast)

  • Motivation: Freud = sexual/biological drives; Erikson = social relationships and identity.
  • Span: Freud stops at puberty (5 stages, ending in the genital stage); Erikson covers the whole lifespan (8 stages).
  • Focus: Freud on the id and unconscious conflict; Erikson on the ego and healthy adaptation.
  • Tone: Freud emphasises pathology from early fixation; Erikson emphasises growth and strengths (virtues).
Trap. Erikson was a neo-Freudian who expanded Freud, keeping stages but making them psychosocial and lifelong. Their childhood stages roughly align in age; the giveaway is that only Erikson has stages for adulthood and old age.

3.7 Lawrence Kohlberg: Moral Development

Who → what. Lawrence Kohlberg extended Piaget's ideas to moral reasoning. Using dilemmas (famously the Heinz dilemma: should a man steal an over-priced drug to save his dying wife?), he judged not the yes/no answer but the reasoning behind it, yielding three levels of two stages each.
LevelStageReasoning basis
I. Preconventional (self-interest; typical of children)1. Obedience & punishment"Avoid punishment." Right = not getting caught
2. Instrumental / self-interest"What's in it for me?" Right = fair exchange, rewards
II. Conventional (social approval & law)3. Good boy / good girlRight = pleasing others, being seen as nice
4. Law & orderRight = obeying rules, laws, duty, authority
III. Postconventional (abstract principles)5. Social contractLaws are social agreements that can be changed for the greater good
6. Universal ethical principlesSelf-chosen ethical principles (justice, human rights) override unjust laws
Key. Kohlberg scores the reasoning, not the choice. Two people who both say "Heinz should steal the drug" can be at completely different stages depending on why.

Carol Gilligan's critique

Carol Gilligan, once Kohlberg's collaborator, argued his theory was gender-biased: it was built on male samples and treated abstract justice as the highest morality, thereby scoring women as "lower." She proposed that women tend to reason from an ethic of care (relationships, compassion, responsibility to others), which is different, not inferior, to the male-typical ethic of justice.

Trap. Gilligan = ethic of care and the charge of gender bias against Kohlberg. Kohlberg = ethic of justice. Do not credit "care ethics" to Kohlberg.

3.8 Attachment

Attachment is the deep, enduring emotional bond between a child and caregiver. Three names dominate: Harlow (that it is not about food), Bowlby (the theory), and Ainsworth (how to measure the types).

Who → what. Harry Harlow raised infant rhesus monkeys with two wire "mothers": one made of bare wire that dispensed milk, and one covered in soft cloth with no milk. The babies clung to the cloth mother and ran to her when frightened, going to the wire mother only to feed. This proved attachment is built on contact comfort, not just food, demolishing the earlier "cupboard love" idea.
Who → what. John Bowlby founded attachment theory: attachment is an evolved, biological system that keeps the infant close to a caregiver for survival. The infant builds an internal working model, a mental template of relationships ("Am I worthy of care? Are others reliable?") that guides later relationships. He also emphasised a sensitive period for forming attachments and the damage of maternal deprivation.
Who → what. Mary Ainsworth devised the Strange Situation, a lab procedure of separations and reunions between an infant, caregiver, and stranger. How the infant behaves, especially at reunion, reveals the attachment style.
Attachment styleBehaviour in the Strange SituationCaregiving linked to it
SecureExplores using caregiver as a base; distressed at separation; easily comforted at reunionSensitive, responsive
Insecure–avoidantLittle distress at separation; ignores/avoids caregiver at reunionUnresponsive, rejecting
Insecure–ambivalent / resistantVery distressed at separation; at reunion seeks yet resists comfort (clingy and angry), hard to sootheInconsistent
DisorganisedConfused, contradictory, fearful behaviour; no coherent strategyFrightening / abusive / traumatic
Key. Ainsworth identified the first three styles. The fourth, disorganised attachment, was added later by Mary Main. If a paper asks who added disorganised attachment, the answer is Mary Main, not Ainsworth.

3.9 Language Development

Language emerges in a strikingly universal order across cultures, which is itself evidence in the nature–nurture debate.

MilestoneApprox. ageWhat it is
Cooing~2 – 3 monthsVowel-like sounds ("ooo", "aaa")
Babbling~6 monthsRepeated consonant–vowel strings ("bababa"); starts universal, then narrows to the native language's sounds
Holophrases (one-word)~12 monthsSingle words carry a whole sentence's meaning ("milk!" = "I want milk")
Telegraphic speech~18 – 24 monthsTwo-word combos with only key content words, function words dropped ("want cookie", "mummy go")

A common error here is overregularisation (applying a grammar rule too widely, e.g. "goed," "foots"), which actually shows the child has learned the rule rather than merely imitating.

Chomsky vs Skinner (the nativist–behaviourist debate)

  • B.F. Skinner (behaviourist): language is learned like any behaviour, through imitation, reinforcement, and shaping by parents.
  • Noam Chomsky (nativist): children learn language far too fast and too creatively for pure reinforcement to explain, and produce sentences they have never heard (the "poverty of the stimulus"). He proposed an innate Language Acquisition Device (LAD), a built-in biological readiness for grammar, and a universal grammar common to all languages.
Trap. LAD and universal grammar = Chomsky (innate/nativist). Reinforcement/imitation = Skinner (learned/behaviourist). Overregularisation errors ("goed") are strong evidence against pure imitation and thus favour Chomsky.

3.10 Lifespan, Adolescence & Aging

Urie Bronfenbrenner: ecological systems theory

Who → what. Urie Bronfenbrenner pictured the developing child at the centre of nested environmental "systems," like a set of Russian dolls, each layer influencing development.
SystemWhat it includes
MicrosystemImmediate, direct settings: family, school, peers, the child's actual daily contacts
MesosystemConnections between microsystems (e.g. parent–teacher relationship)
ExosystemSettings that affect the child indirectly (e.g. a parent's workplace, local government)
MacrosystemThe broad culture, values, laws, customs, and economy
ChronosystemThe dimension of time: life transitions and historical events over the lifespan
Trap. Meso = links between the child's own settings; exo = a setting the child is not directly in but which still affects them. Chronosystem is the time layer, added later.

James Marcia: identity statuses (adolescence)

James Marcia operationalised Erikson's stage 5 using two dimensions: exploration (actively trying out options) and commitment (settling on a choice). Their combinations give four identity statuses:

StatusExplorationCommitment
Identity diffusionNoNo
ForeclosureNoYes (commitment without exploring, e.g. adopting parents' plan)
MoratoriumYes (in crisis, exploring now)No (not yet)
Identity achievementYes (explored)Yes (then committed)
Key. The maturity order is diffusion → foreclosure → moratorium → achievement. Foreclosure = commitment without exploration; moratorium = exploration without commitment yet. These two are the most confused pair.

Paul Baltes: lifespan development principles

Paul Baltes argued development is a lifelong process best summarised by a few principles:

  • Lifelong: development continues from birth to death; no age dominates.
  • Multidimensional & multidirectional: it involves many domains, and abilities can rise and fall at the same time (some grow, some decline).
  • Gain–loss balance: every stage brings both gains and losses together.
  • Plasticity: capacities remain modifiable across life.
  • Contextual: shaped by history, culture, and non-normative life events.

Baltes also described healthy aging as Selective Optimisation with Compensation (SOC): selecting fewer goals, optimising effort on them, and compensating for losses. In cognition he distinguished fluid intelligence (raw processing speed, declines with age) from crystallised intelligence (accumulated knowledge, stable or rising).

Elisabeth Kubler-Ross: stages of grief

Who → what. Elisabeth Kubler-Ross, studying the terminally ill, described five stages of coping with dying and grief: Denial → Anger → Bargaining → Depression → Acceptance (mnemonic DABDA).
Trap. The stages are not a rigid, universal sequence; people skip stages, repeat them, or reorder them. Modern view treats them as common reactions, not a fixed ladder. The ordered five for recall: Denial, Anger, Bargaining, Depression, Acceptance.
Recap. The big debates (nature/nurture, continuity/discontinuity, critical/sensitive periods) and designs (cross-sectional cohort effect vs longitudinal attrition) frame everything. Prenatal = germinal, embryonic (peak teratogen risk), fetal. Piaget = 4 cognitive stages (object permanence → egocentrism → conservation/reversibility → abstract) driven by assimilation/accommodation; Vygotsky counters with ZPD, scaffolding, MKO, and "learning leads development." Erikson = 8 lifelong psychosocial crises with virtues (identity vs role confusion in adolescence); Freud = 5 psychosexual stages plus fixation. Kohlberg = pre/conventional/postconventional (6 stages, judges reasoning); Gilligan adds the ethic of care. Attachment: Harlow (contact comfort), Bowlby (internal working model), Ainsworth's Strange Situation (secure/avoidant/ambivalent) plus Main's disorganised. Language runs cooing → babbling → holophrases → telegraphic, with Chomsky's LAD against Skinner's reinforcement. Lifespan: Bronfenbrenner's nested systems, Marcia's four identity statuses, Baltes's lifespan principles, and Kubler-Ross's DABDA grief stages.

3.11 Urie Bronfenbrenner's Ecological Systems Theory

Most developmental theories look inside the child: at stages of thought, at drives, at attachment bonds. Urie Bronfenbrenner (1917–2005), a Russian-born American psychologist, argued that this was only half the picture. A child never develops in a vacuum. Development happens inside a set of nested environments, layered like the rings of an onion, each one influencing the child and being influenced in turn. His ecological systems theory (later renamed the bioecological model to stress the child's own biology) maps these layers from the ones closest to the child outward to the broadest cultural forces.

Who → what. Urie Bronfenbrenner → ecological (bioecological) systems theory: the developing child sits at the centre of five nested environmental systems, from the immediate microsystem out to the time-based chronosystem.

The five systems, moving outward from the child, are these.

  • Microsystem. The innermost ring: the immediate settings the child directly experiences and acts within. Family, classroom, peer group, the local playground. Here influence is a two-way street. The child shapes these settings just as they shape the child; a cheerful, easy baby draws warm responses, while an irritable one may draw tension.
  • Mesosystem. Not a new place, but the web of connections between microsystems. What happens between home and school, between family and peers. A child whose parents attend school meetings and know the teacher tends to do better than one whose home and school never connect.
  • Exosystem. Settings the child is not part of but which still affect them through the microsystem. A parent's workplace: a stressful job or a supportive one, night shifts, a layoff, or a generous parental-leave policy all reach the child indirectly.
  • Macrosystem. The broad cultural blanket: the values, laws, customs, religion, and economic patterns of the wider society. Whether a culture prizes individual achievement or family duty; whether education is free; caste, class, and gender expectations.
  • Chronosystem. The dimension of time: how the child and every other layer change across the lifespan and across historical eras. A parent's divorce (its impact differs one year later versus five), or growing up with smartphones versus growing up without them.
Why. The model is a favourite in exams because it makes influence concrete at every distance from the child. If asked which system a parent's job belongs to, the answer is the exosystem, because the child does not live in that setting yet is shaped by it.
