Trap. Do not confuse the mesosystem with the exosystem. The mesosystem is the link between two settings the child does take part in (home–school). The exosystem is a setting the child does not take part in (the parent's office). Also, the chronosystem is about time, not a physical place at all.

3.12 Temperament and Parenting

Thomas and Chess: temperamental types and goodness of fit

Long before a child can think in Piagetian stages or form Eriksonian identity, they already have a characteristic style of responding to the world. Temperament is the early-appearing, biologically rooted pattern of emotional reactivity and self-regulation, the raw material on which later personality is built. Two American child psychiatrists, Alexander Thomas (1914–2003) and Stella Chess (1914–2007), launched the landmark New York Longitudinal Study, following children from infancy and rating them on dimensions such as activity level, rhythmicity (regularity of sleep and hunger), approach or withdrawal to new things, adaptability, mood, and intensity of reaction. From these they identified three broad temperamental types.

  • Easy child (about 40% of their sample): regular in habits, cheerful in mood, adapts readily to new situations and people.
  • Difficult child (about 10%): irregular in habits, slow to accept change, intense and often negative in mood, prone to crying and tantrums.
  • Slow-to-warm-up child (about 15%): mild and low in activity, initially withdraws from novelty but adapts slowly with gentle, repeated exposure. (The remainder did not fit neatly into one type.)
Who → what. Alexander Thomas & Stella Chess → three temperament types (easy, difficult, slow-to-warm-up) and the principle of goodness of fit: healthy development depends on the match between the child's temperament and the demands of their environment.

Their most important idea is goodness of fit. No temperament is doomed or destined; what matters is the match between the child's style and the expectations and handling of the caregivers. A difficult child with patient, flexible parents who do not take the intensity personally may thrive. The same child with harsh or overwhelmed parents may spiral into conflict. A slow-to-warm-up child pushed to perform in front of strangers fits poorly with that demand and grows anxious, but the same child given time to observe fits well.

Why. Goodness of fit dissolves the false question "is it the child or the parenting?" The answer is the fit between the two. This is a frequently tested phrase, so tie it firmly to Thomas and Chess.

Baumrind's parenting styles, extended by Maccoby and Martin

The American developmental psychologist Diana Baumrind (1927–2018) studied preschoolers and their parents and identified distinct styles of parenting that predicted how children turned out. She originally described three.

  • Authoritative. High warmth and high control together. Parents set clear, firm rules but explain them, listen to the child, and adjust. Consistently linked to the best outcomes: confident, socially competent, self-reliant children.
  • Authoritarian. High control but low warmth. Rules are strict and unexplained ("because I said so"), obedience is demanded, and there is little give-and-take. Children tend to be obedient but anxious, withdrawn, or lacking in initiative.
  • Permissive (indulgent). High warmth but low control. Parents are affectionate and lenient, making few demands and setting few limits. Children may be impulsive, self-centred, and poor at self-regulation.

Later, Eleanor Maccoby (1917–2018) and John Martin reorganised Baumrind's scheme along two clean dimensions, demandingness (control, expectations) and responsiveness (warmth, acceptance), which produces a two-by-two grid. That grid revealed a fourth cell Baumrind had not named as a distinct style: low in both dimensions.

  • Neglectful (uninvolved). Low warmth and low control. Parents are detached, meeting basic physical needs but otherwise disengaged from the child's life. Linked to the poorest outcomes across social, emotional, and academic measures.
Who → what. Diana Baumrind → three parenting styles (authoritative, authoritarian, permissive). Eleanor Maccoby & John Martin → recast them on demandingness × responsiveness and added the fourth, neglectful/uninvolved, style.
StyleDemandingness (control)Responsiveness (warmth)Typical child outcome
AuthoritativeHighHighConfident, socially competent, self-reliant
AuthoritarianHighLowObedient but anxious, low initiative
Permissive (indulgent)LowHighImpulsive, poor self-control
Neglectful (uninvolved)LowLowPoorest outcomes overall
Trap. The two easily confused pairs are authoritative (high warmth + high control, the good one) versus authoritarian (low warmth + high control, the harsh one). The words look alike; the outcomes do not. Remember: authoritative = valued and warm. Also do not credit the fourth style to Baumrind; the neglectful style came from Maccoby and Martin.

3.13 Gender and Moral Development Critiques

Gender identity and gender constancy

Gender identity is a child's private sense of being a boy or a girl, usually established by about age three. But young children do not yet grasp that gender is permanent. The understanding that one's gender stays the same across time and situations, regardless of hairstyle, clothing, or activity, is called gender constancy, a concept drawn from Lawrence Kohlberg's cognitive account of gender. It arrives in steps: first the child can label gender, then grasps its stability over time (a boy will become a man), and finally its consistency across appearances (a boy in a dress is still a boy). Full constancy typically appears around age six or seven, in step with the concrete-operational gains in conservation.

Sandra Bem: gender schema theory and androgyny

The American psychologist Sandra Bem (1944–2014) proposed gender schema theory: children build a mental framework, a schema, of what counts as masculine or feminine in their culture, and then filter new information through it, paying attention to and remembering what fits and shaping their own behaviour to match. Bem also challenged the old assumption that masculine and feminine were opposite ends of a single line. She argued they are two separate dimensions, so a person can score high on both. She called this combination androgyny (from Greek andro, male, and gyne, female): possessing both instrumental "masculine" traits (assertiveness, independence) and expressive "feminine" traits (warmth, nurturance). She built the Bem Sex-Role Inventory to measure it and argued that androgynous people are more flexible and better adjusted across varied situations.

Who → what. Sandra Bem → gender schema theory (children process the world through a learned gender framework) and androgyny as a healthy blend of masculine and feminine traits, measured by the Bem Sex-Role Inventory.

Carol Gilligan: the ethic of care

Carol Gilligan (born 1936), an American psychologist and once a colleague of Lawrence Kohlberg, mounted the best-known feminist critique of his stage theory of moral development. Kohlberg had built his stages from studies of boys and treated the highest morality as abstract justice and universal principles. Gilligan argued this yardstick was biased: it made women look morally "lower" simply because they often reasoned differently. In her book In a Different Voice (1982) she proposed that women more often speak in an ethic of care, grounded in relationships, responsibility, and not wanting to hurt others, rather than an ethic of justice based on rules and rights. Neither voice is superior; Kohlberg's scale had simply mistaken one voice for the whole of morality.

Trap. Match the pair correctly: Kohlberg = ethic of justice (rules, rights, abstract principles); Gilligan = ethic of care (relationships, responsibility, compassion). Gilligan's charge was gender bias in Kohlberg's method, not that women lack morality.

Harry Harlow: contact comfort in rhesus monkeys

Why does a baby cling to its mother? The behaviourist answer of the mid-twentieth century was simple: the mother is associated with food, so the infant learns to love the source of feeding. The American psychologist Harry Harlow (1905–1981) overturned this "cupboard love" view with his famous studies of infant rhesus monkeys. He raised babies with two artificial surrogate "mothers": one made of bare wire that dispensed milk, and one made of soft terrycloth that gave no food. The behaviourist prediction was that the infants would bond with the feeding wire mother. Instead, they clung to the soft cloth mother almost all day, running to her when frightened and visiting the wire mother only briefly to feed. Harlow called the comfort of soft touch contact comfort and showed it, not food, was the basis of the bond.

Who → what. Harry Harlow → rhesus-monkey surrogate-mother studies proving contact comfort (soft touch), not feeding, drives attachment; this undercut the drive-reduction "cupboard love" theory and supported later attachment theory.
Why. Harlow's work is the empirical bridge to Bowlby and Ainsworth: it demonstrated that the need for a comforting, secure body to cling to is primary, not a by-product of hunger. Frightened infants used the cloth mother as a base from which to explore, foreshadowing the "secure base" idea.

3.14 Lifespan and Later-Life Models

Paul Baltes: the lifespan perspective and SOC

The German psychologist Paul Baltes (1939–2006) insisted that development does not stop at adolescence and is not simply decline thereafter. His lifespan developmental perspective holds that development is lifelong, multidimensional (many domains at once), multidirectional (some capacities grow while others shrink), plastic (changeable), and always embedded in history and culture. At every age there is a shifting balance of gains and losses.

To explain how people adapt to loss, especially in old age, Baltes and his collaborator Margret Baltes described selective optimization with compensation (SOC). Ageing people cope by three coordinated strategies.

  • Selection. Narrowing goals to the few that matter most, given shrinking resources. An older musician keeps only a small repertoire.
  • Optimization. Investing effort and practice into those chosen goals to keep performance high.
  • Compensation. Using new means or aids to offset losses. The pianist Arthur Rubinstein, Baltes's own example, played fewer pieces (selection), practised them more (optimization), and slowed before fast passages so the contrast made them seem faster (compensation).
Who → what. Paul Baltes → the lifespan perspective (development is lifelong, multidirectional, plastic, gain-and-loss) and selective optimization with compensation (SOC) as the strategy for adapting to age-related decline.

Ainsworth's Strange Situation classifications, with Main's fourth pattern

Building on John Bowlby's attachment theory, the American-Canadian psychologist Mary Ainsworth (1913–1999) devised a laboratory procedure, the Strange Situation, to measure the quality of an infant's attachment. In a series of episodes a one-year-old is observed exploring a room, meeting a stranger, being briefly separated from the mother, and, crucially, reunited with her. The reunion behaviour reveals the attachment pattern. Ainsworth described three, and later Mary Main (1943–2023), with Judith Solomon, added a fourth.

ClassificationOn separationOn reunionCaregiving linked to it
SecureMay be distressed; uses mother as a base to exploreSeeks her, is soothed, returns to playSensitive, responsive
Insecure-avoidantLittle distressIgnores or avoids motherRejecting, unresponsive
Insecure-ambivalent (resistant)Very distressedSeeks contact yet resists it, hard to soothe, angryInconsistent
Disorganized (Main)Confused, no clear strategyContradictory, dazed, or fearful behaviourFrightening, often linked to abuse or trauma
Who → what. Mary Ainsworth → the Strange Situation and three attachment types (secure, avoidant, ambivalent/resistant). Mary Main & Judith Solomon → the fourth, disorganized, type, marked by contradictory, fearful reunion behaviour.
Trap. Distinguish the two insecure "organised" types by the reunion. Avoidant infants seem indifferent and turn away; ambivalent/resistant infants are intensely distressed and cannot be comforted, clinging and pushing away at once. The disorganized type has no consistent strategy at all, and it is credited to Main, not Ainsworth.

Self-concept across childhood

Self-concept is the set of beliefs a person holds about who they are. It is not fixed at birth but grows and reorganises across childhood. The famous rouge test (also called the mirror or mark test), studied by Michael Lewis and Jeanne Brooks-Gunn, shows that self-recognition emerges around 18 to 24 months: a toddler with a spot of rouge secretly dabbed on the nose will touch their own nose when shown a mirror, proving they recognise the reflection as themselves. From there the self-concept matures in a clear direction.

  • Early childhood. The self is described in concrete, observable, physical terms ("I have brown hair, I have a dog, I can run fast") and is often unrealistically positive.
  • Middle childhood. Descriptions become psychological and comparative ("I am kind, I am better at maths than my brother"), as social comparison sharpens.
  • Adolescence. The self becomes abstract, integrated, and reflective, weaving traits, values, beliefs, and future goals into a coherent identity, linking directly to Erikson's identity task.
Why. Notice the same trajectory that runs through cognitive development: from concrete and outer to abstract and inner. The child who first knows the self by hair colour later knows it by values, mirroring the move from concrete to formal operations.
Recap. Bronfenbrenner nested the child in five systems from the immediate microsystem out to the time-based chronosystem, with the mesosystem linking settings the child inhabits and the exosystem touching them from settings they do not. Thomas and Chess named three temperaments (easy, difficult, slow-to-warm-up) and made goodness of fit the key. Baumrind gave three parenting styles, and Maccoby and Martin added the neglectful fourth on demandingness × responsiveness, with authoritative (warm + firm) the healthiest. Bem offered gender schema theory and androgyny; Gilligan answered Kohlberg's justice ethic with an ethic of care; Harlow proved contact comfort, not food, drives attachment. Baltes made development lifelong and gave SOC for adapting to loss; Ainsworth's Strange Situation classified attachment into secure, avoidant, and ambivalent, with Main adding disorganized; and self-concept matures from concrete outer traits toward abstract inner identity.

3.15 The complete map: every theory of development and its theorist

Developmental psychology asks how thought, personality, morality, and relationships change from infancy to old age. This map gathers the whole landscape in five families: cognitive & language, psychosocial & personality, moral, attachment & social, and ecological & lifespan. Learn the name, the theorist, and the one-line hook so any paper cue is instantly placed.

Key. Each row pairs a theory or model with the theorist who is the standard exam answer and a single-line core idea. Rows are numbered continuously across all five families; a theory is filed under the family that best fits its central question.

Family A: Cognitive & language development (how does thinking and language grow?)

#Theory / modelTheorist(s)Core idea in one line
1Cognitive stagesJean PiagetThinking matures through four fixed stages (sensorimotor, preoperational, concrete operational, formal operational) via assimilation and accommodation.
2Sociocultural theory & ZPDLev VygotskyCognition is built socially; learning happens in the zone of proximal development with a more-knowledgeable other.
3Scaffolding & modes of representationJerome BrunerAdults scaffold the ZPD; knowledge is coded enactive → iconic → symbolic through a spiral curriculum.
4Information-processing viewRobert SieglerDevelopment is gradual growth in memory, attention, and strategy use, not discrete stages; the mind as a computer.
5Language acquisition device (nativist)Noam ChomskyChildren are born with an innate LAD and universal grammar, so language emerges naturally on exposure.
6Behaviourist language learningB.F. SkinnerLanguage is learned like any behaviour, through imitation, reinforcement, and shaping of verbal responses.
7Theory of mindDavid Premack & Simon Baron-CohenAround age 4 children grasp that others hold beliefs and desires different from their own (false-belief task).

Family B: Psychosocial & personality development (how does the self and personality form?)

#Theory / modelTheorist(s)Core idea in one line
8Psychosexual stagesSigmund FreudPersonality forms through five stages (oral, anal, phallic, latency, genital) driven by libido; fixation shapes the adult.
9Psychosocial eight stagesErik EriksonLife is eight crises from trust vs mistrust to integrity vs despair; each stage must resolve a social conflict.
10Identity statusesJames MarciaAdolescent identity falls into four statuses (diffusion, foreclosure, moratorium, achievement) by crisis and commitment.
11Adolescent egocentrismDavid ElkindTeens feel watched by an imaginary audience and believe in a personal fable of uniqueness and invulnerability.
12Separation-individuationMargaret MahlerThe infant psychologically emerges from fusion with mother through phases to a separate, individuated self.
13Maturation theoryArnold GesellDevelopment unfolds on a biological timetable; behaviour appears in a fixed, genetically driven maturational sequence.
14Developmental tasksRobert HavighurstEach life period sets tasks that must be mastered for happiness and success at the next stage.

Family C: Moral development (how do we learn right from wrong?)

#Theory / modelTheorist(s)Core idea in one line
15Stages of moral developmentLawrence KohlbergMoral reasoning grows through three levels (preconventional, conventional, postconventional) and six stages.
16Ethic of careCarol GilliganWomen reason morally through care and relationships, not just abstract justice; a critique of Kohlberg's male bias.
17Heteronomous to autonomous moralityJean PiagetChildren move from rigid rule-bound moral realism to flexible, intention-based moral relativism.

Family D: Attachment & social development (how do early bonds form?)

#Theory / modelTheorist(s)Core idea in one line
18Attachment theoryJohn BowlbyInfants are biologically primed to bond with a caregiver; a secure base and internal working model shape later life.
19Strange Situation & attachment stylesMary AinsworthA lab procedure classifies infants as secure, anxious-avoidant, or anxious-resistant by their response to separation.
20Contact comfort / surrogate motherHarry HarlowRhesus monkeys chose a soft cloth mother over a wire feeder, showing attachment rests on comfort, not food.
21ImprintingKonrad LorenzNewly hatched birds bond to the first moving object during a critical period, an innate rapid attachment.
22Social learning / observational learningAlbert BanduraChildren learn behaviour by watching and imitating models (the Bobo doll study) through vicarious reinforcement.
23Parenting stylesDiana BaumrindParenting varies along warmth and control into authoritative, authoritarian, and permissive styles.
24Temperament & goodness of fitAlexander Thomas & Stella ChessBabies show innate temperaments (easy, difficult, slow-to-warm-up); outcomes depend on fit with the environment.

Family E: Ecological & lifespan development (how do context and the whole life shape growth?)

#Theory / modelTheorist(s)Core idea in one line
25Ecological systems theoryUrie BronfenbrennerDevelopment is nested in layered systems: micro-, meso-, exo-, macro-, and chronosystem.
26Lifespan perspective & SOCPaul BaltesDevelopment is lifelong; ageing well means selective optimization with compensation (SOC) across gains and losses.
27Transactional modelArnold SameroffChild and environment continuously shape each other over time; outcomes emerge from reciprocal transactions.
28ResilienceMichael RutterSome children thrive despite adversity through protective factors that buffer risk (steeling effect).
Trap. Do not swap the famous pairs: Piaget = cognitive stages while Vygotsky = social ZPD; Bowlby founded attachment theory but Ainsworth ran the Strange Situation; Kohlberg = justice stages while Gilligan = ethic of care; Harlow (contact comfort, monkeys) is not Lorenz (imprinting, birds); Chomsky (innate LAD) is the opposite of Skinner (learned language).
Recap. Twenty-eight theories across five families: cognitive & language (Piaget, Vygotsky, Bruner, Siegler, Chomsky vs Skinner, theory of mind), psychosocial & personality (Freud, Erikson, Marcia, Elkind, Mahler, Gesell, Havighurst), moral (Kohlberg, Gilligan, Piaget), attachment & social (Bowlby, Ainsworth, Harlow, Lorenz, Bandura, Baumrind, Thomas & Chess), and ecological & lifespan (Bronfenbrenner, Baltes, Sameroff, Rutter). Fix each name to its theorist and one-line idea and no developmental cue can catch you out.

4 Abnormal & Clinical Psychology

Abnormal psychology studies patterns of thought, feeling and behaviour that fall outside the ordinary range and cause problems for the person or those around them. Clinical psychology takes that knowledge and applies it: assessment, diagnosis, formulation and treatment of psychological distress. This section builds from the ground up – what "abnormal" even means, how disorders are classified, why they arise, and the main families of disorder you must recognise for the entrance.

4.1 What counts as "abnormal"? The 4 Ds

There is no single line that separates the normal from the abnormal. Instead, clinicians weigh several overlapping criteria, remembered as the 4 Ds. The more of them that apply, and the more strongly, the more confidently we call a pattern disordered.

  • Deviance – the behaviour departs from what a society considers acceptable or statistically usual. Hearing voices no one else hears deviates from shared norms of perception.
  • Distress – the person suffers. Someone with severe anxiety feels genuine anguish. (Note: distress is not always present; a person in a manic episode may feel wonderful.)
  • Dysfunction – the pattern interferes with daily life: work, study, relationships, self-care. This is the most clinically weighted of the four.
  • Danger – the behaviour poses risk of harm to self or others. This is the least common criterion and is over-emphasised in popular thinking.
Key. No single D is necessary or sufficient. A skydiver deviates and courts danger yet has no disorder; a grieving widow feels intense distress that is not pathological. Diagnosis rests on the pattern across the Ds, its persistence, and its context.
Trap. "Abnormal = statistically rare" is only one narrow model. Very high intelligence is statistically rare but not a disorder; depression is common yet clearly pathological. Rarity alone never defines abnormality.

4.2 Models of abnormality

Before modern classification, different lenses competed to define what "abnormal" was. Each still shapes how we think today.

  • Statistical model – abnormality as deviation from the average (e.g. an IQ two standard deviations below the mean). Clean but blind to whether the deviation is harmful.
  • Sociocultural model – abnormality is defined by violation of a culture's norms and roles; what is disordered in one society may be accepted in another. Highlights labelling and cultural relativity.
  • Biological (medical) model – disorders are illnesses with organic causes (genes, neurotransmitters, brain structure) treated like physical disease.
  • Psychological model – abnormality arises from mental processes: unconscious conflict, faulty learning, distorted cognition, or blocked growth, depending on the school.

A short history

Who → what. Philippe Pinel (France, late 1700s) pioneered moral treatment – he unchained inmates at the Bicêtre and Salpêtrière asylums and insisted the mentally ill be treated humanely rather than as prisoners or the possessed. He shifted madness from a moral or demonic problem to a medical one.
Who → what. Emil Kraepelin (Germany, late 1800s) is the father of modern psychiatric classification. He grouped disorders by observed symptom clusters and course, distinguishing dementia praecox (later renamed schizophrenia) from manic-depressive insanity (mood disorder). His descriptive method is the direct ancestor of the DSM and ICD.

4.3 Classification systems: DSM-5-TR vs ICD-11

Two manuals dominate. Knowing who publishes each, how they are built, and how they differ is a common exam target.

FeatureDSM-5-TRICD-11
PublisherAmerican Psychiatric Association (APA)World Health Organization (WHO)
Full nameDiagnostic and Statistical Manual of Mental Disorders, 5th ed., Text Revision (2022)International Classification of Diseases, 11th revision (in force 2022)
ScopeMental disorders onlyAll diseases and health conditions; mental disorders are one chapter
Primary useDetailed clinical/research diagnosis, dominant in the USAGlobal health statistics, morbidity/mortality coding, used worldwide
StructureExplicit diagnostic criteria sets with symptom counts and durationsPrototype/clinical descriptions with diagnostic guidelines, more flexible
AccessPurchased (copyright revenue for APA)Freely available
ApproachLargely categorical (present/absent), with growing dimensional elementsCategorical but more openly dimensional (e.g. personality disorder severity)
Key. Categorical classification asks "does the person have the disorder, yes or no?" and sorts people into discrete boxes. Dimensional classification rates traits or severity along a continuum (mild → severe). Real distress is continuous, so both manuals are drifting toward dimensional thinking, most visibly in how ICD-11 rates personality disorder by severity rather than by named type.
Trap. The DSM is not a global standard. Outside North America, and for all official health statistics, the WHO's ICD is the reference. Do not assume "DSM" means "the world's manual".

4.4 Etiological models: why disorders arise

"Etiology" means cause. Several theoretical models each explain psychopathology through a different mechanism.

  • Biological – genetics, neurotransmitter imbalance (e.g. serotonin in depression, dopamine in psychosis), hormones, brain structure and injury.
  • Psychodynamic – symptoms express unconscious conflict, usually rooted in early childhood, as defended-against impulses break through (Sigmund Freud's legacy).
  • Behavioural – disorders are learned: maladaptive responses acquired through classical conditioning, operant reinforcement or modelling. A phobia is a conditioned fear.
  • Cognitive – distorted, irrational or negative thinking drives the disorder; change the thought and the emotion follows (Aaron Beck, Albert Ellis).
  • Humanistic – distress arises when growth toward one's potential is blocked, often by conditions of worth imposed by others (Carl Rogers, Abraham Maslow).
  • Sociocultural – family systems, poverty, discrimination, culture and social stress shape and label disorder.

Integrative models

Modern clinical psychology rarely picks just one lens.

  • Biopsychosocial model (George Engel) – every disorder reflects an interaction of biological, psychological and social factors; assessment and treatment must address all three.
  • Diathesis-stress model – a person carries a predisposition or vulnerability (the diathesis, e.g. genetic risk) that only becomes a disorder when enough environmental stress is applied. Neither alone is sufficient; disorder emerges from their interaction.
Why. The diathesis-stress idea explains why two people facing the same trauma diverge: one develops PTSD, the other does not. Their thresholds differ because their underlying vulnerabilities differ. It is the single most examined integrative concept in this topic.

4.5 Anxiety disorders

Anxiety disorders share excessive, persistent fear or worry that is out of proportion to real threat and impairs functioning. In DSM-5-TR the anxiety chapter was slimmed down: OCD and PTSD were moved out into their own chapters (see below).

DisorderCore feature
Generalised anxiety disorder (GAD)Chronic, free-floating, uncontrollable worry about many everyday things for ≥6 months, with restlessness, fatigue, tension, poor sleep.
Panic disorderRecurrent unexpected panic attacks (surges of terror with palpitations, breathlessness, chest pain, fear of dying) plus persistent worry about further attacks.
Specific phobiaMarked, irrational fear of a specific object or situation (heights, spiders, blood, flying), with avoidance.
AgoraphobiaFear of places from which escape is hard or help unavailable (crowds, open spaces, public transport); may lead to being housebound.
Social anxiety disorderIntense fear of scrutiny and negative evaluation in social or performance situations, driving avoidance.

A 24-year-old avoids buses and shopping malls; last month, boxed in a crowd, her heart raced, she felt she was suffocating and about to die, and she fled. She now maps her day around escape routes. → Panic attacks with agoraphobic avoidance.

OCD and related disorders (now a separate DSM-5 chapter)

Obsessive-compulsive disorder (OCD) combines obsessions (intrusive, unwanted thoughts, images or urges that cause anxiety, e.g. contamination fears) with compulsions (repetitive acts or mental rituals performed to reduce that anxiety, e.g. washing, checking, counting). The compulsion briefly relieves distress, which reinforces it. Related disorders in this chapter include body dysmorphic disorder, hoarding disorder and trichotillomania (hair-pulling).

Trauma- and stressor-related disorders (also a separate DSM-5 chapter)

Post-traumatic stress disorder (PTSD) follows exposure to a life-threatening or horrifying event and is defined by four symptom clusters: intrusion (flashbacks, nightmares), avoidance of reminders, negative changes in mood and cognition, and hyperarousal (startle, hypervigilance), lasting more than one month. Acute stress disorder is the same picture within the first month.

Trap. In DSM-5-TR, OCD and PTSD are not anxiety disorders – each has its own chapter. In the older DSM-IV they were housed under anxiety, and ICD keeps them nearby. Many exam items hinge on this reclassification.

4.6 Mood (affective) disorders

Mood disorders centre on disturbance of emotional state – either depressed, elevated, or swinging between the two.

DisorderCore feature
Major depressive disorder (MDD)≥2 weeks of depressed mood and/or loss of interest (anhedonia) plus symptoms such as weight/appetite change, sleep disturbance, fatigue, worthlessness, poor concentration, thoughts of death. Episodic.
Persistent depressive disorder (dysthymia)Chronic low-grade depression lasting ≥2 years; less intense than MDD but grinding and long-lasting.
Bipolar I disorderAt least one full manic episode (elevated/irritable mood, grandiosity, reduced sleep, pressured speech, risky behaviour) ≥1 week; depressive episodes usually occur too but are not required.
Bipolar II disorderAt least one hypomanic episode (milder, shorter elevation, no marked impairment or psychosis) plus at least one major depressive episode. Never a full mania.
Cyclothymic disorder≥2 years of fluctuating hypomanic and depressive symptoms that never reach full episode criteria; the "milder chronic" cousin of bipolar.
Key. The line between Bipolar I and II is the presence of full mania (I) versus only hypomania (II). Mania causes marked impairment or psychosis or hospitalisation; hypomania does not. Dysthymia is to MDD what cyclothymia is to bipolar – the chronic, milder shadow.
Who → what. Aaron Beck proposed the cognitive triad of depression: the depressed person holds automatic negative views of the (1) self ("I am worthless"), (2) world/experience ("everything is against me"), and (3) future ("it will never get better"). These distortions, not external reality, sustain the low mood, and correcting them is the target of cognitive therapy (CBT).

A 30-year-old man reports two weeks of not being able to enjoy anything, sleeping twelve hours yet exhausted, feeling he has "ruined everything" and that his family would be better off without him. → Major depressive episode with the cognitive triad clearly active.

4.7 Schizophrenia spectrum

Schizophrenia is a severe disorder of thought, perception and behaviour in which contact with reality is impaired (psychosis). Symptoms are grouped as positive and negative.

  • Positive symptoms (added to normal experience): delusions (fixed false beliefs), hallucinations (usually auditory), disorganised speech, grossly disorganised or catatonic behaviour.
  • Negative symptoms (normal functions lost): flat affect, alogia (poverty of speech), avolition (lack of drive), anhedonia, social withdrawal. These respond poorly to medication and predict worse outcome.
Who → what. Eugen Bleuler coined the term "schizophrenia" (splitting of psychic functions, not "split personality") and defined its core fundamental symptoms as the 4 A's: Association disturbance (loosened, illogical thought), Affect disturbance (blunted or inappropriate emotion), Ambivalence (contradictory feelings/impulses at once), and Autism (withdrawal into an inner private world). Hallucinations and delusions he regarded as secondary.
Who → what. Kurt Schneider listed first-rank symptoms he considered strongly suggestive of schizophrenia: audible thoughts, voices arguing or commenting on one's actions, thought insertion, thought withdrawal, thought broadcasting, made (externally controlled) feelings, impulses and acts, and delusional perception. Useful clinically, though not disorder-specific.

Subtypes: a historical note

DSM-IV and ICD-10 named subtypes – paranoid, disorganised (hebephrenic), catatonic, undifferentiated and residual. DSM-5 abolished these subtypes because they were unstable and had poor predictive value, replacing them with a dimensional rating of symptom severity.

Cause and course

The dopamine hypothesis proposes that positive symptoms arise from overactivity of dopamine transmission (especially D2 receptors) in mesolimbic pathways; antipsychotics block D2 receptors, and dopamine agonists like amphetamine can trigger psychosis. It is an incomplete picture (glutamate and serotonin also matter) but a heavily tested one. Prognosis is better with acute/sudden onset, clear precipitating stress, good pre-morbid functioning, prominent mood/positive symptoms and late onset; it is worse with insidious onset, prominent negative symptoms and early onset.

Trap. "Schizophrenia" does not mean "split/multiple personality" – that is dissociative identity disorder. Bleuler's "splitting" referred to the fragmentation of thought, emotion and will within one mind, not several personalities.

4.8 Personality disorders

A personality disorder is an enduring, inflexible, pervasive pattern of inner experience and behaviour that deviates markedly from cultural expectation, is stable from adolescence/early adulthood, and causes distress or impairment. DSM-5 groups the ten into three clusters.

ClusterThemeDisorders
AOdd / eccentricParanoid (pervasive distrust), Schizoid (detachment, indifference to relationships), Schizotypal (odd beliefs, magical thinking, eccentric behaviour)
BDramatic / emotional / erraticAntisocial (disregard for others' rights, deceit, no remorse), Borderline (unstable relationships, self-image and affect, impulsivity, fear of abandonment), Histrionic (excessive emotionality and attention-seeking), Narcissistic (grandiosity, need for admiration, lack of empathy)
CAnxious / fearfulAvoidant (social inhibition, hypersensitivity to rejection), Dependent (excessive need to be cared for, clinging), Obsessive-compulsive personality (rigid perfectionism, orderliness, control)
Trap. Obsessive-compulsive personality disorder (OCPD) is a Cluster C personality style of rigid perfectionism and is ego-syntonic (the person sees nothing wrong). OCD is the anxiety-related disorder of obsessions and compulsions and is ego-dystonic (the person is tormented by it). Different diagnoses, easy to confuse.

4.9 Other major categories (briefly)

  • Dissociative disorders – disruption of memory, identity or awareness: dissociative identity disorder (two or more distinct personality states, formerly "multiple personality"), dissociative amnesia (inability to recall important, often traumatic personal information), depersonalisation/derealisation disorder (feeling detached from oneself or that the world is unreal).
  • Somatic symptom & related disorders – distressing bodily symptoms with disproportionate thoughts and behaviour: somatic symptom disorder, illness anxiety disorder (health anxiety), and conversion disorder (functional neurological symptoms such as paralysis or blindness with no organic cause).
  • Feeding & eating disorders – anorexia nervosa (restriction, very low weight, intense fear of gaining, distorted body image), bulimia nervosa (binge–purge cycles at normal or above weight), binge-eating disorder (recurrent binges without compensatory purging).
  • Neurodevelopmental disorders – onset in the developmental period: intellectual disability (deficits in intellectual and adaptive functioning), autism spectrum disorder (social-communication deficits plus restricted, repetitive behaviours), attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (ADHD) (inattention and/or hyperactivity-impulsivity).
  • Substance-related and addictive disorders – problematic patterns of substance use (tolerance, withdrawal, craving, loss of control, use despite harm) plus behavioural addictions such as gambling disorder.

4.10 Clinical process basics

Diagnosis is only part of clinical work. Two foundational tools:

The Mental Status Examination (MSE)

The MSE is a structured snapshot of the patient's current mental functioning at the time of interview – the psychological equivalent of a physical exam. Its components:

  1. Appearance and behaviour – grooming, dress, eye contact, psychomotor activity, cooperativeness.
  2. Speech – rate, volume, tone, fluency.
  3. Mood and affect – mood is the patient's reported emotional state; affect is the observed, moment-to-moment expression (its range, appropriateness, congruence).
  4. Thought process – the form/flow of thinking (logical, tangential, loosened, flight of ideas).
  5. Thought content – delusions, obsessions, suicidal or homicidal ideation.
  6. Perception – hallucinations, illusions.
  7. Cognition – orientation (time, place, person), attention, memory.
  8. Insight and judgement – awareness of illness and quality of decision-making.
Key. Distinguish mood (subjective, reported, the "climate") from affect (objective, observed, the "weather"). And distinguish thought process (how thoughts flow) from thought content (what the thoughts are about). Both pairs are classic viva and MCQ traps.

Formulation and regulation

Case formulation goes beyond the diagnostic label to build a working explanation of this person's problems – often organised as predisposing, precipitating, perpetuating and protective factors (the "4 Ps") – and it directly guides the treatment plan. Where diagnosis says "what", formulation says "why, for this person, now".

Key. In India, the practice of clinical psychology is regulated by the Rehabilitation Council of India (RCI) under the RCI Act, 1992. Only professionals with an RCI-recognised qualification (such as the M.Phil in Clinical Psychology, now transitioning to an RCI-approved MA route) registered on the Central Rehabilitation Register may practise as clinical psychologists. This is why entrance exams like SGT's feed into RCI-recognised programmes.
Recap. Abnormality is judged by the 4 Ds (deviance, distress, dysfunction, danger), read through statistical, sociocultural, biological and psychological models, on a road paved by Philippe Pinel's moral treatment and Emil Kraepelin's classification. Two manuals classify disorders – the APA's DSM-5-TR (detailed, criteria-based, categorical drifting dimensional) and the WHO's ICD-11 (global, guideline-based, more dimensional). Causes are framed biologically, psychodynamically, behaviourally, cognitively, humanistically and socioculturally, integrated by the biopsychosocial and diathesis-stress models. The major families: anxiety (GAD, panic, phobias, agoraphobia, social anxiety) with OCD and PTSD now split off; mood disorders (MDD, dysthymia, bipolar I vs II, cyclothymia, with Beck's cognitive triad); the schizophrenia spectrum (positive vs negative symptoms, Bleuler's 4 A's, Schneider's first-rank symptoms, the dopamine hypothesis); ten personality disorders in Clusters A, B and C; and dissociative, somatic, eating, neurodevelopmental and substance-related disorders. Clinical work rests on the MSE and case formulation, and in India is governed by the RCI.

4.11 Neurodevelopmental and childhood disorders

Neurodevelopmental disorders are a group of conditions with onset in the developmental period, typically before a child enters grade school. Their defining feature is that they produce impairments of personal, social, academic, or occupational functioning that arise from disturbances in the growth and maturation of the brain and nervous system. Because the deficits appear early and shape the course of later development, they are placed at the very front of the DSM-5-TR (Diagnostic and Statistical Manual of Mental Disorders, Fifth Edition, Text Revision) classification. This subsection covers the five clusters most likely to be tested: intellectual developmental disorder, autism spectrum disorder, attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder, specific learning disorder, and the disruptive behaviour disorders of childhood.

Intellectual developmental disorder

Intellectual developmental disorder (the term used in ICD-11, the Eleventh Revision of the International Classification of Diseases; DSM-5-TR names it intellectual developmental disorder or intellectual disability) is defined by deficits in two domains that both begin during the developmental period. The first is intellectual functioning: reasoning, problem solving, planning, abstract thinking, judgement, and learning, confirmed both by clinical assessment and by standardized, individually administered intelligence testing. The second is adaptive functioning: how well the person meets everyday standards of personal independence and social responsibility across the conceptual (language, reading, money, time), social (empathy, friendship, social judgement), and practical (self-care, job, money management) domains.

Key. A modern diagnosis of intellectual developmental disorder does not rest on an IQ number alone. Severity is graded by adaptive functioning, not by the intelligence quotient, because it is adaptive skill that determines the level of support a person needs. This is a deliberate shift away from the older practice of defining the categories purely by IQ bands.

DSM-5-TR recognizes four severity levels, defined by how much support the person requires across the conceptual, social, and practical domains.

  • Mild: the largest group; academic difficulties emerge in school years, but many adults live and work with intermittent support. May hold a job and manage a household with help on complex tasks such as banking or legal decisions.
  • Moderate: markedly limited conceptual skills; language and academic progress are slow; ongoing support for daily living is needed.
  • Severe: very limited conceptual skills; little understanding of written language or number; the person needs support for all activities of daily living.
  • Profound: conceptual skills largely involve the physical world; the person depends on others for all aspects of daily physical care, health, and safety, though may take part in some routine activities.
Trap. Do not confuse intellectual developmental disorder with global developmental delay. Global developmental delay is a provisional label reserved for children under age five who cannot yet be reliably assessed by standardized testing. It is a placeholder, not a lifelong diagnosis, and must be re-evaluated as the child grows.

Autism spectrum disorder

Autism spectrum disorder is a neurodevelopmental condition built on two core symptom clusters that must both be present. The DSM-5-TR merged several earlier separate labels (autistic disorder, Asperger's disorder, and pervasive developmental disorder) into a single spectrum precisely because they shared this dyad and differed mainly in severity.

  1. Persistent deficits in social communication and social interaction across multiple contexts: problems in social-emotional reciprocity (back-and-forth conversation, sharing of interests and emotions); deficits in nonverbal communicative behaviours (eye contact, gesture, facial expression); and difficulties developing, maintaining, and understanding relationships.
  2. Restricted, repetitive patterns of behaviour, interests, or activities, shown by at least two of: stereotyped or repetitive motor movements, speech, or use of objects; insistence on sameness and inflexible routines; highly restricted, fixated interests of abnormal intensity; and hyper- or hypo-reactivity to sensory input (for example, indifference to pain or adverse reaction to specific sounds or textures).

Symptoms must be present in the early developmental period, though they may not fully manifest until social demands exceed capacities. Severity is rated by the level of support required (Level 1 requiring support, Level 2 requiring substantial support, Level 3 requiring very substantial support), and clinicians specify whether it occurs with or without accompanying intellectual or language impairment.

Attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder

Attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (ADHD) is a persistent pattern of inattention and/or hyperactivity-impulsivity that interferes with functioning or development. Several symptoms must be present before age 12, in two or more settings (for example, home and school), a criterion designed to rule out situational misbehaviour. DSM-5-TR describes three presentations, and knowing the difference is a common exam point.

  • Predominantly inattentive presentation: difficulty sustaining attention, careless mistakes, not seeming to listen, disorganization, forgetfulness, and losing things, without enough hyperactive symptoms.
  • Predominantly hyperactive-impulsive presentation: fidgeting, leaving one's seat, restlessness, talking excessively, blurting out answers, difficulty waiting turns, and interrupting.
  • Combined presentation: criteria for both inattention and hyperactivity-impulsivity are met; this is the most common presentation in clinical samples.
Trap. ADHD uses the word presentation, not subtype, because the picture can change over a person's life: a child who is combined may look predominantly inattentive as a teenager. Also, the onset criterion is before age 12 in DSM-5-TR, raised from the "before age 7" used in the earlier DSM-IV. Examiners often test this specific number.

Specific learning disorder

Specific learning disorder is a difficulty learning and using academic skills that is well below what is expected for the person's age and not explained by intellectual disability, poor schooling, or sensory problems. DSM-5-TR folds the older separate reading, writing, and mathematics disorders into one diagnosis with specifiers.

  • With impairment in reading (dyslexia): problems with word reading accuracy, reading rate or fluency, and reading comprehension.
  • With impairment in written expression (dysgraphia): difficulties with spelling accuracy, grammar and punctuation, and clarity or organization of written expression.
  • With impairment in mathematics (dyscalculia): difficulties with number sense, memorization of arithmetic facts, accurate or fluent calculation, and mathematical reasoning.

Disruptive behaviour disorders

Two childhood disorders are marked by problems in self-control of emotions and behaviour that bring the child into conflict with authority and social norms.

Oppositional defiant disorder is a recurrent pattern of angry or irritable mood, argumentative or defiant behaviour, and vindictiveness lasting at least six months, directed especially at authority figures. The child loses their temper, argues with adults, defies rules, and blames others, but generally does not seriously violate the basic rights of others or major societal rules.

Conduct disorder is more severe: a repetitive pattern in which the basic rights of others or major age-appropriate norms are violated, grouped into aggression to people and animals, destruction of property, deceitfulness or theft, and serious rule violations (for example, truancy or running away). Conduct disorder in childhood is a recognized precursor to antisocial personality disorder in adulthood, which cannot be diagnosed before age 18.

Childhood disorderCore defining feature
Intellectual developmental disorderDeficits in intellectual AND adaptive functioning, onset in developmental period
Autism spectrum disorderSocial-communication deficits PLUS restricted, repetitive behaviours
Attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorderInattention and/or hyperactivity-impulsivity, onset before age 12, two or more settings
Specific learning disorderAcademic skill (reading, writing, or maths) far below expected, not due to low IQ
Oppositional defiant disorderAngry, argumentative, defiant pattern toward authority, without violating others' rights
Conduct disorderRepeated violation of others' rights and major social norms

4.12 Trauma-, stressor-, dissociative, and somatic disorders

These three families share a common thread: psychological distress that follows overwhelming experience or is expressed through disruptions of consciousness, memory, identity, or bodily function. DSM-5-TR groups the trauma- and stressor-related disorders together, treating exposure to a stressful or traumatic event as an explicit diagnostic requirement, a rare instance in the manual where cause is written into the definition.

Trauma- and stressor-related disorders

Post-traumatic stress disorder (PTSD) follows exposure to actual or threatened death, serious injury, or sexual violence, whether experienced directly, witnessed, learned about happening to a close other, or through repeated exposure to aversive details (as with first responders). Its symptoms fall into four clusters:

  • Intrusion: recurrent involuntary memories, distressing dreams, flashbacks in which the event feels as if it is recurring, and marked distress at reminders.
  • Avoidance: effortful avoidance of memories, thoughts, feelings, or external reminders of the event.
  • Negative alterations in cognition and mood: inability to recall key features, persistent negative beliefs, distorted blame, persistent negative emotional states, detachment, and inability to feel positive emotion.
  • Alterations in arousal and reactivity: irritability, reckless behaviour, hypervigilance, exaggerated startle, concentration problems, and sleep disturbance.
Key. The single most tested distinction here is duration. Acute stress disorder describes a similar reaction lasting from three days up to one month after the trauma. If the disturbance persists beyond one month, the diagnosis becomes post-traumatic stress disorder. Acute stress disorder is not simply "mild PTSD"; it is defined by its short window.

Adjustment disorder is a broader, milder category: emotional or behavioural symptoms (depressed mood, anxiety, or conduct disturbance) that develop within three months of an identifiable stressor and are out of proportion to it or cause significant impairment. The stressor need not be traumatic; it can be an ordinary life event such as a divorce, job loss, or relocation. Symptoms resolve within six months once the stressor or its consequences end.

Trap. Adjustment disorder is often called a residual or "wastebasket" category, but it has strict boundaries: it applies only when the reaction does not meet criteria for another specific disorder and does not represent normal bereavement. If the full PTSD or major depressive picture is present, that specific diagnosis takes priority.

Dissociative disorders

Dissociation is a disruption of the normally integrated functions of consciousness, memory, identity, emotion, perception, and behaviour. The dissociative disorders are frequently, though not always, linked to trauma.

  • Dissociative identity disorder: the presence of two or more distinct personality states (in some cultures described as an experience of possession), accompanied by recurrent gaps in the recall of everyday events, personal information, or traumatic events that go beyond ordinary forgetting. It was formerly called multiple personality disorder.
  • Dissociative amnesia: an inability to recall important autobiographical information, usually of a traumatic or stressful nature, that is too extensive to be ordinary forgetfulness. It may occur with dissociative fugue, apparently purposeful travel or bewildered wandering with amnesia for identity.
  • Depersonalization/derealization disorder: persistent or recurrent experiences of unreality or detachment from one's own self (depersonalization: feeling like an outside observer of one's thoughts or body) or from surroundings (derealization: the world feeling dreamlike, foggy, or visually distorted), while reality testing remains intact.
Trap. Dissociative identity disorder is heavily mythologized. Two corrections matter for exams. First, it is not the same as schizophrenia; "split personality" is a popular misnomer for schizophrenia, which involves psychosis, not separate identities. Second, in depersonalization/derealization the person knows the experience is not real; intact reality testing is what separates it from a psychotic disorder.

Somatic symptom and related disorders

These disorders feature distressing bodily symptoms together with abnormal thoughts, feelings, and behaviours in response to them. DSM-5-TR deliberately shifted the emphasis away from whether a symptom is "medically unexplained" and toward the person's excessive reaction, because unexplained-symptom judgements proved unreliable.

  • Somatic symptom disorder: one or more distressing somatic symptoms plus disproportionate and persistent thoughts, high anxiety about health, or excessive time and energy devoted to the symptoms. The bodily symptom is real and present; the problem lies in the excessive response.
  • Illness anxiety disorder: preoccupation with having or acquiring a serious illness when somatic symptoms are absent or only mild. Anxiety centres on the meaning and implication of health, not on a present symptom. This category absorbed much of what was formerly called hypochondriasis.
  • Functional neurological symptom disorder (conversion disorder): symptoms of altered voluntary motor or sensory function (for example, weakness, paralysis, non-epileptic seizures, or blindness) that are incompatible with recognized neurological disease. The classic finding is la belle indifférence, a striking lack of concern about the deficit, though it is not required for diagnosis.
Key. Distinguish these from factitious disorder and malingering. In somatic symptom, illness anxiety, and conversion disorders the symptoms are not consciously produced; the suffering is genuine. In factitious disorder the person deliberately falsifies symptoms to assume the sick role (an internal motive). In malingering symptoms are faked for an external reward (money, avoiding work), and malingering is not a mental disorder at all.

4.13 Feeding/eating, substance-related, and neurocognitive disorders

Feeding and eating disorders

The eating disorders are disturbances of eating or eating-related behaviour that harm physical health and psychosocial functioning. Three are central to the syllabus.

  • Anorexia nervosa: restriction of energy intake leading to significantly low body weight, an intense fear of gaining weight or persistent behaviour that prevents weight gain, and a disturbance in the way body weight or shape is experienced. Specified as restricting type or binge-eating/purging type. The hallmark is the low body weight.
  • Bulimia nervosa: recurrent episodes of binge eating (eating an unusually large amount with a sense of loss of control) followed by recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviour to prevent weight gain (self-induced vomiting, laxatives, fasting, or excessive exercise), occurring on average at least once a week for three months. Body weight is typically normal or above.
  • Binge-eating disorder: recurrent binge eating with marked distress but without the regular compensatory behaviours seen in bulimia. It is the most common eating disorder and was added as a formal diagnosis in DSM-5.
FeatureAnorexia nervosaBulimia nervosaBinge-eating disorder
Body weightSignificantly lowUsually normal or aboveOften overweight
Binge eatingOnly in one subtypeYes, definingYes, defining
Compensatory behaviourPossible (purging subtype)Yes, definingNo
Core fear/featureFear of weight gain, low weightBinge-purge cycle, loss of controlDistress over bingeing
Trap. The presence or absence of a single feature separates these. If body weight is significantly low, think anorexia nervosa even when bingeing and purging occur. Bulimia nervosa versus binge-eating disorder turns on compensatory behaviour: both binge, but only bulimia regularly purges, fasts, or over-exercises to undo it.

Substance-related and addictive disorders

DSM-5-TR made a major structural change here. The earlier DSM-IV split problem use into two separate diagnoses, substance abuse (a milder pattern) and substance dependence (the more severe, physiological pattern). DSM-5-TR abolished that split and combined them into a single substance use disorder measured on a severity continuum.

Who → what. The American Psychiatric Association, in publishing DSM-5 (2013), collapsed the abuse-versus-dependence dichotomy into one substance use disorder rated as mild (2 to 3 symptoms), moderate (4 to 5), or severe (6 or more) out of eleven criteria spanning impaired control, social impairment, risky use, and pharmacological features.

Two pharmacological criteria deserve careful definition because they are routinely confused.

  • Tolerance: needing markedly increased amounts of the substance to achieve the desired effect, or a markedly diminished effect from the same amount. It reflects the body adapting so that more is required.
  • Withdrawal: a substance-specific syndrome of unpleasant physical and psychological symptoms that appears when a heavy, prolonged user cuts down or stops, and which is often relieved by taking the substance again.
Trap. Tolerance and withdrawal are not the same thing and neither alone equals addiction. Tolerance is "I need more to feel it"; withdrawal is "I feel sick when I stop." A patient on prescribed opioids for pain can develop both physiological tolerance and withdrawal without meeting the behavioural criteria for a substance use disorder, so DSM-5-TR notes that tolerance and withdrawal from appropriately prescribed medication do not count toward the diagnosis.

Neurocognitive disorders

Neurocognitive disorders are conditions in which the primary deficit is an acquired decline in cognition (attention, memory, language, executive function, perception, or social cognition) from a previously higher level. The word "acquired" separates them from the neurodevelopmental disorders, where the deficit was present from early life. Two forms anchor this topic.

Delirium is an acute disturbance of attention and awareness that develops over hours to days, tends to fluctuate through the day, and is accompanied by additional cognitive disturbance. It is a direct physiological consequence of a medical condition, substance intoxication or withdrawal, medication, or a combination. Critically, delirium is usually reversible when the underlying cause is treated.

Major neurocognitive disorder (the DSM-5-TR term that replaced dementia) is a significant decline in one or more cognitive domains that interferes with independence in everyday activities. When the decline is present but does not yet compromise independence, it is labelled mild neurocognitive disorder. The most common cause is Alzheimer's disease, a progressive neurodegenerative condition marked pathologically by amyloid plaques and neurofibrillary tangles, with insidious onset and gradual worsening, memory being the earliest and most prominent loss.

Who → what. Alois Alzheimer, the German psychiatrist and neuropathologist, described the disease that bears his name in 1906 after examining a patient, Auguste Deter, and identifying the characteristic plaques and tangles in her brain at autopsy. His work gave the most common form of dementia its name.

Trap. Delirium versus dementia (major neurocognitive disorder) is a favourite examination contrast. Delirium has acute onset (hours to days), a fluctuating course, impaired attention and consciousness, and is usually reversible. Dementia has gradual onset (months to years), a stable or progressive course, relatively preserved consciousness until late, prominent memory loss, and is usually irreversible. The two can coexist, and a person with dementia is at higher risk of developing a superimposed delirium.

Recap. Neurodevelopmental disorders (intellectual developmental disorder graded by adaptive functioning, autism spectrum disorder's social-communication-plus-repetitive dyad, ADHD's three presentations with onset before age 12, specific learning disorder, and the oppositional defiant/conduct disorder pair) begin in early life. The trauma-related disorders hinge on duration (acute stress disorder within one month, PTSD beyond it, adjustment disorder for milder stressors), while dissociative disorders disrupt identity, memory, and perception with intact reality testing, and somatic disorders express distress through excessive reactions to bodily symptoms. Finally, eating disorders separate on weight and compensatory behaviour, substance use disorder now runs on a single severity continuum (with tolerance and withdrawal kept distinct), and neurocognitive disorders contrast reversible, fluctuating delirium against progressive, memory-led major neurocognitive disorder, the commonest form named after Alois Alzheimer.

4.14 The complete map: models of abnormality & the people behind them

Abnormal psychology asks two linked questions: what causes disorder, and how do we name it. This is the full roster of explanatory models, the disorder-specific theories examiners quote, the classification landmarks, and the critical voices who pushed back. Learn the pairing of model to person to one-line idea; single-best-answer items live exactly here.

Key. A model is a lens that decides what "abnormal" means, where it comes from, and how you treat it. Two big jobs: explain (why does it happen) and classify (what do we call it). Most modern practice is integrative (biopsychosocial), but exams still test each pure model and its author.

Family A: Foundational models of abnormality (where does disorder come from?)

#Model / theory / figureWhoCore idea in one line
1Biological / medical modelEmil Kraepelin (tradition)Disorders are illnesses of the brain and body: genes, neurochemistry, structure; treat with medicine.
2Psychodynamic modelSigmund FreudSymptoms are the surface of unconscious conflict between id, ego, and superego rooted in early childhood.
3Behavioural modelJohn B. Watson, B.F. Skinner, Joseph WolpeAbnormal behaviour is learned through conditioning and reinforcement, so it can be unlearned (Wolpe: systematic desensitisation).
4Cognitive model (cognitive triad)Aaron BeckDistorted automatic thoughts about self, world, and future (the negative cognitive triad) drive depression.
5Rational-emotive model (ABC)Albert EllisNot the Activating event but irrational Beliefs about it cause emotional Consequences; dispute the beliefs.
6Humanistic-existential modelCarl Rogers, Abraham MaslowDisorder is blocked growth: incongruence and conditions of worth (Rogers) or thwarted self-actualisation (Maslow).
7Diathesis-stress modelPaul Meehl (tradition)A predisposing vulnerability (diathesis) becomes disorder only when triggered by enough stress.
8Biopsychosocial modelGeorge EngelIllness emerges from biological, psychological, and social factors together, not one alone.
9Sociocultural modelSociocultural traditionDisorder is shaped by society, culture, family, and social roles, not just the individual.
10General adaptation syndrome (stress)Hans SelyeThe body meets any stressor in three stages: alarm, resistance, exhaustion; chronic stress harms health.
11Family systems viewSystems traditionThe symptom belongs to the whole family system, not the "identified patient" alone.

Family B: Cognitive-behavioural theories of specific disorders (why this particular disorder?)

#Model / theory / figureWhoCore idea in one line
12Learned helplessnessMartin SeligmanUncontrollable aversive events teach that nothing one does matters, producing depression-like giving up.
13Reformulated (attributional) helplessnessLyn Abramson, Seligman & TeasdaleDepression follows internal, stable, global attributions for bad events (a pessimistic explanatory style).
14Hopelessness theory of depressionLyn Abramson, Alloy & MetalskyA later revision: expecting bad outcomes with no control (hopelessness) is the proximal cause of depression.
15Dopamine hypothesis of schizophreniaBiological traditionPositive symptoms link to excess dopamine activity, especially at D2 receptors in mesolimbic pathways.
16Monoamine hypothesis of depressionJoseph SchildkrautDepression reflects a deficit of monoamine neurotransmitters (serotonin, noradrenaline, dopamine).
17Expressed emotion & relapseGeorge Brown, Vaughn & LeffHigh family criticism, hostility, and over-involvement predict relapse in schizophrenia.

Family C: Classification systems & landmark figures (what do we call it?)

21Dementia praecoxEmil KraepelinHis name for an early-onset deteriorating psychosis, later renamed schizophrenia.
22Coining "schizophrenia"Eugen BleulerRenamed dementia praecox schizophrenia (a "splitting" of mind); the four A's (associations, affect, ambivalence, autism).
