Recap. All therapies share common factors, above all the therapeutic alliance (the "Dodo bird verdict"). Trace the family tree: Freud (psychoanalysis) and the neo-Freudians Jung and Adler; behaviour therapy from Mary Cover Jones, Joseph Wolpe (systematic desensitization) and B.F. Skinner (operant methods); the cognitive pair Aaron Beck (Socratic, cognitive triad) vs Albert Ellis (confrontational ABC model); the humanists Carl Rogers (unconditional positive regard, empathy, congruence), Fritz Perls (empty chair) and Victor Frankl (meaning); the third wave of Marsha Linehan (DBT), Steven Hayes (ACT), MBCT and Motivational Interviewing (Miller & Rollnick, with Prochaska & DiClemente's stages of change); and the systems thinkers Minuchin, Bowen and Yalom. If you can attach each technique to its founder and say who it best helps, this section is exam-ready.

6.10 Cognitive and behavioural techniques in depth

The earlier subsections introduced cognitive therapy and behaviour therapy as broad families. This subsection goes inside them, to the actual techniques a therapist uses in the room. Two names dominate the cognitive side: Aaron Temkin Beck, an American psychiatrist who built Cognitive Therapy, and Albert Ellis, an American clinical psychologist who built Rational Emotive Behaviour Therapy. On the behavioural side the central name is Joseph Wolpe, a South African psychiatrist who created systematic desensitization. Learn to tell these approaches apart, because entrance papers love to swap them.

Aaron Beck's cognitive model

Who → what. Aaron Temkin Beck (1921–2021) developed Cognitive Therapy in the 1960s, originally for depression. He proposed that emotional disorders are maintained not by events themselves but by the biased thoughts a person has about those events.

Beck's model rests on three linked ideas. First, the cognitive triad: in depression a person holds negative views of the self ("I am worthless"), the world or current experience ("everything is against me"), and the future ("nothing will ever improve"). These three negative streams feed one another. Second, automatic thoughts: rapid, involuntary appraisals that pop into the mind without deliberate reasoning. They feel true and go unquestioned, which is exactly why they are powerful. Third, deeper schemas or core beliefs: stable underlying assumptions ("I am unlovable") laid down early in life that generate the automatic thoughts.

Beck catalogued recurring errors of reasoning, called cognitive distortions. A working list every candidate should recognise:

  • All-or-nothing thinking (also called black-and-white or dichotomous thinking): seeing things in only two categories, with no middle ground. "If I am not perfect I am a total failure."
  • Overgeneralization: treating one negative event as a never-ending pattern. "I failed this test, so I fail at everything."
  • Catastrophizing (magnification): expecting the worst possible outcome and treating it as certain. "If I stumble in the interview my whole career is over."
  • Personalization: taking responsibility for events that were not one's fault. "My friend is quiet today; it must be because of me."
  • Mental filter (selective abstraction): focusing on a single negative detail while ignoring the whole picture. Dwelling on one critical remark inside a page of praise.
  • Mind reading and fortune telling: assuming you know others' thoughts, or predicting the future negatively, without evidence.
  • Emotional reasoning: treating a feeling as proof of fact. "I feel guilty, so I must have done something wrong."
  • "Should" statements: rigid rules about how oneself and others must behave, producing guilt or anger when broken.
  • Labelling: attaching a fixed global label to oneself. "I am a loser," rather than "I made a mistake."

Beck's therapy trains the client to catch automatic thoughts, test them against evidence, and generate more balanced alternatives, often recorded on a thought record (a worksheet with columns for the situation, the emotion, the automatic thought, evidence for and against, and a balanced response).

Albert Ellis and Rational Emotive Behaviour Therapy

Who → what. Albert Ellis (1913–2007) founded Rational Emotive Therapy in 1955, later renamed Rational Emotive Behaviour Therapy (REBT). It is usually considered the earliest form of cognitive behaviour therapy, predating Beck's clinical work.

Ellis's central tool is the ABC framework:

  • A – Activating event: the situation that seems to trigger distress.
  • B – Belief: the thought or, more precisely, the irrational belief the person holds about the event.
  • C – Consequence: the emotional and behavioural result.

Ellis's key claim is that A does not cause C. Instead B causes C. The same event (failing an exam) produces despair in one person and mild disappointment in another, because their beliefs differ. Ellis argued that distress flows from rigid, absolute demands he summed up as "musts" (which he playfully called musturbation): "I must succeed," "others must treat me well," "the world must be fair." From these grow secondary irrational beliefs such as awfulizing ("it is awful and unbearable"), low frustration tolerance, and global self-rating ("I am worthless").

To ABC he added D and E. D – Disputing: actively challenging the irrational belief. The therapist asks whether the belief is logical, whether there is evidence for it, and whether it is helpful. E – Effective new belief (and new emotion): a flexible preference ("I would strongly prefer to succeed, but I can stand it if I do not") replacing the rigid demand. REBT disputing is comparatively direct and confrontational, in keeping with Ellis's style.

Trap. Beck vs Ellis. Beck → Cognitive Therapy, cognitive triad, automatic thoughts and distortions, a gentle collaborative-empirical style using guided discovery. Ellis → REBT, the ABC(DE) model, irrational "must" beliefs, a more direct disputing style. Both attack thinking, but the labels and vocabulary are distinct. Do not credit the ABC model to Beck or the cognitive triad to Ellis.

Socratic questioning and guided discovery

Socratic questioning is the signature conversational method of Beckian cognitive therapy. Rather than telling the client that a thought is wrong, the therapist asks a series of open questions that lead the client to examine the thought and reach a new conclusion themselves: "What is the evidence for that? Is there another way to see this? What would you say to a friend who thought this?" This process of leading someone to their own insight through questioning is called guided discovery. It is collaborative, not lecturing, and it builds the client's own reasoning skill.

Behavioural experiments

A behavioural experiment is a planned real-world test of a belief. If a socially anxious client believes "if I speak up people will laugh at me," the therapist and client design a small experiment (asking a question in a group) and observe what actually happens. Experience, not argument, updates the belief. Behavioural experiments bridge the cognitive and behavioural halves of CBT: the thought is cognitive, but the test is behavioural.

The behaviour-therapy toolkit

Who → what. Joseph Wolpe (1915–1997) developed systematic desensitization in the 1950s, applying reciprocal inhibition to human fears. It is the classic behavioural treatment for phobias.

Reciprocal inhibition is the principle that two opposing responses cannot occur strongly at the same time: a person cannot be deeply relaxed and intensely anxious simultaneously. Wolpe used relaxation to inhibit anxiety. Systematic desensitization combines three steps:

  1. Train the client in deep relaxation (usually progressive muscle relaxation).
  2. Build an exposure hierarchy (also called an anxiety or fear hierarchy): a ranked ladder of feared situations from least to most frightening, each rated on a subjective scale.
  3. Pair relaxation with the feared images or situations, starting at the bottom of the ladder and climbing only when each step is comfortable, so relaxation replaces anxiety at every rung.

Because desensitization is graded (gradual) and paired with relaxation, it is gentle. Contrast this with the intense exposure methods:

  • Flooding: exposing the client directly to the most feared real situation at full intensity, with no gradual ladder, until anxiety naturally subsides through extinction and habituation. There is no relaxation pairing.
  • Implosion (implosive therapy): like flooding but conducted in imagination rather than real life, often with vividly exaggerated feared scenes.
Trap. Systematic desensitization vs flooding. Desensitization → gradual, uses a hierarchy, pairs exposure with relaxation (reciprocal inhibition), starts at the easy end. Flooding → maximum intensity at once, no hierarchy, no relaxation, relies on habituation. Implosion is flooding done in imagination.

The remaining tools rest on operant conditioning (consequences shape behaviour) or classical conditioning (associations shape responses):

  • Aversion therapy: pairing an unwanted behaviour with an unpleasant stimulus so the behaviour becomes distasteful. A drug that induces nausea when alcohol is drunk. This uses classical conditioning to attach a negative response.
  • Token economy: a structured programme, common on inpatient wards, in which desired behaviours earn tokens (secondary reinforcers) that can later be exchanged for privileges or goods. It is an application of operant reinforcement, associated with Burrhus Frederic Skinner's principles and developed clinically by Teodoro Ayllon and Nathan Azrin.
  • Contingency management: arranging clear consequences (rewards, or loss of rewards) so they follow specific behaviours, making the link between action and outcome explicit. A token economy is one form of it.
  • Relaxation training: teaching bodily calm, most often through progressive muscle relaxation (tensing and releasing muscle groups in sequence), described by Edmund Jacobson, plus controlled breathing. It underpins desensitization and is a treatment for anxiety in its own right.
Key. Cognitive techniques change thoughts (distortions, irrational beliefs) through questioning and testing. Behavioural techniques change behaviour through conditioning (exposure, reinforcement, association). CBT deliberately uses both, because thoughts and actions maintain each other.

6.11 Specialised and process-focused approaches

Beyond the main schools sit several targeted methods and, cutting across every therapy, a set of process constructs that describe what actually happens in the relationship. Both are heavily examined.

Motivational interviewing

Who → what. William Richard Miller and Stephen Rollnick developed Motivational Interviewing (MI), first for addictions. It is a collaborative, client-centred style for strengthening a person's own motivation to change.

MI assumes that lecturing a reluctant person increases resistance. Instead the therapist works with the client's ambivalence (the coexisting wish to change and wish to stay the same), guiding them to voice their own reasons for change, called change talk. Its spirit is often summarised as partnership, acceptance, compassion, and evocation (drawing motivation out of the client rather than imposing it). A well-known principle is rolling with resistance rather than arguing against it.

Crisis intervention

Crisis intervention is brief, immediate, active help for a person overwhelmed by an acute crisis (a suicidal state, disaster, sudden loss). Its goals are short-term: ensure safety, reduce immediate distress, mobilise coping and support, and restore the person to at least their pre-crisis level of functioning. It is time-limited and problem-focused, not deep personality change.

Grief and bereavement work

Grief counselling helps a person mourn a loss and adapt to life without the person or thing lost. Elisabeth Kübler-Ross described commonly cited stages of dying and grief (denial, anger, bargaining, depression, acceptance), though these are now understood as possible reactions rather than a fixed sequence everyone must pass through. Modern approaches also speak of the tasks of mourning (accepting the reality of the loss, working through the pain, adjusting, and finding an enduring connection while moving on), associated with J. William Worden. A key clinical distinction is between normal grief and complicated or prolonged grief that does not ease over time.

Dialectical Behaviour Therapy in depth

Who → what. Marsha M. Linehan developed Dialectical Behaviour Therapy (DBT), originally for chronically suicidal clients and borderline personality disorder. Its "dialectic" is the balance between acceptance of the client as they are and the push for change.

Standard DBT combines individual therapy with a skills group teaching four skill modules:

  • Mindfulness: observing and describing present experience without judgement; the core skill underlying the others.
  • Distress tolerance: surviving crises without making them worse (self-soothing, distraction, radical acceptance).
  • Emotion regulation: understanding, reducing vulnerability to, and changing painful emotions.
  • Interpersonal effectiveness: asking for what one needs and saying no while keeping relationships and self-respect.

DBT is usually classed as a "third-wave" cognitive behavioural therapy because of its heavy use of mindfulness and acceptance.

Eye-Movement Desensitization and Reprocessing

Who → what. Francine Shapiro developed Eye-Movement Desensitization and Reprocessing (EMDR) in the late 1980s, primarily for trauma and post-traumatic stress disorder.

In EMDR the client holds a distressing memory in mind while following a repeated bilateral stimulation (typically the therapist's finger moving side to side, producing back-and-forth eye movements, or alternating taps or tones). Over sets, the memory is meant to lose its intensity and be reprocessed into a less disturbing, more adaptive form. EMDR is a recognised trauma treatment; note that its distinctive ingredient is the bilateral eye-movement component paired with structured recall.

Interpersonal Psychotherapy

Interpersonal Psychotherapy (IPT), developed by Gerald Klerman and Myrna Weissman, is a brief, structured, evidence-based treatment for depression. It links mood symptoms to current relationship problems and focuses on a small number of problem areas: grief (complicated bereavement), role disputes (conflict with a significant person), role transitions (a major life change such as divorce or retirement), and interpersonal deficits (loneliness and difficulty forming relationships). It works on the present social world, not childhood.

Technique / approachOriginatorWhat it does
Cognitive Therapy (triad, automatic thoughts)Aaron Temkin BeckIdentifies and tests distorted thoughts through guided discovery
REBT (ABC model, disputing)Albert EllisDisputes rigid irrational "must" beliefs that cause distress
Systematic desensitizationJoseph WolpeGraded exposure paired with relaxation (reciprocal inhibition)
Progressive muscle relaxationEdmund JacobsonTense-and-release calming used within many therapies
Motivational InterviewingWilliam R. Miller & Stephen RollnickEvokes the client's own change talk, rolls with resistance
Dialectical Behaviour TherapyMarsha M. LinehanBalances acceptance and change; four skill modules
EMDRFrancine ShapiroBilateral eye movements plus recall to reprocess trauma
Interpersonal PsychotherapyGerald Klerman & Myrna WeissmanTreats depression through current relationship problems

Process constructs that cut across all therapies

Whatever the school, certain relationship processes appear in every therapy, and questions often test the definitions.

  • Therapeutic alliance (working alliance): the collaborative bond between client and therapist, made of an emotional bond plus agreement on the goals and tasks of therapy. Its strength is one of the most reliable predictors of good outcome, described in this three-part form by Edward Bordin.
  • Transference: the client unconsciously redirects feelings from important past figures (often a parent) onto the therapist. A concept from Sigmund Freud's psychoanalysis.
  • Countertransference: the therapist's own emotional reactions to the client, whether stirred up by the client or arising from the therapist's own history. It must be recognised so it does not distort the work.
  • Resistance: anything in the client that works against the progress of therapy (missing sessions, changing the subject, falling silent). Psychodynamic therapy treats resistance as meaningful material to be understood, not merely an obstacle.
  • Cultural competence: the therapist's awareness of, respect for, and skill in working across the client's culture, language, values, and identity, so that therapy fits the client rather than imposing the therapist's assumptions.
  • Ethical boundaries and confidentiality: keeping the relationship professional (no exploitation, no dual relationships), and protecting the privacy of what the client discloses. Confidentiality has recognised limits: risk of serious harm to self or others, abuse of a child, or a legal order can require disclosure. Beyond these limits, privacy is a foundation of trust.
Trap. Transference vs countertransference. Transference → the client's feelings displaced onto the therapist. Countertransference → the therapist's feelings toward the client. Match the direction to the correct person.

The common-factors view

Who → what. Saul Rosenzweig in 1936 argued that different therapies work about equally well and that their shared ingredients matter more than their unique techniques. He borrowed the Dodo Bird's line from Alice in Wonderland: "Everybody has won, and all must have prizes," giving the idea its nickname, the Dodo Bird Verdict.

Decades later Bruce E. Wampold gathered the modern evidence for this position in his "contextual model," arguing that factors common to all effective therapies (a strong alliance, the therapist's empathy and belief in the treatment, a shared rationale that makes sense to the client, and the client's own expectation of change) account for much of therapy's benefit, alongside specific techniques. The common-factors view does not say technique is worthless; it says the relationship and shared ingredients carry a great deal of the weight.

Recap. Beck built Cognitive Therapy (cognitive triad, automatic thoughts, distortions, guided discovery via Socratic questioning); Ellis built REBT (the ABC, then D disputing and E, targeting irrational "must" beliefs). Wolpe's systematic desensitization pairs a graded exposure hierarchy with relaxation through reciprocal inhibition, unlike full-intensity flooding or imaginal implosion; the behavioural toolkit also holds aversion therapy, token economies, contingency management, and relaxation training. Specialised methods include Miller and Rollnick's motivational interviewing, crisis intervention, grief work, Linehan's DBT with its four skill modules, Shapiro's EMDR, and Klerman and Weissman's interpersonal psychotherapy. Cutting across all of them are the alliance, transference and countertransference, resistance, cultural competence, ethical boundaries and confidentiality, and the common-factors "Dodo Bird" view from Rosenzweig to Wampold.

6.12 The complete map: every therapy and its founder

This is the safety net for the therapy question you did not see coming. An aptitude paper can name any approach and ask only for its founder or its one central idea. Read this roster from top to bottom once, then again by family, until every name binds to a single line you can recall on sight.

Key. Rows are numbered continuously across all five families so you can quiz yourself by number. Full founder names are given first (later mentions may drop to a surname). "Core idea" is the one clause an examiner would accept as the essence, not the whole theory.
