Trap. Papers love near-miss pairings. Watch these: Wundt founded the lab (1879), but Titchener named structuralism; Watson founded behaviourism, but Pavlov (a physiologist) did the dog experiments; Witmer opened the first clinic (1896), while Hall opened the first American lab; Maslow gave the hierarchy of needs, Rogers gave person-centred therapy (both humanistic, different people); and do not confuse Jung (analytical, collective unconscious) with Adler (individual, inferiority).
Recap. Five families, thirty-five rows: the early schools (structuralism, functionalism, Gestalt), the behaviourist line (Watson to Skinner, Tolman, Hull), depth psychology and its humanistic reply (Freud, Jung, Adler, the neo-Freudians, Maslow, Rogers), the cognitive and modern movements (the cognitive revolution, evolutionary, positive psychology), and the founding firsts (1879 lab, 1896 clinic, 1915 Calcutta department). If you can name the school, the person, and the one-line idea for each, no history-of-psychology item on the aptitude paper should surprise you.

9 Learning & Conditioning

Almost everything a person does, from fearing a dentist's drill to typing without looking at the keys, was learned. Psychology treats learning not as a vague idea but as a precise process that can be measured in the laboratory. This chapter builds the topic from the ground up: what learning is (and is not), the two great forms of conditioning that dominate the exam, the observational and cognitive kinds of learning that go beyond simple stimulus and response, and the clinical applications that flow from all of them. The material is dense with names, so each theorist, test, and study is given in full at first mention; the test loves to ask you to match a person to an idea, and the surest way to lose marks is to confuse two similar-sounding mechanisms.

9.1 What learning is (and is not)

Learning is defined as a relatively permanent change in behaviour or in behavioural potential that results from experience. Each clause of that definition matters. Relatively permanent excludes brief changes caused by fatigue, illness, hunger, or drugs, which fade once the state passes. Change in behaviour or behavioural potential allows for learning that has occurred but is not yet shown (a person may have learned a route without walking it). Results from experience is the decisive clause: it distinguishes learning from changes that are wired into the body by biology.

Two such biological changes must be told apart from learning. Maturation is a change in behaviour due to physical growth and the unfolding of the genetic programme, not experience: an infant walks when the nervous system and muscles are ready, whether or not anyone teaches it. A reflex is an automatic, unlearned response to a stimulus, such as the knee-jerk or the eye-blink to a puff of air; reflexes are present from birth and do not require practice. Learning, by contrast, always traces back to what the organism has experienced.

Trap. A change caused by fatigue, drugs, or maturation is not learning, even though behaviour changes. The exam sets traps by describing a behaviour change and asking whether it counts as learning; check whether the cause was experience.

9.2 Classical conditioning: Ivan Pavlov

Classical conditioning (also called Pavlovian or respondent conditioning) is learning in which a neutral stimulus, by being paired with a stimulus that already produces a response, comes to produce that response by itself. It was discovered by the Russian physiologist Ivan Petrovich Pavlov while he studied salivation in dogs. Pavlov noticed that the dogs salivated not only when food touched the mouth but also at the sight of the attendant who fed them, and he set out to study this systematically.

Who → what. Ivan Petrovich Pavlov → classical (respondent) conditioning; the salivating-dog experiments; the terms unconditioned and conditioned stimulus and response.

The four core terms follow directly from his procedure. The unconditioned stimulus (UCS) is a stimulus that naturally and automatically triggers a response without any learning; for Pavlov this was the food (meat powder). The unconditioned response (UCR) is the natural, automatic reaction to the UCS; here, salivation to food. The conditioned stimulus (CS) is an originally neutral stimulus (a bell or metronome) that, after being paired repeatedly with the UCS, comes to trigger a response. The conditioned response (CR) is the learned reaction to the CS; salivation to the bell alone. Note that the CR and UCR are often the same behaviour (salivation) but are named by what triggers them.

Why. Conditioning works because the CS comes to signal or predict the UCS. The dog salivates to the bell because the bell has become a reliable forecaster of food; learning is essentially the picking up of predictive relationships between events.

Acquisition, extinction, and recovery

Acquisition is the initial stage in which the CS and UCS are paired and the CR is established and strengthened. It works best when the CS appears just before the UCS (forward pairing) rather than at the same time or afterward. Extinction is the weakening and eventual disappearance of the CR when the CS is presented repeatedly without the UCS; the bell keeps ringing but no food comes, and salivation to the bell fades. Spontaneous recovery is the reappearance of a weakened CR after a rest period, showing that extinction does not erase the original learning but suppresses it.

Generalization, discrimination, higher-order conditioning

Stimulus generalization is the tendency for stimuli similar to the CS to also trigger the CR: a dog conditioned to a bell of one pitch will salivate somewhat to bells of nearby pitches. Stimulus discrimination is the learned ability to respond to the CS but not to other, similar stimuli, achieved when only the true CS is paired with the UCS. Higher-order conditioning (second-order conditioning) occurs when an established CS is used to condition a new neutral stimulus: once a bell reliably produces salivation, pairing a light with the bell can make the light produce salivation too, even though the light was never paired directly with food.

Trap. Do not confuse generalization with discrimination. Generalization is responding to similar stimuli (widening); discrimination is responding only to the exact CS (narrowing). They are opposite processes.

9.3 Watson, Little Albert, and Garcia's taste aversion

John Broadus Watson, the American founder of the school of behaviourism, argued that psychology should study only observable behaviour and that emotions could be conditioned. With his assistant Rosalie Rayner, Watson conducted the famous Little Albert experiment in 1920. An eleven-month-old infant, "Albert," was shown a white rat (originally neutral, the CS), and each time he reached for it a loud clanging noise (UCS) was made behind him, producing fear and crying (UCR). After several pairings Albert cried and withdrew at the sight of the rat alone (CR), and this fear generalized to other furry white objects such as a rabbit and a fur coat. The study showed that an emotional reaction, a phobia, could be classically conditioned.

Who → what. John Broadus Watson (with Rosalie Rayner) → behaviourism; the Little Albert study proving fear can be conditioned in humans.

John Garcia demonstrated a striking exception to the rule that any CS can be paired with any UCS. In his studies of taste aversion, rats that consumed a flavoured solution and later felt nauseated (even hours afterward) developed a strong avoidance of that taste after a single pairing. Crucially, the aversion attached readily to tastes but not to sights or sounds. This is now called the Garcia effect, and it illustrates biological preparedness: organisms are evolutionarily prepared to associate certain stimuli (taste with illness) far more easily than arbitrary ones, because such associations aided survival.

Who → what. John Garcia → conditioned taste aversion (the Garcia effect) and biological preparedness; learning with a long CS–UCS delay and in a single trial.
Why. Taste aversion breaks two "rules" of ordinary conditioning: it forms after one trial and across a delay of hours. This is adaptive; an animal that got sick after eating something must avoid that food to survive, so evolution biased the learning system toward taste–illness links.

9.4 Operant conditioning: Thorndike and Skinner

Operant conditioning (also called instrumental conditioning) is learning in which the consequences of a behaviour determine how likely that behaviour is to be repeated. Where classical conditioning concerns involuntary, reflexive responses elicited by stimuli, operant conditioning concerns voluntary behaviours emitted by the organism and shaped by their outcomes.

The groundwork was laid by Edward Lee Thorndike, who studied how cats escaped from a puzzle box he built. A hungry cat placed inside had to operate a latch to get out and reach food. Over successive trials the cat escaped faster, and Thorndike concluded that behaviours followed by satisfying consequences are "stamped in." He named this the law of effect: responses that produce a satisfying outcome become more likely, and responses that produce discomfort become less likely, in a given situation. He described the improvement as gradual trial-and-error learning, not sudden insight.

Who → what. Edward Lee Thorndike → the puzzle box, trial-and-error learning, and the law of effect (satisfying consequences strengthen a response).

Burrhus Frederic Skinner extended this into a full experimental system. He invented the operant chamber, universally known as the Skinner box, in which an animal such as a rat or pigeon presses a lever or pecks a key to receive food, with responses recorded automatically. Skinner replaced Thorndike's mentalistic word "satisfying" with the observable concept of reinforcement: any consequence that increases the frequency of the behaviour it follows.

Who → what. Burrhus Frederic Skinner → the operant chamber (Skinner box), the language of reinforcement and punishment, and schedules of reinforcement.

Reinforcement and punishment

Two dimensions define the four consequences. "Positive" means a stimulus is added; "negative" means a stimulus is removed. "Reinforcement" always increases behaviour; "punishment" always decreases it. Combining them gives four cases.

  • Positive reinforcement: adding a pleasant stimulus to increase behaviour. A rat gets a food pellet for pressing the lever, so it presses more.
  • Negative reinforcement: removing an unpleasant stimulus to increase behaviour. A rat presses the lever to switch off a loud noise, so it presses more.
  • Positive punishment: adding an unpleasant stimulus to decrease behaviour. A child touches a hot stove, feels pain, and stops touching it.
  • Negative punishment: removing a pleasant stimulus to decrease behaviour. A teenager loses phone privileges for coming home late, so lateness drops.
Trap. Negative reinforcement is NOT punishment. Both involve something unpleasant, but negative reinforcement increases a behaviour by removing the unpleasant thing, whereas punishment decreases behaviour. This is one of the most heavily tested confusions on the paper.

Reinforcers themselves come in two kinds. A primary reinforcer satisfies a biological need and is reinforcing on its own, such as food, water, or warmth. A secondary reinforcer (also called a conditioned reinforcer) gains its power by being associated with a primary reinforcer; money, praise, and tokens are secondary reinforcers because they can be exchanged for or have been paired with primary rewards.

Schedules of reinforcement

A schedule of reinforcement is the rule determining which responses get reinforced. Under continuous reinforcement every correct response is reinforced; this produces fast learning but also fast extinction once reinforcement stops. Under partial (intermittent) reinforcement only some responses are reinforced, which produces slower learning but far greater resistance to extinction. There are four classic partial schedules, defined by whether reinforcement depends on the number of responses (ratio) or the passage of time (interval), and on whether the requirement is fixed or variable.

ScheduleRuleResponse patternExample
Fixed-ratio (FR)Reinforce after a set number of responsesHigh rate; brief pause after each reinforcerPay per 10 items assembled
Variable-ratio (VR)Reinforce after an unpredictable number of responsesVery high, steady rate; most resistant to extinctionSlot machine, gambling
Fixed-interval (FI)Reinforce first response after a set timeScalloped; responding speeds up as the time nearsChecking mail near delivery time
Variable-interval (VI)Reinforce first response after an unpredictable timeSlow, steady, consistent rateChecking for a text that may arrive any time
Key. Variable-ratio schedules produce the highest response rate and the greatest resistance to extinction, which is exactly why gambling is so persistent. Ratio schedules generally yield higher rates than interval schedules.

Partial reinforcement also explains a robust finding: the partial-reinforcement extinction effect, which states that behaviours learned under partial reinforcement resist extinction longer than those learned under continuous reinforcement. When reinforcement has always been intermittent, its absence is harder to detect, so the behaviour persists.

Building complex behaviour

New behaviours are rarely produced whole, so they are built up. Shaping is the reinforcement of successive approximations, that is, steps that come progressively closer to the target behaviour; to teach a rat to press a lever, one first reinforces facing the lever, then approaching it, then touching it, then pressing. Chaining links a sequence of separate behaviours into one continuous performance, with each step serving as the cue for the next. A related tool is the Premack principle, named after David Premack, which states that a more probable (preferred) behaviour can reinforce a less probable one: "eat your vegetables and then you may have dessert" uses the preferred dessert to reinforce the less-preferred vegetables.

Who → what. David Premack → the Premack principle (a high-frequency, preferred activity can reinforce a low-frequency one).

Escape and avoidance

Two forms of negative-reinforcement learning are distinguished by timing. Escape learning is learning a response that terminates an aversive stimulus already present, such as opening an umbrella once the rain has started. Avoidance learning is learning a response that prevents an aversive stimulus from occurring at all, cued by a warning signal, such as taking an umbrella when clouds gather. Avoidance is important clinically because it maintains phobias: the person who flees before anxiety peaks never learns that the feared outcome would not have happened.

9.5 Observational learning: Albert Bandura

Observational learning (also called social learning or modeling) is learning by watching the behaviour of others, called models, and its consequences, without direct reinforcement of one's own behaviour. Its leading figure is Albert Bandura, whose Bobo doll experiment demonstrated it. Children watched an adult behave aggressively toward an inflatable "Bobo" doll, hitting and kicking it; when later left alone with the doll, these children imitated the aggression far more than children who had not seen the model, and they even copied novel actions they had never performed before.

Who → what. Albert Bandura → observational (social) learning, the Bobo doll experiment, and the concept of vicarious reinforcement.

Bandura identified four processes required for observational learning to occur. In order they are: attention (the observer must notice the model's behaviour), retention (the behaviour must be remembered, usually stored as a mental representation), reproduction (the observer must be physically able to reproduce the action), and motivation (the observer must have a reason to perform it). Motivation is often supplied by vicarious reinforcement, which is the increase in a behaviour after seeing a model rewarded for it (and the corresponding vicarious punishment when the model is penalized). In the Bobo studies, children who saw the aggressive model rewarded imitated more than those who saw the model punished.

Key. Bandura's four processes in order: Attention → Retention → Reproduction → Motivation. Learning can occur through observation alone, without the observer ever being directly reinforced.

9.6 Cognitive learning: Tolman, Kohler, Seligman

Strict behaviourism treated the mind as a "black box," but several findings forced the recognition that internal mental processes matter in learning. Edward Chace Tolman studied rats in mazes and found evidence of latent learning, learning that occurs without reinforcement and remains hidden until there is a reason to show it. Rats allowed to wander a maze with no reward later learned the rewarded route far faster than rats with no prior exposure, proving they had learned the layout all along. Tolman argued they had formed a cognitive map, an internal mental representation of the spatial environment.

Who → what. Edward Chace Tolman → latent learning and cognitive maps; evidence that learning can occur without reinforcement and be stored mentally.

Wolfgang Kohler, a founder of Gestalt psychology, studied problem solving in chimpanzees and described insight learning: the sudden grasp of a solution, experienced as an "aha" moment, rather than gradual trial and error. His chimpanzee Sultan, faced with a banana out of reach, suddenly fitted two sticks together to rake it in, or stacked boxes to climb to a hanging fruit, having apparently solved the problem mentally before acting. Insight contrasts sharply with Thorndike's trial-and-error curve.

Who → what. Wolfgang Kohler → insight learning; the chimpanzee Sultan solving problems suddenly by mental restructuring, not trial and error.

Martin Seligman described learned helplessness, a state in which an organism exposed to unavoidable, uncontrollable aversive events stops trying to escape even when escape later becomes possible. Dogs given inescapable shocks failed to jump a low barrier to safety in a later situation where escape was easy, having "learned" that their actions did not matter. Seligman linked this to human depression, seeing a parallel between the passive giving-up of the animals and the hopelessness of a depressed person.

Who → what. Martin Seligman → learned helplessness (giving up after uncontrollable aversive events) and its link to depression.

9.7 Classical versus operant conditioning

Because the two forms of conditioning are constantly contrasted on the exam, the differences are worth setting side by side.

FeatureClassical conditioningOperant conditioning
Chief figuresPavlov, WatsonThorndike, Skinner
Type of behaviourInvoluntary, reflexive, elicitedVoluntary, emitted
Basis of learningAssociation of two stimuli (CS with UCS)Association of behaviour with its consequence
Role of the organismPassive; response is drawn outActive; organism operates on the environment
Order of eventsStimulus comes before the responseConsequence comes after the response
ExampleSalivating to a bellPressing a lever for food
Trap. Classical conditioning shapes involuntary responses (salivation, fear, reflexes); operant conditioning shapes voluntary behaviour (lever-pressing, studying). If a question describes an automatic bodily reaction, think classical; if it describes a chosen action followed by a reward or penalty, think operant.

9.8 Applications

The principles above underlie a family of practical techniques. Behaviour modification is the systematic use of operant principles (reinforcement, extinction, shaping) to change maladaptive behaviour and build adaptive behaviour, widely used with children, in classrooms, and in institutions. A prominent example is the token economy, in which desired behaviours earn tokens (secondary reinforcers) that can later be exchanged for privileges or goods; token economies have been used in psychiatric wards and special-education settings to encourage self-care and cooperation.

Biofeedback applies conditioning to normally involuntary bodily processes. Instruments give the person moment-to-moment information about a physiological function such as muscle tension, heart rate, or skin temperature, and by using that feedback as a reinforcing signal the person gradually learns some voluntary control, which helps with tension headaches, hypertension, and anxiety.

Key. A token economy is an application of operant conditioning: tokens are secondary (conditioned) reinforcers. Biofeedback teaches control over involuntary functions by turning a bodily signal into feedback the person can respond to.
Recap. Learning is a relatively permanent change in behaviour due to experience, distinct from maturation and reflex. Classical conditioning (Pavlov; UCS, UCR, CS, CR; acquisition, extinction, spontaneous recovery, generalization, discrimination, higher-order conditioning; Watson's Little Albert; Garcia's prepared taste aversion) links stimuli and governs involuntary responses. Operant conditioning (Thorndike's law of effect; Skinner's chamber; the four consequences; primary and secondary reinforcers; the four reinforcement schedules with variable-ratio strongest; shaping, chaining, Premack; escape versus avoidance) links behaviour to its consequences and governs voluntary action. Bandura's observational learning adds modeling, four processes, and vicarious reinforcement; Tolman (latent learning, cognitive maps), Kohler (insight, Sultan), and Seligman (learned helplessness) add the cognitive dimension. Applications include behaviour modification, token economies, and biofeedback. Remember the two headline traps: negative reinforcement is not punishment, and classical is involuntary while operant is voluntary.

9.9 The complete map: every theory of learning and its theorist

This is the safety-net roster: the full set of learning theories an aptitude paper can name, each pinned to its theorist and a one-line core idea. If a name shows up in a question stem, it should be recognisable from this table. Read it as three families: behavioural and associative, cognitive, and social and applied.

Key. Match the theorist to the mechanism, not just the label. Pavlov, Thorndike, Skinner and Guthrie all study association, but the unit differs: reflex pairing, stimulus and response bond, response and consequence, and stimulus and movement contiguity. Aptitude items reward that precision.

Family A: Behavioural and associative theories

#Theory / principleTheorist(s)Core idea in one line
1Classical conditioningIvan PavlovA neutral stimulus paired with an unconditioned stimulus comes to evoke a conditioned response.
2Law of effect, connectionism, trial-and-errorEdward ThorndikeResponses followed by satisfaction are stamped in; learning is the strengthening of stimulus and response bonds.
3Operant conditioning and reinforcement schedulesBurrhus Frederic SkinnerBehaviour is shaped by its consequences; schedules of reinforcement control response rate and persistence.
4Contiguity and one-trial learningEdwin GuthrieA stimulus and a movement occurring together become linked in full on a single pairing; no reinforcement needed.
5Drive-reduction and hypothetico-deductive learningClark HullLearning occurs when a response reduces a drive; behaviour is derived from formal postulates and habit strength.
6Two-process avoidance learningOrval Hobart MowrerFear is learned by classical conditioning, then avoidance is maintained by operant fear-reduction.
7Preparedness and biological constraintsMartin SeligmanOrganisms are biologically prepared to learn some associations (fears, aversions) far more readily than others.
8Taste aversion learningJohn GarciaA single pairing of taste with later nausea produces strong aversion despite a long delay (the Garcia effect).
9Rescorla-Wagner model of prediction errorRobert Rescorla & Allan WagnerConditioning depends on how surprising the outcome is; learning tracks the discrepancy between expected and actual.
10Premack principleDavid PremackA more probable behaviour can reinforce a less probable one (access to the preferred activity rewards the other).
11Verbal learning and the forgetting curveHermann EbbinghausNonsense-syllable studies mapped how retention decays over time and how relearning saves effort.
12Hebbian (neural) learningDonald HebbCells that fire together wire together; repeated co-activation strengthens the synaptic connection.

Family B: Cognitive theories

#Theory / principleTheorist(s)Core idea in one line
13Purposive behaviourism, cognitive maps, latent learningEdward TolmanLearning is goal-directed and can stay hidden; organisms build internal cognitive maps shown only when useful.
14Insight learning and sign-gestaltWolfgang KohlerProblems are solved by sudden reorganisation of the perceptual field, not gradual trial-and-error.
15Discovery learningJerome BrunerLearners construct knowledge best by exploring and discovering principles for themselves.
16Meaningful reception learning and advance organizersDavid AusubelNew material is learned by anchoring it to existing ideas; advance organizers bridge what is known to what is new.
17Cognitive constructivismJean PiagetChildren actively build knowledge through assimilation and accommodation across developmental stages.
18Social constructivismLev VygotskyLearning is socially mediated; guidance within the zone of proximal development lifts the learner beyond solo ability.
19Conditions of learningRobert GagneDifferent learning outcomes need different internal and external conditions, sequenced by nine instructional events.

Family C: Social and applied theories

#Theory / principleTheorist(s)Core idea in one line
20Social learning, observational learning, Bobo dollAlbert BanduraPeople learn by watching models; behaviour is acquired through attention, retention, reproduction and motivation.
21Experiential learning cycleDavid KolbLearning cycles through concrete experience, reflection, abstract conceptualisation and active experimentation.
22Mastery learningBenjamin BloomGiven enough time and feedback, almost all learners can master material before moving to the next unit.
23Programmed instructionBurrhus Frederic SkinnerContent is broken into small steps with immediate feedback, letting each learner progress at their own pace.
24Errorless learningHerbert TerraceDiscriminations are taught so the learner rarely responds wrongly, reducing the frustration of errors.
Trap. Watch the near-neighbours. Latent learning is Tolman, insight is Kohler; do not swap them. Discovery learning is Bruner while meaningful reception learning is Ausubel, and they are near opposites in method. Piaget is cognitive (individual) constructivism whereas Vygotsky is social constructivism. Programmed instruction and errorless learning are both applied, but the former is Skinner and the latter is Terrace.
Recap. Behavioural theories explain learning through association and consequence (Pavlov, Thorndike, Skinner, Guthrie, Hull, Mowrer, plus the biological and formal refinements of Seligman, Garcia, Rescorla and Wagner, Premack, Ebbinghaus and Hebb). Cognitive theories add internal representation and meaning (Tolman, Kohler, Bruner, Ausubel, Piaget, Vygotsky, Gagne). Social and applied theories put it to work in the classroom and clinic (Bandura, Kolb, Bloom, Skinner's programmed instruction, Terrace's errorless learning). Know the theorist, the unit of learning, and the one-line mechanism for each.

10 Memory, Attention & Cognition

Every waking moment floods the mind with more information than it can possibly process, so the first task of the cognitive system is to decide what to let in. From that filtered stream, some material is held for a few seconds, some is rehearsed and stored for a lifetime, and much is simply lost. On top of these memory operations sit the higher activities of thinking: forming concepts, solving problems, reasoning, judging, deciding, and using language. This chapter builds the whole picture from the ground up, defining each term as it appears and naming each theorist in full. The organising theme is that the human being is an information processor: information enters through the senses, is transformed (encoded), kept (stored), and later brought back (retrieved), and every stage can succeed or fail.

10.1 Attention: the gateway to processing

Attention is the process of selectively concentrating on one aspect of the environment while ignoring others. Because processing capacity is limited, attention acts as a gate that admits some inputs and blocks the rest. The classic demonstration is the cocktail-party effect: at a noisy party you can follow one conversation and screen out the others, yet if someone across the room says your name, your attention snaps to it. This shows that unattended information is not fully discarded; at least some of it is monitored for personal relevance.

Selective attention and filter (bottleneck) models

Selective attention means focusing on one source of information among many. Researchers described this with bottleneck models, so called because information must squeeze through a narrow channel where only one message passes at a time. They disagreed about where along the processing stream the bottleneck sits.

Who → what. Donald Broadbent proposed the first filter model (early selection, 1958). Incoming messages are held briefly in a sensory buffer, then a filter selects one message on the basis of its physical features (which ear, pitch, loudness) and passes only that one on for meaning to be extracted. The unattended channel is blocked before its meaning is analysed.
Trap. Broadbent's filter is early selection: it screens by physical properties before meaning. The cocktail-party name-detection finding is a problem for a strict early filter, because if the other channel were fully blocked you could never notice your own name in it.
Who → what. Anne Treisman answered that criticism with the attenuation model (1964). The unattended channel is not blocked outright but attenuated (turned down, like lowering a volume dial). Weakened signals still reach meaning analysis, and words with a low "threshold" for activation, such as your own name or a highly expected word, can still get through even when turned down.
Who → what. J. Anthony Deutsch and Diana Deutsch (1963), later developed by Donald Norman, proposed late-selection theory. Here all incoming messages are processed for meaning, and the bottleneck comes only at the end, at the point of response or conscious awareness, where the most relevant item is selected. Selection is late, after semantic analysis, not before it.
ModelTheoristWhere selection happens
Filter (early)Donald BroadbentBefore meaning; blocks by physical features
AttenuationAnne TreismanUnattended channel turned down, not blocked
Late selectionDeutsch, Deutsch & NormanAfter meaning; at response/awareness

Divided attention; automatic vs controlled processing

Divided attention is the attempt to attend to two or more things at once, as in driving while talking. Whether this succeeds depends on how much mental effort each task needs. Controlled processing is slow, effortful, conscious, and demands attention (a learner driver managing the clutch). Automatic processing is fast, effortless, and runs with little conscious control (an experienced driver changing gear without thinking). Practice converts controlled processing into automatic processing, which frees capacity for other tasks. The Stroop effect, in which naming the ink colour of a colour word (the word "red" printed in blue ink) is slowed because reading is automatic, shows how hard automatic processes are to switch off.

Why. Automaticity is the reason skilled activity feels easy: by offloading routine steps to automatic processing, the limited pool of controlled attention is spared for novelty and problem solving.

10.2 Memory as information processing: the multi-store model

The dominant framework treats memory as a flow through stores. Encoding is converting information into a form the system can hold; storage is keeping it over time; retrieval is getting it back out. A failure at any of these three produces what we loosely call "forgetting".

Who → what. Richard Atkinson and Richard Shiffrin proposed the multi-store model (also called the modal model, 1968): information passes through a sensory register, then a short-term store, then, if rehearsed, a long-term store.

The sensory register

The sensory register (sensory memory) holds an exact but very brief copy of raw sensory input. Iconic memory is the visual sensory store, lasting about a quarter to half a second. Echoic memory is the auditory sensory store, lasting a few seconds, which is what lets you "replay" the end of a sentence you barely heard.

Who → what. George Sperling (1960) demonstrated iconic memory with the partial-report technique. Shown a grid of letters for a fraction of a second, people could report only about four in a "whole report", but if a tone cued them which row to report immediately after the display vanished, they could report almost any row. This proved that nearly all the letters were briefly available in a rapidly fading icon.

Short-term store and working capacity

The short-term store (short-term memory, STM) holds a small amount of information for roughly 15 to 30 seconds unless it is refreshed by rehearsal (mental repetition). Information is lost through decay or displacement by new items.

Who → what. George Miller (1956), in "The Magical Number Seven, Plus or Minus Two", argued that short-term capacity is about seven items (range five to nine). The limit is not the amount of raw data but the number of chunks. Chunking is grouping items into meaningful units, so that "1 9 4 7 1 9 9 1" (eight digits) becomes two dates (two chunks), vastly expanding what fits.

Long-term store

The long-term store (long-term memory, LTM) has effectively unlimited capacity and can hold information for years or a lifetime. In the multi-store model, material moves from STM to LTM through elaborative rehearsal (linking it to existing knowledge), not mere repetition.

StoreCapacityDurationCoding
Sensory registerVery large (all input)Iconic ~0.5 s; echoic ~2–4 sRaw sensory (visual, auditory)
Short-term store7 ± 2 chunks~15–30 s without rehearsalMainly acoustic (sound-based)
Long-term storeEffectively unlimitedMinutes to a lifetimeMainly semantic (meaning-based)
Key. The three stores differ on three dimensions: capacity, duration, and coding. STM codes mostly by sound (people confuse "B" and "V", which sound alike), LTM codes mostly by meaning (people confuse "big" and "large", which mean alike).

10.3 Working memory: an active workbench

Who → what. Alan Baddeley and Graham Hitch (1974) replaced the single passive short-term store with a working memory model, an active system that both holds and manipulates information. It has four parts.
  • Central executive: the attentional controller that directs the other components, allocates effort, and switches between tasks. It has limited capacity and no storage of its own.
  • Phonological loop: handles sound-based and verbal information. It has a store (the "inner ear") and an articulatory rehearsal process (the "inner voice") that keeps words alive by silent repetition.
  • Visuospatial sketchpad: the "inner eye", which holds and manipulates visual and spatial information, such as picturing a route or rotating a shape.
  • Episodic buffer: added by Baddeley in 2000, a limited store that integrates information from the loop, the sketchpad, and long-term memory into a single coherent episode.
Why. Working memory explains why you can do two tasks at once only if they use different components: repeating digits (phonological loop) while tracking a moving dot (visuospatial sketchpad) is easy, but two verbal tasks compete and interfere.

10.4 Levels of processing and the serial position effect

Who → what. Fergus Craik and Robert Lockhart (1972) offered the levels-of-processing account, arguing that memory strength depends not on which store an item sits in but on how deeply it is processed. Shallow processing attends to surface features (is the word in capitals?); deep (semantic) processing attends to meaning (does the word fit this sentence?). Deeper, more meaningful processing yields stronger, longer-lasting memories.

The serial position effect describes the finding that, when people recall a list, memory is best for items at the start and end and worst for the middle. The primacy effect is superior recall of early items, thought to reflect extra rehearsal that moved them into long-term memory. The recency effect is superior recall of the last items, thought to reflect their still being in short-term memory at the moment of recall.

Trap. Do not swap them. Primacy = first items (long-term memory); recency = recent, i.e. last items (short-term memory). A filled delay before recall wipes out the recency effect but leaves primacy intact, strong evidence for two separate stores.

10.5 Types of long-term memory

Long-term memory is not one thing. The broadest split is between explicit (declarative) memory, which is conscious and can be "declared" or stated, and implicit (non-declarative) memory, which shows up in behaviour without conscious recollection.

Who → what. Endel Tulving (1972) divided explicit memory into episodic memory, the record of personally experienced events tied to a time and place (your last birthday), and semantic memory, general knowledge of facts and meanings with no personal time-stamp (the capital of France).
  • Episodic memory: events, autobiographical, "remembering" a specific occasion.
  • Semantic memory: facts, concepts, vocabulary, "knowing" without reliving.
  • Procedural memory: implicit memory for skills and how to do things (cycling, typing), expressed through performance.
  • Priming: implicit facilitation in which recent exposure to a stimulus speeds later processing of it or a related one, without any conscious memory of the exposure.
Trap. Episodic vs semantic: both are explicit, but episodic is where/when it happened to me and semantic is context-free knowledge. "I remember eating mango yesterday" is episodic; "mangoes are fruit" is semantic.

10.6 Retrieval, context, and vivid memories

Who → what. Endel Tulving and Donald Thomson stated the encoding specificity principle: retrieval is best when the cues present at recall match those present at encoding. Memory is not a pure copy; it depends on the fit between learning and testing conditions.

Two consequences follow. Context-dependent memory means recall improves when the external environment at retrieval matches that at encoding; divers who learned words underwater recalled them better underwater. State-dependent memory means recall improves when the internal state (mood, arousal, or the effect of a substance) matches. A flashbulb memory is a vivid, detailed, and confidently held memory of the moment one learned of a shocking event; such memories feel photographic but research shows they can be inaccurate despite the confidence.

10.7 Schemas and reconstructive memory

Who → what. Frederic Bartlett (1932) argued that remembering is reconstructive, not reproductive: we rebuild the past using schemas, organised packets of prior knowledge and expectations. In his "War of the Ghosts" study, British participants read an unfamiliar Native American folk tale and, on repeated recall, unconsciously distorted it to fit their own cultural schemas, dropping strange details and rationalising odd events.
Key. Memory is an active reconstruction shaped by schemas, not a video replay. This makes memory efficient and meaningful but also systematically prone to distortion.

Eyewitness memory and the misinformation effect

Who → what. Elizabeth Loftus demonstrated the misinformation effect, in which information encountered after an event alters the memory of the event itself. In the classic study, participants who saw a car accident and were asked how fast the cars were going when they "smashed" gave higher speed estimates, and a week later were more likely to falsely report broken glass, than those asked about cars that "hit". Leading questions reshape memory, which is why eyewitness testimony can be confidently wrong.

10.8 Forgetting and amnesia

Who → what. Hermann Ebbinghaus (1885), studying his own memory for nonsense syllables, plotted the forgetting curve: forgetting is rapid at first and then levels off. He measured memory with the savings method, comparing how much less effort it took to relearn material than to learn it originally; the "saving" reveals memory that recognition alone would miss.

Several theories explain why forgetting occurs.

  • Decay theory: memory traces fade with the passage of time if not used.
  • Interference theory: other memories compete and block the target. Proactive interference is when old learning disrupts new (your old phone number intrudes on the new one). Retroactive interference is when new learning disrupts old (a new password makes the old one hard to recall).
  • Retrieval failure / cue-dependent forgetting: the memory is stored but the right cue is missing, as in the "tip of the tongue" state; the word returns once a cue appears.
  • Motivated forgetting: in the Freudian tradition, threatening or painful memories are pushed out of awareness (repression).
Trap. Proactive vs retroactive: think of the direction in time. Proactive acts forward, old harming new. Retroactive acts backward, new harming old.

Amnesia

Amnesia is memory loss from brain injury or disease. Anterograde amnesia is the inability to form new long-term memories after the injury; the past is intact but new events do not stick. Retrograde amnesia is the loss of memories formed before the injury.

Who → what. The patient H.M. (Henry Molaison), whose hippocampus was surgically removed to treat epilepsy, developed profound anterograde amnesia: he could not form new explicit memories, yet he could learn new motor skills (procedural memory) while having no memory of practising them. His case proved that explicit and implicit memory rely on different brain systems and that the hippocampus is critical for forming new declarative memories.

10.9 Cognition: concepts, imagery, and problem solving

Cognition refers to all mental activities involved in acquiring, storing, and using knowledge. Its building blocks are concepts, mental categories that group objects or ideas by shared features (the concept "bird"). Many concepts are organised around a prototype, the most typical or representative example; a robin is a better prototype of "bird" than a penguin, so we judge category membership faster for typical members.

Mental imagery is the representation of information in a picture-like form, allowing operations such as mentally rotating an object; the time to rotate an image increases with the angle of rotation, suggesting imagery preserves spatial structure.

Problem solving

A problem exists when there is a gap between a current state and a goal state with no obvious route between them. Strategies vary in reliability.

  • Algorithm: a step-by-step procedure that guarantees a correct solution if followed (working through every possible move), but can be slow.
  • Heuristic: a mental shortcut or rule of thumb that is fast and usually works but does not guarantee success.
  • Insight: a sudden realisation of a solution, the "aha" experience, in which the answer appears whole rather than through gradual steps.

Two obstacles frequently block solutions. Functional fixedness is the tendency to see an object as usable only in its customary way (failing to see a coin could serve as a screwdriver). Mental set is the tendency to persist with a strategy that worked before even when a simpler one is now available.

10.10 Reasoning, judgment, and decision making

Reasoning is drawing conclusions from information. Deductive reasoning moves from general premises to a specific, logically certain conclusion (all men are mortal; Socrates is a man; therefore Socrates is mortal). Inductive reasoning moves from specific observations to a probable general conclusion (every crow I have seen is black, so crows are probably black); its conclusions are likely but never guaranteed.

Who → what. Amos Tversky and Daniel Kahneman showed that everyday judgment relies on heuristics that, while efficient, produce systematic errors (biases).
  • Availability heuristic: judging how likely or frequent something is by how easily examples come to mind. Vivid, recent events (plane crashes) seem more common than they are.
  • Representativeness heuristic: judging probability by how well something matches a typical example (a prototype), while ignoring base rates. A quiet man who likes poetry is judged more likely to be a librarian than a farmer, even though farmers vastly outnumber librarians.
  • Anchoring and adjustment: starting from an initial value (the anchor) and adjusting insufficiently, so the final estimate stays biased toward the anchor.

The framing effect shows that decisions shift with how options are worded even when the facts are identical: a treatment described as having a "90% survival rate" is chosen more often than the same treatment described as having a "10% mortality rate".

Why. These heuristics are not signs of stupidity; they are efficient adaptations to a world of limited time and information. They mislead only in specific, predictable situations, which is exactly what makes the biases scientifically interesting.

10.11 Language

Language is a system of symbols combined by rules to communicate meaning. It is studied at several levels.

  • Phonemes: the smallest units of sound that distinguish meaning (the /b/ and /p/ that separate "bat" from "pat").
  • Morphemes: the smallest units of meaning (the word "cats" has two: "cat" and the plural "-s").
  • Syntax: the rules of grammar governing how words combine into sentences.
  • Semantics: the rules that assign meaning to words and sentences.
Who → what. Noam Chomsky argued that children acquire language too fast and from too little input to be explained by imitation alone, so the brain must contain an innate language acquisition device (LAD). He proposed a universal grammar, a set of grammatical principles common to all human languages, which the child applies to whatever specific language surrounds them.
Who → what. Benjamin Lee Whorf, with Edward Sapir, proposed linguistic relativity (the Sapir-Whorf hypothesis): the language one speaks shapes how one thinks and perceives the world, for example by the categories its vocabulary provides. The strong "language determines thought" version is now rejected, but a weaker "language influences thought" version has empirical support.

Children pass through broadly universal stages of language acquisition: cooing (vowel-like sounds, from about two months), babbling (repeated consonant-vowel sounds such as "ba-ba", from about six months), the one-word (holophrastic) stage (single words standing for whole ideas, around one year), the two-word (telegraphic) stage (two-word combinations stripped of grammatical extras, "want milk", around two years), and then rapid expansion into full grammatical sentences.

Recap. Attention gates a flood of input; filter theories differ on whether selection is early (Donald Broadbent), attenuated (Anne Treisman), or late (Deutsch and Norman), and practice turns controlled processing automatic. Memory is information processing across stores (Richard Atkinson and Richard Shiffrin): a fleeting sensory register (iconic, George Sperling; echoic), a limited short-term store of seven plus or minus two chunks (George Miller), and an unlimited long-term store, with the stores differing in capacity, duration, and coding. Working memory (Alan Baddeley and Graham Hitch) makes short-term memory an active workbench of central executive, phonological loop, visuospatial sketchpad, and episodic buffer, while depth of processing (Fergus Craik and Robert Lockhart) and the serial position effect (primacy = first, recency = last) shape what endures. Long-term memory splits into explicit episodic and semantic (Endel Tulving) and implicit procedural and priming; retrieval follows encoding specificity, and memory is reconstructive and schema-driven (Frederic Bartlett), hence the misinformation effect (Elizabeth Loftus). Forgetting follows the Ebbinghaus curve through decay, proactive and retroactive interference, cue-dependent failure, and motivated forgetting, with amnesia (patient H.M.) dissociating explicit from implicit memory. Higher cognition works through concepts and prototypes, imagery, algorithms, heuristics, insight, and reasoning, with the judgment heuristics of Amos Tversky and Daniel Kahneman (availability, representativeness, anchoring) and the framing effect, and language builds from phonemes and morphemes through syntax and semantics, guided by Noam Chomsky's language acquisition device and universal grammar and questioned by Benjamin Lee Whorf's linguistic relativity.

10.12 The complete map: models of memory, attention & cognition and their authors

This is the safety-net roster for the cognitive block: every named model, effect, or theory of memory, attention, language, and thought that an aptitude paper could pin to an author. Learn the pairing (name plus person) and the one-line idea; that is usually all a single mark asks. Rows are numbered continuously across families.

Key. A model is a proposed structure or process; an effect is an observed regularity in the data. In an exam, the safest anchor is the author's surname plus one distinguishing word (store, loop, level, schema, filter, chunk). Full names are given first, then the tag you will actually recall.

Family A: Memory stores & models

#Model / effectAuthor(s)Core idea in one line

2Working memory modelAlan Baddeley & Graham HitchShort-term store is an active workspace: central executive plus phonological loop, visuospatial sketchpad (later episodic buffer).

Broadbent is early selection, Deutsch-Deutsch and Norman are late selection, Treisman sits in between. Godden and Baddeley give context-dependent (external place), while Bower gives state-dependent (internal mood); the diver study is context, not state. Miller's seven is short-term capacity, not the forgetting curve (that is Ebbinghaus).
Recap.
