Forgetting: Ebbinghaus (curve), Brown-Peterson (decay), McGeoch (interference), Loftus (misinformation), Brown-Kulik (flashbulb), Murdock (serial position). Attention: Broadbent (early filter), Treisman (attenuation, feature integration), Deutsch-Deutsch/Norman (late), Kahneman (capacity), Cherry (cocktail party), Stroop, Posner (networks). Thought and language: Newell-Simon (problem space), Anderson (ACT), Collins-Quillian/Loftus (semantic network), Neisser (field), Tversky-Kahneman (heuristics), Chomsky (grammar), Sapir-Whorf (relativity).

11 Motivation & Emotion

Two questions run underneath almost everything a psychologist studies. The first is why an organism does anything at all: why it eats now rather than later, why one student grinds for a first-class degree while another drifts, why a frightened animal freezes and another one flees. That is the study of motivation. The second is what it feels like from the inside and how the body registers it: the pounding heart before an exam, the flush of anger, the warmth of relief. That is the study of emotion. The two are tightly braided (a strong feeling pushes us to act, and acting changes what we feel), so psychology treats them together. This chapter builds both from the ground up: first the vocabulary and the major theories of motivation, then the biological motives, then emotion and its competing theories, and finally stress, where motivation and emotion meet the body most sharply.

11.1 The vocabulary of motivation

Motivation is the internal process that initiates, directs, and sustains behaviour toward a goal. It explains not just that behaviour happens but its energy (how vigorous), its direction (toward what), and its persistence (for how long). Because motivation cannot be seen directly, it is an intervening variable: we infer it from what an organism does. Four closely related terms must be kept apart.

  • Need. A state of deprivation or requirement, either biological (food, water, warmth) or psychological (achievement, belonging). A need is the underlying lack.
  • Drive. The aroused, tense internal state that a need produces and that pushes the organism to act. Hunger the need produces the hunger drive; the drive is the felt push. Example: not having eaten for a day is a need; the gnawing restlessness that makes you seek food is the drive.
  • Motive. A more general term for any specific internal reason for behaving, often used for learned or social motivations (a motive to achieve, to affiliate, to dominate).
  • Incentive. An external goal object that pulls behaviour. Where a drive pushes from inside, an incentive pulls from outside. Example: the smell of fresh bread can make you hungry even when you have just eaten; the bread is an incentive.
Key. Drives push from within (internal states of tension); incentives pull from without (external goal objects). Most real behaviour is a mix of both.

11.2 Instinct theory: William McDougall

The earliest scientific account of motivation was instinct theory. An instinct is a complex, unlearned, fixed pattern of behaviour that is characteristic of an entire species (birds building nests, salmon swimming upstream). The psychologist William McDougall argued that human behaviour too is driven by a set of inborn instincts (he listed motives such as curiosity, pugnacity, and parental care), and that these instincts supply the energy behind all conduct.

Who → what. William McDougall proposed the first influential instinct theory of human motivation, holding that inborn, species-wide instincts energize behaviour.

Instinct theory faded because it explained too much and predicted too little. Psychologists kept adding instincts (one survey counted thousands proposed by various writers) until naming an instinct became merely relabelling the behaviour it was supposed to explain. Saying a person fights because of a fighting instinct is circular. Instinct theory does survive, in refined form, in ethology (the biological study of animal behaviour) and in evolutionary psychology, which treats many motives as adaptations shaped by natural selection.

Trap. Instinct theory was not disproved by evidence so much as abandoned for being circular and unfalsifiable. Do not say it was refuted by an experiment.

11.3 Drive-reduction theory: Clark Hull and homeostasis

Drive-reduction theory, associated most with Clark Leonard Hull, rests on the idea of homeostasis: the body's tendency to maintain a stable, balanced internal state (constant temperature, blood sugar, fluid levels). When the body strays from its balance point (a physiological need arises), a drive is created; behaviour that reduces the need and restores balance reduces the drive, and that reduction is reinforcing. So we act to return to equilibrium, the way a thermostat switches the heating off once the set temperature is reached.

Who → what. Clark L. Hull built drive-reduction theory: needs create drives, behaviour that reduces the drive restores homeostasis and is reinforced.

Hull distinguished two kinds of drive:

  • Primary drives. Unlearned, tied directly to biological survival (hunger, thirst, warmth, sleep, sex).
  • Secondary drives. Learned through association with the satisfaction of primary drives. Example: money satisfies no biological need directly, but because it buys food and shelter it becomes a powerful learned drive.
Trap. Drive reduction cannot explain behaviour that raises tension: riding roller coasters, solving puzzles, exploring for its own sake. We do not always seek to reduce arousal; sometimes we seek to increase it. That gap is exactly what arousal theory fills.

11.4 Arousal theory and the Yerkes-Dodson law

Arousal theory holds that organisms are motivated to maintain an optimal level of arousal (alertness and activation), not a minimum. When arousal is too low we seek stimulation (we get bored and go looking for something to do); when it is too high we seek calm. Curiosity and play, which drive-reduction theory struggled with, fall out naturally: they raise arousal toward the optimum.

The classic principle here is the Yerkes-Dodson law (Robert Yerkes and John Dillingham Dodson): performance improves with arousal up to a point and then declines, producing an inverted-U curve. Some arousal helps; too much hurts. The law adds a refinement: the optimal level depends on task difficulty. For easy or well-learned tasks a higher level of arousal is best; for hard or complex tasks a lower level is best, because high arousal disrupts fine control.

Who → what. Robert M. Yerkes and John D. Dodson gave the Yerkes-Dodson law: an inverted-U between arousal and performance, with the optimum lower for difficult tasks.
Key. A little pre-exam nerves sharpens performance; being paralysed with panic ruins it. Same person, same exam, different points on the same inverted-U.

11.5 Incentive theory and expectancy-value

Where drive theory looks inward, incentive theory looks outward: behaviour is pulled by the anticipated value of external rewards and the wish to avoid punishments. The strength of the pull depends on how attractive the incentive is and how likely it seems that acting will secure it. Expectancy-value theory formalizes this: the motivation to pursue a goal is roughly the product of expectancy (how likely am I to succeed?) and value (how much do I want it?). If either is near zero, motivation is near zero: a huge prize you are certain you cannot win does not motivate, and neither does a trivial prize you are certain you can win.

11.6 Maslow's hierarchy of needs

Abraham Harold Maslow, a founder of humanistic psychology, arranged human needs into a hierarchy, usually drawn as a pyramid. Lower needs are said to be more basic and to demand satisfaction first; only as they are reasonably met does the person become concerned with the needs above. From the bottom up the five classic levels are:

  1. Physiological needs: food, water, air, sleep, warmth. The survival floor.
  2. Safety needs: security, shelter, stability, freedom from threat.
  3. Belongingness and love needs: friendship, intimacy, acceptance, being part of a group.
  4. Esteem needs: achievement, competence, respect from others and self-respect.
  5. Self-actualization: realizing one's full potential, becoming all one is capable of becoming.

Maslow later added, above esteem, cognitive needs (to know and understand), aesthetic needs (for order and beauty), and, at the very top, self-transcendence (connection to something beyond the self). He drew a crucial distinction between the lower four, which he called deficiency needs (D-needs, arising from lack, and quieted once filled), and growth needs or being-needs (B-needs, such as self-actualization, which are never fully satisfied and grow stronger the more they are engaged).

Who → what. Abraham Maslow proposed the hierarchy of needs and the concept of self-actualization, and split deficiency needs from growth needs.
Trap. The strict "lower before higher" ordering is an idealization, not an iron law. People starve for a cause, artists neglect comfort for their work, and belonging can override safety. Exam answers should present the levels as Maslow ordered them but note the hierarchy is not rigidly fixed.

11.7 Murray's manifest needs and the Thematic Apperception Test

Henry Alexander Murray catalogued a long list of psychological (rather than biological) needs, which he called manifest needs, among them the need for achievement, affiliation, dominance, order, and autonomy. He argued these needs vary in strength from person to person and are often not consciously acknowledged. To measure them indirectly he co-developed (with Christiana Morgan) the Thematic Apperception Test (TAT), a projective test in which a person makes up stories about ambiguous pictures; the themes that recur in the stories are read as clues to the person's dominant needs.

Who → what. Henry Murray catalogued manifest needs and co-created the Thematic Apperception Test (TAT) to measure them by the stories people project onto ambiguous pictures.

11.8 McClelland: achievement, affiliation, and power

David Clarence McClelland took three of Murray's needs and studied them intensively, often measuring them from TAT-style stories. His theory is sometimes called the acquired-needs or three-needs theory, because he held these motives are largely learned through culture and experience.

  • Need for achievement (nAch): the drive to meet standards of excellence, to master tasks, to do things well. High-nAch people prefer moderately difficult goals (challenging but attainable) and want clear feedback on results.
  • Need for affiliation (nAff): the drive for warm, friendly relationships and social acceptance.
  • Need for power (nPow): the drive to influence, control, or have impact on others.
Who → what. David McClelland developed the study of the need for achievement, affiliation, and power as acquired social motives.
Trap. Do not confuse the need for achievement (a drive to do a task well against a standard of excellence) with the need for power (a drive to influence or control other people). One is about personal mastery; the other is about impact on others. A common multiple-choice trap swaps them.

11.9 Self-determination theory: intrinsic vs extrinsic motivation

Self-determination theory (SDT), developed by Edward Deci and Richard Ryan, distinguishes two broad sources of motivation. Intrinsic motivation is doing something for its own sake, because it is interesting or satisfying in itself (reading for pleasure, playing music for the joy of it). Extrinsic motivation is doing something for a separable outcome (a reward, a grade, avoiding punishment). SDT holds that intrinsic motivation flourishes when three basic psychological needs are met: autonomy (a sense of choice), competence (a sense of effectiveness), and relatedness (a sense of connection).

A striking prediction of SDT is the overjustification effect: paying people to do something they already enjoy can reduce their intrinsic interest, because they come to see the activity as done for the reward rather than for itself. When the reward stops, the behaviour drops off more than it would have if no reward had ever been given.

Who → what. Edward Deci and Richard Ryan built self-determination theory, distinguishing intrinsic from extrinsic motivation and naming the overjustification effect.
Why. The overjustification effect matters practically: rewarding children for reading, or bonuses for already-loved work, can quietly kill the very interest they were meant to boost. Extrinsic rewards work best where intrinsic interest is low to begin with.

11.10 Biological motives

Hunger

Hunger is regulated in large part by the hypothalamus, a small brain structure just below the thalamus. Two regions were classically identified as opposing centres. The lateral hypothalamus (LH) acts as a hunger centre: stimulating it makes an animal eat, damaging it makes the animal stop eating. The ventromedial hypothalamus (VMH) acts as a satiety centre: stimulating it stops eating, damaging it leads to overeating and obesity. A simple mnemonic: Lateral = Let's eat; VentroMedial = Very Much full.

Chemical signals also regulate appetite over the longer term. Leptin is a hormone released by fat cells; higher fat stores mean more leptin, which signals fullness and suppresses appetite. Ghrelin is a hormone released mainly by the stomach when it is empty; it stimulates hunger. Roughly, leptin says "stop" and ghrelin says "go."

Key. Lateral hypothalamus = ON switch for eating (hunger centre). Ventromedial hypothalamus = OFF switch (satiety centre). Leptin (from fat) suppresses hunger; ghrelin (from the stomach) triggers it.

Thirst

Thirst arises from two kinds of signal: a drop in the volume of fluid in the body (for instance after bleeding or heavy sweating) and a rise in the concentration of salts in the blood (which pulls water out of cells). Both are monitored by the hypothalamus, which triggers the drive to drink and the release of hormones that make the kidneys conserve water.

Sexual motivation

Sexual motivation is unusual among biological motives: it is essential to the species but not to the survival of the individual, and in humans it is shaped as much by learning, culture, and relationships as by hormones such as testosterone and oestrogen. Unlike hunger and thirst, it is not a homeostatic drive that builds from deprivation and switches off when a deficit is repaired.

11.11 Emotion: the three components

An emotion is a complex reaction pattern with three components that psychologists agree on, even while they argue about their order:

  • Physiological arousal: bodily changes driven largely by the autonomic nervous system (racing heart, sweating, adrenaline).
  • Cognitive appraisal: the mental interpretation of the situation and of one's own state (labelling something as a threat, an insult, or good news).
  • Behavioural / expressive component: outward expression and action tendency (a facial expression, a raised voice, the urge to approach or flee).

The great theories of emotion differ mainly on which component comes first and causes the others: does the body react and then we feel, do we feel and the body reacts together, or must we interpret our arousal before an emotion exists at all?

11.12 Theories of emotion

Who → what. William James and (independently) Carl Lange gave the James-Lange theory: the bodily response comes first, and the emotion is our perception of that bodily response.

James-Lange theory. The counter-intuitive claim is that we do not tremble because we are afraid; we are afraid because we tremble. A stimulus triggers a specific pattern of bodily arousal, and the emotion is our awareness of that pattern. Order: stimulus → arousal → emotion. A strength is that it takes the body seriously; a weakness is that different emotions can share very similar arousal (fear and excitement both race the heart), so bodily feedback alone may not tell them apart.

Who → what. Walter Cannon and Philip Bard gave the Cannon-Bard theory: arousal and the felt emotion occur at the same time, both triggered together (via the thalamus) rather than one causing the other.

Cannon-Bard theory. Walter Cannon objected that bodily changes are too slow and too similar across emotions to be the source of feeling. He and Philip Bard proposed that a stimulus is routed to the brain, which fires off the bodily arousal and the conscious emotion simultaneously and independently. Order: stimulus → arousal AND emotion together.

Who → what. Stanley Schachter and Jerome Singer gave the two-factor (cognitive-arousal) theory: emotion requires both physiological arousal and a cognitive label for that arousal.

Schachter-Singer two-factor theory. Here emotion is arousal plus interpretation. We notice we are aroused, look to the situation for an explanation, and label the arousal accordingly; the same racing heart might be labelled fear on a cliff edge or love on a date. Order: stimulus → arousal → cognitive label → emotion. This theory explains why context matters so much to what we feel.

Who → what. Richard Lazarus argued that cognitive appraisal comes first: even a rapid, unconscious appraisal of the situation must precede and shape the emotion.

Lazarus appraisal theory. For Richard Lazarus, appraisal is primary; there is no emotion without some evaluation of the event's meaning for one's wellbeing, even if that appraisal is fast and outside awareness.

Who → what. Robert Zajonc argued the opposite of Lazarus: affect can precede cognition, so we can feel (like or fear something) before we have consciously thought about it.

Zajonc and Lazarus held a famous debate. Zajonc pointed to instant preferences and fears that arise before any conscious analysis (the mere-exposure effect, in which we come to like things simply because they are familiar, is his). The modern view is that both are right: some emotional responses are fast and pre-cognitive, others depend on deliberate appraisal.

Facial-feedback hypothesis. Facial expressions do not just show emotion; they can feed back and intensify or even produce it. Holding the face in a smile can make a situation feel a little more pleasant; frowning can make it feel worse. This is the expressive component acting as a cause, a cousin of the James-Lange idea.

TheoryWhat comes firstCore claim
James-LangeArousal, then emotionEmotion is the perception of bodily changes
Cannon-BardArousal and emotion togetherBrain triggers both at once, independently
Schachter-SingerArousal, then cognitive labelEmotion = arousal + interpretation of it
LazarusCognitive appraisalAppraisal is necessary and primary
ZajoncAffect (can precede cognition)We can feel before we think
Trap. The three most-confused theories differ only in ordering. James-Lange: arousal then emotion (sequential). Cannon-Bard: arousal and emotion together (simultaneous). Schachter-Singer: arousal then a cognitive label then emotion. Memorize which word (then, together, label) goes with which name.

11.13 Basic emotions: Ekman and Plutchik

Paul Ekman argued that a small set of basic emotions are universal: recognized and expressed through the same facial expressions across all human cultures, which suggests they are biologically built in rather than learned. His classic list is happiness, sadness, fear, anger, surprise, and disgust (he later added contempt). His cross-cultural studies, including with people who had little contact with the outside world, found the same expressions read the same way everywhere.

Who → what. Paul Ekman established a set of basic, universal emotions with cross-culturally recognized facial expressions.

Robert Plutchik proposed a wheel of emotions: eight primary emotions arranged in four opposing pairs (joy vs sadness, trust vs disgust, fear vs anger, surprise vs anticipation). On his model, emotions vary in intensity (annoyance shading into rage) and combine like colours to form more complex feelings (for example, joy plus trust yields love).

Who → what. Robert Plutchik created the wheel of emotions: eight primary emotions in opposing pairs that vary in intensity and blend into complex emotions.

11.14 Stress and coping

Stress is the process by which we perceive and respond to events (called stressors) that we appraise as threatening or challenging. Its study sits at the junction of motivation, emotion, and the body.

Who → what. Walter Cannon described the fight-or-flight response: an acute stressor triggers the sympathetic nervous system and adrenaline, preparing the body to confront or escape the threat.

Fight-or-flight is the immediate emergency reaction: heart rate and breathing speed up, blood is diverted to the muscles, and the body is primed for vigorous action. It is adaptive for short, physical threats but costly if switched on chronically.

Who → what. Hans Selye described the General Adaptation Syndrome (GAS), the body's three-stage response to prolonged stress.

Hans Selye's General Adaptation Syndrome (GAS) traces how the body responds to sustained stress in three stages:

  1. Alarm reaction. The body's first mobilization, essentially fight-or-flight: resources are marshalled and resistance briefly dips then rises.
  2. Resistance. The body adapts and copes at a high but sustainable level; stress hormones stay elevated as it holds the line.
  3. Exhaustion. If the stressor persists too long, reserves are depleted; vulnerability to illness, breakdown, and burnout rises.
Trap. Cannon's fight-or-flight is the acute, immediate emergency response; Selye's GAS is the longer, three-stage course of prolonged stress. The alarm stage of GAS contains fight-or-flight; do not treat the two theories as rivals.

Richard Lazarus and Susan Folkman stressed that what makes an event stressful is appraisal. In primary appraisal we ask "is this a threat?"; in secondary appraisal we ask "can I cope with it?" They distinguished two broad styles of coping:

  • Problem-focused coping: acting on the stressor itself to change or remove it (making a study plan for a hard exam). Best when the situation is controllable.
  • Emotion-focused coping: managing one's emotional reaction rather than the stressor (relaxation, reframing, seeking comfort). Best when the situation cannot be changed.
Who → what. Richard Lazarus and Susan Folkman built the appraisal-and-coping model, distinguishing problem-focused from emotion-focused coping.

Finally, Thomas Holmes and Richard Rahe tried to quantify life stress with the Social Readjustment Rating Scale (SRRS). They assigned life change units to major life events (death of a spouse scored highest, followed by divorce, and so on down to minor events); a person's total over a year was meant to predict the risk of stress-related illness. The scale is important historically for treating stress as measurable, though it has been criticized for ignoring how differently individuals appraise the same event.

Who → what. Thomas Holmes and Richard Rahe created the Social Readjustment Rating Scale, scoring life events in life change units to estimate stress-related illness risk.
Recap. Motivation initiates, directs, and sustains behaviour; keep need (lack), drive (internal push), motive (reason), and incentive (external pull) apart. The theories run from McDougall's instincts through Hull's drive reduction (homeostasis, primary vs secondary drives) to arousal theory with the inverted-U Yerkes-Dodson law, then incentive and expectancy-value. Content theories add Maslow's hierarchy (deficiency vs growth needs), Murray's manifest needs (measured by the TAT), McClelland's achievement/affiliation/power, and Deci and Ryan's self-determination theory with the overjustification effect. Biological motives centre on the hypothalamus (lateral = hunger, ventromedial = satiety) and hormones (leptin suppresses, ghrelin stimulates). Emotion has three components (arousal, cognition, expression); the theories differ on order: James-Lange (arousal then emotion), Cannon-Bard (both at once), Schachter-Singer (arousal plus label), Lazarus (appraisal first), Zajonc (affect can precede cognition), plus facial feedback, Ekman's universal basic emotions, and Plutchik's wheel. Stress runs from Cannon's fight-or-flight through Selye's three-stage GAS to Lazarus and Folkman's appraisal-and-coping (problem- vs emotion-focused) and the Holmes-Rahe scale.

11.15 The complete map: theories of motivation & emotion and their theorists

Aptitude papers love to pair a named theory of motivation or emotion with the wrong theorist. This is the safety-net roster: every model likely to be named, tied to its author, with a one-line core idea. Learn the person and the one-line hook for each, then no matching item can surprise you.

Key. Motivation asks why we act (what energises and directs behaviour); emotion asks what we feel and in what order body, thought and feeling arrive. Families A and B cover motivation (biological then cognitive/work); Families C and D cover emotion (physiological then cognitive/appraisal).

Family A: Motivation, biological & drive theories

#Theory / modelTheorist(s)Core idea in one line
1Instinct theoryWilliam McDougallBehaviour is driven by inherited instincts, each pairing a perception with an emotion and an action.
2Drive-reduction theoryClark HullA physiological need creates a drive; behaviour that reduces the drive and restores homeostasis is reinforced.
3Arousal theory / Yerkes-Dodson lawRobert Yerkes & John DodsonPerformance peaks at a moderate level of arousal and falls off when arousal is too low or too high.
4Optimal arousal theoryDonald HebbOrganisms act to keep arousal near a preferred set-point, seeking stimulation when bored and calm when overwhelmed.
5Incentive theoryEdward TolmanBehaviour is pulled by the value of external rewards (incentives), not only pushed by internal drives.
6Opponent-process theory of motivationRichard SolomonEvery emotional state triggers an opposite after-reaction that grows with repetition, explaining tolerance and craving.

Family B: Motivation, cognitive, humanistic & work theories

#Theory / modelTheorist(s)Core idea in one line
7Hierarchy of needsAbraham MaslowNeeds form a pyramid from physiological to self-actualisation; lower needs must be met before higher ones motivate.
8ERG theoryClayton AlderferMaslow condensed into three needs (existence, relatedness, growth) that can be pursued at once, with frustration-regression.
9Need theory (achievement, affiliation, power)Henry Murray; David McClelland; John AtkinsonLearned social needs (nAch, nAff, nPow) drive behaviour; measured through the Thematic Apperception Test.
10Two-factor (motivation-hygiene) theoryFrederick HerzbergHygiene factors prevent dissatisfaction; only motivators (achievement, recognition) create real job satisfaction.
11Self-determination theoryEdward Deci & Richard RyanIntrinsic motivation grows when autonomy, competence and relatedness are satisfied.
12Expectancy theoryVictor VroomEffort depends on expectancy, instrumentality and valence multiplied together (effort to performance to reward).
13Goal-setting theoryEdwin Locke & Gary LathamSpecific, difficult, accepted goals raise performance more than vague "do your best" goals.
14Equity theoryJohn Stacey AdamsPeople compare their input-to-outcome ratio with others and act to restore fairness when it feels unequal.
15Self-efficacy theoryAlbert BanduraBelief in one's capability to succeed at a task shapes effort, persistence and the goals one attempts.
16FlowMihaly CsikszentmihalyiDeep absorption arises when challenge and skill are both high and matched, producing intrinsic reward.
17Theory X and Theory YDouglas McGregorManagers act on one of two views: workers dislike work and need control (X) or seek responsibility (Y).

Family C: Emotion, physiological theories

#Theory / modelTheorist(s)Core idea in one line
18James-Lange theoryWilliam James & Carl LangeBodily arousal comes first; the emotion we feel is our perception of that bodily change (we are afraid because we tremble).
19Cannon-Bard theoryWalter Cannon & Philip BardBodily arousal and the felt emotion occur at the same time and independently via the thalamus.
20Papez circuit / limbic theory of emotionJames Papez; Paul MacLeanEmotion is processed by a circuit of limbic structures (hypothalamus, cingulate, hippocampus); MacLean named it the limbic system.
21Amygdala fear pathwayJoseph LeDouxA fast "low road" routes threat signals straight to the amygdala before the cortex fully interprets them.

Family D: Emotion, cognitive & appraisal theories

#Theory / modelTheorist(s)Core idea in one line
22Two-factor (cognitive-arousal) theoryStanley Schachter & Jerome SingerEmotion equals physiological arousal plus a cognitive label drawn from the situation.
23Cognitive appraisal theoryRichard Lazarus; Magda ArnoldAppraisal of an event's meaning for wellbeing comes first and determines which emotion is felt.
24Affective primacy / mere-exposure effectRobert ZajoncEmotional reactions can occur before and without conscious thought; mere repeated exposure breeds liking.
25Facial-feedback hypothesisSilvan Tomkins; Paul EkmanFeedback from one's own facial expression influences and intensifies the emotion experienced.
26Basic emotions & universal facial expressionsPaul EkmanA small set of basic emotions (happiness, sadness, anger, fear, disgust, surprise) is recognised across all cultures.
27Differential emotions theoryCarroll IzardA set of discrete, innate emotions each has its own neural, expressive and experiential signature from infancy.
28Wheel of emotionsRobert PlutchikEight primary emotions in opposing pairs combine and vary in intensity, arranged on a colour wheel.
29Circumplex model of affectJames RussellAll feelings map onto two dimensions, valence (pleasant to unpleasant) and arousal (high to low).
30Broaden-and-build theoryBarbara FredricksonPositive emotions widen thought-action range and build lasting personal resources over time.
Trap. Papers swap the order-of-events theories: James-Lange puts the body first, Cannon-Bard makes body and feeling simultaneous, and Schachter-Singer adds a cognitive label to arousal. Also do not confuse Maslow's five-level hierarchy with Alderfer's three-need ERG, or attribute the need for achievement to Maslow (it belongs to Murray and McClelland).
Recap. Motivation splits into push (drive, instinct, arousal) and pull/cognitive (incentive, expectancy, goal-setting, self-determination) plus the work theories (Herzberg, Vroom, Adams, McGregor). Emotion splits into physiological ordering theories (James-Lange, Cannon-Bard, limbic and amygdala pathways) and cognitive/appraisal theories (Schachter-Singer, Lazarus, Zajonc, Ekman, Plutchik, Russell, Fredrickson). Anchor each to its theorist and its one-line hook.

12 Social Psychology

Human beings are profoundly social animals. Almost nothing we do, from the opinions we hold to the shirts we buy to the way we behave in an emergency, is untouched by the presence, real or imagined, of other people. Social psychology is the scientific study of how the thoughts, feelings, and behaviours of individuals are influenced by the actual, imagined, or implied presence of others. This definition, given by the American psychologist Gordon Allport, deliberately includes imagined and implied presence: you slow your car on an empty highway because a police officer might be watching, and you behave more politely because you imagine how others would judge you. This chapter builds the field from zero: how we form and change attitudes, how we explain behaviour, how groups and authorities sway us, why prejudice arises, when we help strangers, and what draws us to one another.

12.1 Attitudes and the ABC Model

An attitude is a relatively enduring evaluation, favourable or unfavourable, of a person, object, idea, or situation. Attitudes are not the same as facts; they are judgements coloured by feeling. Psychologists analyse an attitude into three components, called the ABC model.

  • Affective component: the emotions and feelings the object evokes. Feeling anxious at the sight of a snake.
  • Behavioural component: the way the attitude disposes you to act. Crossing the road to avoid the snake.
  • Cognitive component: the thoughts and beliefs you hold about the object. Believing that snakes are dangerous.

Attitudes are learned, not innate. They form through classical conditioning (an object repeatedly paired with pleasant or unpleasant feelings acquires that emotional tone), through operant conditioning (attitudes that earn approval are strengthened), through observational learning (children absorb the attitudes of parents and peers), and through direct experience with the object itself, which tends to produce stronger and more stable attitudes.

12.2 The Attitude-Behaviour Gap

Common sense says attitudes drive behaviour. Often they do, but the link is looser than expected. The classic demonstration came from the American sociologist Richard LaPiere. In the early 1930s he travelled across the United States with a young Chinese couple, stopping at over 250 hotels and restaurants. They were refused service only once. Months later he wrote to the same establishments asking whether they would serve Chinese guests; over 90 percent of those who replied said they would not. Their stated attitude (prejudice) flatly contradicted their actual behaviour (courteous service).

Who → what. Richard LaPiere (1934) exposed the attitude-behaviour gap: what people say their attitude is often fails to predict what they actually do.

Later research clarified when attitudes do predict behaviour: when the attitude is strong, specific to the behaviour in question, formed through direct experience, and when situational pressures are weak. Weak or general attitudes are easily overridden by the demands of the moment.

12.3 Persuasion: Changing Attitudes

Persuasion is the deliberate effort to change attitudes through communication. Systematic study of it began during the Second World War with the American psychologist Carl Hovland and his colleagues at Yale University, whose work is called the Yale approach (or Yale communication model). They asked, in effect, who says what to whom, and identified the factors that make a message persuasive.

  • The source: credible, expert, trustworthy, and likeable communicators persuade more.
  • The message: two-sided arguments, moderate fear appeals, and clear conclusions work better in many settings.
  • The audience: intelligence, self-esteem, age, and prior commitment all affect how a message lands.
Who → what. Carl Hovland led the Yale attitude-change programme, framing persuasion as source, message, channel, and audience factors.

The Elaboration Likelihood Model

The most influential modern theory of persuasion is the elaboration likelihood model of the American psychologists Richard Petty and John Cacioppo. Elaboration means the amount of careful, effortful thinking a person devotes to a message. The model proposes two routes to persuasion.

  • Central route: when the listener is motivated and able to think, they scrutinise the actual quality of the arguments. Attitude change through this route is deep, lasting, and resistant to counter-persuasion.
  • Peripheral route: when the listener is unmotivated or distracted, they rely on superficial cues (an attractive spokesperson, a catchy jingle, the sheer number of arguments). Change through this route is shallow and short-lived.
Why. Advertisers exploit both routes: a technical product aimed at experts uses central-route evidence, while an impulse product uses peripheral cues like celebrity and music.

12.4 Cognitive Dissonance and Self-Perception

Sometimes changing our behaviour changes our attitudes, rather than the reverse. The landmark theory here is cognitive dissonance theory, proposed in 1957 by the American psychologist Leon Festinger. Dissonance is the uncomfortable psychological tension that arises when we hold two inconsistent thoughts, or when our behaviour clashes with our attitude. Because the tension is unpleasant, we are motivated to reduce it, usually by changing the attitude to fit the behaviour.

In the famous 1959 experiment by Leon Festinger and James Carlsmith, participants performed an extremely boring task and were then paid either one dollar or twenty dollars to tell the next person it was enjoyable. Those paid only one dollar later rated the task as more enjoyable than those paid twenty dollars. Why? The twenty-dollar group had ample external justification for lying (the money), so felt no dissonance. The one-dollar group had insufficient justification, so to reduce the tension of having lied for almost nothing, they convinced themselves the task really was interesting.

Who → what. Leon Festinger (1957) proposed cognitive dissonance theory: inconsistency between attitude and behaviour creates tension that pushes the attitude to shift.

A rival explanation is self-perception theory, offered by the American psychologist Daryl Bem. Bem argued that we often do not have strong pre-existing attitudes at all; instead we infer our attitudes by observing our own behaviour, just as an outside observer would. "I keep eating salad, so I must like salad." Where dissonance theory relies on inner tension, self-perception relies on cool self-observation. Both survive; dissonance better explains cases involving clear conflict, self-perception better explains cases where the attitude was weak or unclear to begin with.

12.5 Social Cognition and Attribution

Attribution is the process of explaining the causes of behaviour, our own and others'. The founder of attribution theory was the Austrian-American psychologist Fritz Heider, who described ordinary people as naive psychologists or naive scientists constantly trying to work out why others act as they do. Heider drew the central distinction of the whole field: we attribute behaviour either to internal (dispositional) causes, such as personality, effort, or attitude, or to external (situational) causes, such as luck, task difficulty, or social pressure.

Who → what. Fritz Heider (1958) founded attribution theory and the internal-versus-external distinction that organises it.

Kelley's Covariation Model

The American psychologist Harold Kelley systematised how people decide between internal and external causes. His covariation model says we act like statisticians, watching how behaviour "covaries" with possible causes across three dimensions.

  • Consensus: do other people behave the same way toward this stimulus? If everyone laughs at the comedian, consensus is high.
  • Consistency: does this person behave the same way across time and situations? If she always laughs at him, consistency is high.
  • Distinctiveness: does the person behave this way only toward this stimulus? If she laughs only at this comedian, distinctiveness is high.

High consensus, high consistency, and high distinctiveness together point to an external attribution (it is the comedian). Low consensus, high consistency, and low distinctiveness point to an internal attribution (she laughs at everything).

Attributional Biases

People are not the cool statisticians Kelley described; attribution is riddled with systematic errors.

  • Fundamental attribution error: the tendency, when explaining other people's behaviour, to overestimate internal dispositions and underestimate the situation. Assuming the driver who cut you off is a rude person, not someone rushing to hospital.
  • Actor-observer bias: we attribute our own behaviour to the situation but others' behaviour to their disposition. "I failed because the exam was unfair; he failed because he is lazy."
  • Self-serving bias: we credit our successes to internal factors (ability) but blame our failures on external factors (bad luck), protecting self-esteem.
  • Just-world hypothesis: the belief that the world is fundamentally fair, so people get what they deserve. This can lead to blaming the victim of misfortune, reasoning that they must somehow have brought it on themselves.
Trap. Do not confuse the fundamental attribution error with the actor-observer bias. The fundamental attribution error is about over-attributing other people's behaviour to disposition. The actor-observer bias is the contrast between how we explain ourselves (situation) versus others (disposition). The actor-observer bias includes the self side; the fundamental attribution error is only about judging others.

12.6 Social Influence: Conformity

Conformity is changing one's behaviour or belief to match that of a group. Two early experiments are essential. The Turkish-American psychologist Muzafer Sherif used the autokinetic effect, an optical illusion in which a stationary pinpoint of light in a dark room appears to move. Alone, participants gave widely varying estimates of how far it "moved". Placed in groups, their estimates converged on a shared group norm, and they kept using that norm even when tested alone again. Because the situation was genuinely ambiguous, this showed conformity born of uncertainty.

The American psychologist Solomon Asch then asked whether people would conform even when the correct answer was obvious. In his line-judgment study (1951), a participant sat with several confederates and judged which of three comparison lines matched a standard line. The answer was clear, yet the confederates unanimously gave a wrong answer on certain trials. About 75 percent of real participants conformed to the obviously wrong majority at least once, and roughly a third of all critical responses went along with the group.

Who → what. Solomon Asch (1951) showed with unambiguous line judgments that people conform to a unanimous majority even against the clear evidence of their own eyes.

Why do we conform? Deutsch and Gerard distinguished two motives.

  • Normative social influence: conforming to be liked and accepted, to avoid rejection or ridicule. This typically produces public compliance without private belief. Asch's participants privately knew the answer but went along to fit in.
  • Informational social influence: conforming because we believe others have accurate information, especially in ambiguous situations. This produces genuine private acceptance. Sherif's participants truly did not know the answer, so they used the group as information.
Trap. Match the study to the motive. Asch's clear lines produced mainly normative influence (fear of standing out). Sherif's ambiguous light produced mainly informational influence (genuine uncertainty). Normative changes public behaviour; informational changes private belief.

12.7 Obedience to Authority

Conformity is yielding to peers; obedience is yielding to a figure of authority who issues a direct command. The definitive research was conducted by the American psychologist Stanley Milgram at Yale University in the early 1960s, partly to understand how ordinary Germans had participated in the Holocaust. Participants were told they were in a "learning" experiment and were instructed by an experimenter in a lab coat to deliver electric shocks of increasing voltage to a "learner" (actually a confederate who received no real shocks) each time he made an error. The shock generator was labelled up to 450 volts and "Danger: Severe Shock".

Despite the learner's scripted screams and eventual silence, a startling 65 percent of participants continued all the way to the maximum 450 volts when prodded by the experimenter's calm insistence that "the experiment requires that you continue". Milgram concluded that ordinary people will inflict serious harm on an innocent person when instructed by a legitimate authority. Obedience rose when the authority was close and prestigious, and fell when the victim was close, the authority was distant, or other "participants" rebelled.

Who → what. Stanley Milgram (1963) demonstrated that about two-thirds of ordinary people would deliver apparently lethal shocks under orders from an authority figure.

12.8 Compliance Techniques

Compliance is agreeing to a direct request. Researchers have catalogued reliable techniques that increase it.

  • Foot-in-the-door: secure agreement to a small request first, then a larger one. Having complied once, people wish to appear consistent. Agreeing to display a small sign, then a large billboard.
  • Door-in-the-face: make a large, unreasonable request that is refused, then follow with the smaller request you actually wanted. The concession triggers reciprocity. "Volunteer for two years?" No. "Then just one afternoon?" Yes.
  • Low-balling: get commitment to an attractive offer, then reveal added costs. Because people have already committed, many stay in. Agreeing to a car price, then being told fees were "forgotten".

12.9 The Stanford Prison Experiment

The American psychologist Philip Zimbardo extended the study of situational power in his 1971 Stanford prison experiment. Volunteer students were randomly assigned to be "guards" or "prisoners" in a simulated basement prison. Within days the guards became cruel and abusive and the prisoners became passive and distressed. The study, planned for two weeks, was halted after six days. Zimbardo argued that the situation and the assigned roles, not the personalities of the students, produced the brutality. The study is now also famous for its serious ethical and methodological criticisms, including the possibility that guards were coached toward harshness.

Trap. Keep the three great social-influence studies distinct. Asch studied conformity to peers (line judgments). Milgram studied obedience to an authority (electric shocks). Zimbardo studied the power of assigned roles and situations (prison simulation). Peers, authority, roles.

12.10 Group Processes

Mere membership of a group changes how we perform and decide.

Social Facilitation

The very first social psychology experiment, by the American psychologist Norman Triplett in 1898, found that cyclists rode faster when racing against others than against the clock. Social facilitation is the tendency for the presence of others to improve performance. The American psychologist Robert Zajonc later resolved an apparent contradiction: the presence of others improves performance on simple or well-learned tasks but impairs it on complex or novel ones, because arousal strengthens the dominant response, which is correct for easy tasks and wrong for hard ones.

Who → what. Norman Triplett (1898) ran arguably the first social psychology experiment; Robert Zajonc explained facilitation and impairment through arousal and the dominant response.

Social Loafing

The opposite can also happen. The French engineer Max Ringelmann found in the 1910s that people pulling together on a rope exerted less individual effort than when pulling alone. Social loafing is the reduction in individual effort when working in a group where personal contributions cannot be identified, because responsibility feels shared.

Deindividuation and Group Polarization

Deindividuation is the loss of individual self-awareness and self-restraint that can occur in a crowd, especially under anonymity, leading to impulsive and sometimes antisocial behaviour, as in riots or online mobs. Group polarization is the tendency for group discussion to strengthen the members' initial leanings, so that a group of mildly cautious people becomes very cautious, and a group of mildly risky people becomes very risky, after debating.

Groupthink

The American psychologist Irving Janis coined groupthink to describe the faulty decision-making that occurs in highly cohesive groups when the desire for harmony and unanimity overrides realistic appraisal of alternatives. Symptoms include an illusion of invulnerability, self-censorship of doubts, pressure on dissenters, and the emergence of self-appointed "mindguards" who shield the group from disturbing information. Janis analysed foreign-policy fiascos such as the 1961 Bay of Pigs invasion as products of groupthink.

Who → what. Irving Janis (1972) named groupthink: cohesive groups suppress dissent and reach poor decisions in pursuit of consensus.

Minority Influence

Influence is not only top-down. The French psychologist Serge Moscovici showed that a consistent, confident minority can, over time, convert the majority to its view. Minority influence tends to produce genuine, private, and lasting attitude change rather than mere public compliance, and is a key engine of social innovation.

12.11 Intergroup Relations: Prejudice and Its Reduction

Three linked concepts describe negative intergroup responses. A stereotype is a cognitive belief that generalises characteristics to all members of a group. Prejudice is an affective negative attitude toward a group and its members. Discrimination is the behavioural expression, unfair treatment based on group membership. They map neatly onto the ABC model: stereotype (cognitive), prejudice (affective), discrimination (behavioural).

Realistic Conflict Theory and Robbers Cave

The Turkish-American psychologist Muzafer Sherif tested where intergroup hostility comes from in his 1954 Robbers Cave study. At a summer camp, boys were split into two groups (the "Eagles" and the "Rattlers"). Competition for scarce prizes rapidly bred hostility, name-calling, and raids. This supports realistic conflict theory: intergroup prejudice arises from real or perceived competition over limited resources. Crucially, Sherif then reduced the hostility not by mere contact but by introducing superordinate goals, shared aims (such as fixing the camp water supply) that neither group could achieve alone, forcing cooperation.

Who → what. Muzafer Sherif (1954) showed with the Robbers Cave study that competition breeds prejudice (realistic conflict theory) and that superordinate goals dissolve it.

Social Identity Theory

The British social psychologist Henri Tajfel argued that competition is not even necessary. In his "minimal group" studies, merely categorising people into arbitrary groups was enough to make them favour their own. His social identity theory holds that part of our self-concept comes from group memberships, so we boost self-esteem by favouring the in-group and derogating the out-group. Prejudice can thus arise from the sheer act of dividing people into "us" and "them".

Who → what. Henri Tajfel developed social identity theory: in-group favouritism can arise from mere categorisation, without any competition for resources.

The Contact Hypothesis

The American psychologist Gordon Allport proposed the contact hypothesis in 1954: prejudice can be reduced by contact between groups, but only under favourable conditions, namely equal status between the groups, common goals, intergroup cooperation, and the support of authorities or social norms. Mere contact without these conditions can even worsen hostility.

12.12 Prosocial Behaviour and the Bystander Effect

Prosocial behaviour is any act intended to benefit another. Research was galvanised by the 1964 murder of Kitty Genovese in New York, which early reports claimed was witnessed by many neighbours who did nothing (later journalism corrected several details, but the psychological question endured). The American psychologists Bibb Latane and John Darley asked why. They demonstrated the bystander effect: the more people present at an emergency, the less likely any one of them is to help.

The key mechanism is diffusion of responsibility: when many are present, each person feels that responsibility to act is shared and diluted, so no one acts. Additional factors are pluralistic ignorance (each bystander looks to others' inaction and concludes there is no emergency) and evaluation apprehension (fear of embarrassment if one intervenes wrongly). Latane and Darley modelled helping as a five-step decision: notice the event, interpret it as an emergency, assume responsibility, decide how to help, and then act.

Who → what. Bibb Latane and John Darley (1968) identified the bystander effect and diffusion of responsibility: help becomes less likely as the number of witnesses grows.

What motivates the helping that does occur? The American psychologist Daniel Batson proposed the empathy-altruism hypothesis: when we feel genuine empathy for a person in need, we are moved to help for their sake alone, a truly altruistic motive, rather than merely to relieve our own discomfort. Without empathy, helping is more likely to be egoistic, aimed at reducing our own distress or gaining reward.

12.13 Aggression

Aggression is behaviour intended to harm another who is motivated to avoid the harm. One classic account is the frustration-aggression hypothesis, put forward in 1939 by the American psychologist John Dollard and colleagues, which held that frustration (the blocking of a goal) always leads to aggression, and aggression always stems from frustration. Later theorists softened this: frustration produces a readiness for aggression that may be displaced onto a substitute target or inhibited, rather than an inevitability.

A second major account is the social learning of aggression, associated with the Canadian-American psychologist Albert Bandura. His "Bobo doll" studies showed that children who watched an adult beat an inflatable doll later imitated that aggression, demonstrating that aggression can be learned by observation and modelling, not only driven by frustration.

12.14 Interpersonal Attraction

Finally, social psychology studies what draws people together. Several robust factors emerge.

  • Proximity: physical or functional nearness is the strongest predictor of who becomes friends; we like those we encounter often.
  • Mere-exposure effect: demonstrated by Robert Zajonc, repeated exposure to a stimulus, including a person, tends to increase liking for it, even without conscious recognition.
  • Similarity: we are attracted to those who share our attitudes, values, and background; the folk idea that "opposites attract" is largely unsupported.
  • Physical attractiveness: attractive people are liked more and, through the "halo effect", are assumed to possess other good qualities.
  • Matching hypothesis: in choosing long-term partners, people tend to pair with others of roughly similar physical attractiveness rather than always pursuing the most attractive available person.

12.15 A Table of Landmark Studies

The following studies recur on entrance examinations; memorise the pairing of researcher, study, and core finding.

ResearcherStudyCore finding
Norman Triplett (1898)Cyclists racingPresence of others speeds performance (social facilitation).
Max Ringelmann (1913)Rope-pullingIndividual effort drops in groups (social loafing).
Richard LaPiere (1934)Travelling with a Chinese coupleStated attitudes fail to predict behaviour (attitude-behaviour gap).
Muzafer Sherif (1935)Autokinetic effectGroups form norms in ambiguity (informational conformity).
Solomon Asch (1951)Line judgmentPeople conform to a wrong unanimous majority (normative conformity).
Muzafer Sherif (1954)Robbers CaveCompetition breeds prejudice; superordinate goals reduce it.
Leon Festinger & James Carlsmith (1959)Boring-task, one dollar vs twentyInsufficient justification shifts attitudes (cognitive dissonance).
Stanley Milgram (1963)Obedience shock study65 percent obeyed to maximum voltage under authority.
Albert Bandura (1961)Bobo dollAggression learned by observation and imitation.
Bibb Latane & John Darley (1968)Bystander emergenciesMore witnesses, less helping (diffusion of responsibility).
Philip Zimbardo (1971)Stanford prisonAssigned roles and situation drive cruelty.
Irving Janis (1972)Analysis of policy fiascosCohesive groups suppress dissent (groupthink).
Key. Social psychology's master lesson is the power of the situation: ordinary people, placed in the right circumstances, will conform against the evidence of their own eyes (Asch), obey to the point of apparent lethal harm (Milgram), adopt brutal roles (Zimbardo), and walk past emergencies (Latane and Darley). Explaining behaviour by disposition alone is the fundamental attribution error itself.
Recap. Attitudes have affective, behavioural, and cognitive components; they predict behaviour only weakly (LaPiere), and can be changed by persuasion through central or peripheral routes (Petty and Cacioppo) or shifted by our own behaviour through cognitive dissonance (Festinger) or self-perception (Bem). We explain behaviour as internal or external (Heider), using consensus, consistency, and distinctiveness (Kelley), but fall into the fundamental attribution error, actor-observer, self-serving, and just-world biases. Social influence spans conformity (Sherif's informational, Asch's normative), obedience (Milgram), compliance tricks, and the power of roles (Zimbardo). Groups show facilitation (Triplett, Zajonc), loafing (Ringelmann), deindividuation, polarization, groupthink (Janis), and minority influence. Prejudice arises from competition (Sherif's Robbers Cave, realistic conflict) and mere categorisation (Tajfel's social identity), and is reduced by superordinate goals and favourable contact (Allport). We help less in crowds (bystander effect) but more when we feel empathy (Batson), aggress from frustration (Dollard) and imitation (Bandura), and are attracted by proximity, mere exposure, similarity, and matching.

12.16 The complete map: every social-psychology theory and its theorist

Social psychology is the general-psychology area an aptitude paper most loves to test through name-matching: a theory in one column, a theorist in the other. This is the safety-net roster. Skim it until every pairing here reads instantly, because on the exam these come as one-mark recognition items, not essays.

Key. Grouped by family (attitudes and self; attribution; social influence and groups; relationships, aggression and prosocial behaviour). Numbering runs continuously across all families so you can count coverage. Full names are given first so you learn the person, not just a surname.

Family A: Attitudes, dissonance and the self

#Theory / phenomenonTheorist(s)Core idea in one line
1Cognitive dissonanceLeon FestingerHolding two clashing cognitions creates tension we reduce by changing an attitude or behaviour.
2Social comparison theoryLeon FestingerWe evaluate our own abilities and opinions by comparing ourselves to similar others.
3Self-perception theoryDaryl BemWe infer our own attitudes by observing our behaviour, as an outsider would.
4Balance theoryFritz HeiderPeople prefer consistent (balanced) relations among self, other and object; imbalance pushes for change.

8Reactance theoryJack BrehmThreats to a freedom trigger a motivational push to reassert it (do the forbidden thing).
9Stereotype threatClaude SteeleFear of confirming a negative group stereotype depresses performance on the relevant task.

12Correspondent inference theoryEdward Jones & Keith DavisWe infer a disposition when behaviour is freely chosen and has few, non-common effects.

14Fundamental attribution errorLee RossWe over-attribute others' behaviour to disposition and under-weight the situation.

17Just-world hypothesisMelvin LernerPeople need to believe the world is fair, so victims are seen as deserving their fate.

19Conformity (line-judgement studies)Solomon AschPeople go along with a clearly wrong majority under group pressure.

21GroupthinkIrving JanisCohesive groups suppress dissent to preserve harmony, producing poor decisions.
22Group polarizationSerge Moscovici & Marisa ZavalloniGroup discussion pushes members' average view to a more extreme position.
23Risky shiftJames StonerGroups often make riskier decisions than the individuals would alone (a form of polarization).
24Social facilitationNorman Triplett; Robert ZajoncThe presence of others boosts dominant responses (helps easy tasks, hurts hard ones).
25Social loafingBibb LatanePeople exert less effort on a task when responsibility is shared in a group.
