Trap. Festinger owns two classics (cognitive dissonance and social comparison); do not let a paper hand one of them to another name. Watch the Kelley overlaps too: he authored the covariation model of attribution but co-authored social exchange theory with Thibaut. And social facilitation has two owners: Triplett ran the first study (1898), Zajonc gave the drive-theory explanation.
Recap. If you can match each of these 38 pairings on sight, the social-psychology name-matching items are free marks. Anchor the tentpoles first (Festinger, Asch, Milgram, Zimbardo, Tajfel, Sherif, Bandura, Cialdini), then fill in the attribution cluster (Heider, Kelley, Jones, Weiner, Ross) and the Latane group-apathy trio (social loafing, bystander effect, diffusion of responsibility).

13 Personality

Why do two people raised on the same street, fed the same food, taught by the same teachers, grow into a shy bookish clerk and a loud restless salesman? Why does a friend behave with such maddening consistency that you can predict, weeks in advance, exactly how they will react to bad news? The answer psychologists reach for is personality: the enduring, organised pattern of thinking, feeling, and behaving that makes a person recognisably themselves across time and across situations. This chapter walks from the oldest attempts to sort people into types, through the trait movement that counted and measured them, into the depth psychologies that looked for hidden engines, and out to the humanistic, behavioural, and Indian views that each redraw the picture. Each family answers three questions differently: what is a human being basically like, where does personality come from, and how can it be measured. Keep those three questions in mind as the theorists file past.

13.1 What personality means

Gordon Allport (Gordon Willard Allport), one of the first psychologists to make personality a respectable scientific field, defined it in 1937 as "the dynamic organization within the individual of those psychophysical systems that determine his characteristic behavior and thought." Unpack that sentence slowly. Dynamic organization means personality is not a heap of separate habits but an active, changing structure. Psychophysical means it is neither purely mental nor purely bodily but both at once, rooted in the nervous system yet expressed in thought. Determine means personality is causal: it actually produces behaviour, it is not just a label pasted on afterwards. Characteristic means it captures what is typical and distinctive about this one person.

Who → what. Gordon Allport → gave psychology its working definition of personality (1937) and launched the modern trait approach; insisted each person is finally unique.

Two research strategies flow from this. The nomothetic approach (from the Greek nomos, law) looks for general laws that apply to everyone, placing each person on the same set of dimensions so people can be compared, ranked, and correlated. The idiographic approach (from idios, one's own) studies the single individual in depth, seeking the pattern that is true of this person and perhaps no other. A questionnaire that scores you on five universal traits is nomothetic; a long case study of one life is idiographic. Allport valued both and warned against letting the search for averages erase the individual.

Key. Nomothetic = universal laws, comparison across people (traits, tests). Idiographic = the unique whole person, studied in depth (case study). Good personality science needs both.

The determinants of personality are usually grouped as biological (genes, temperament, hormones, brain), environmental and cultural (family, upbringing, society, language), and situational (the demands of the moment). The great debates in this chapter are largely arguments about which determinant matters most and how they combine.

13.2 Type theories: sorting people into kinds

The oldest idea is that people come in a small number of types, discrete categories you either belong to or do not. It is intuitive but crude, because real people shade into one another rather than falling into neat boxes.

The four-humour theory descends from the Greek physician Hippocrates (Hippocrates of Kos, around 400 BCE) and was systematised by the Roman physician Galen (Aelius Galenus, second century CE). They held that the body contains four fluids or humours, and that the one in excess sets the temperament: too much blood makes a sanguine person (cheerful, sociable), yellow bile a choleric person (irritable, driven), black bile a melancholic person (sad, thoughtful), and phlegm a phlegmatic person (calm, sluggish). The physiology is long dead, but the four labels survive because they name recognisable moods.

Who → what. Hippocrates → proposed the four humours; Galen → turned them into the four temperaments (sanguine, choleric, melancholic, phlegmatic), the ancestor of all type theory.

Two twentieth-century theories tied personality to body build. The German psychiatrist Ernst Kretschmer proposed that slender asthenic (or leptosomic) builds tend toward a withdrawn, schizoid temperament, while short round pyknic builds tend toward the sociable, mood-swinging cyclothymic temperament, with a muscular athletic type in between. The American psychologist William Sheldon (William Herbert Sheldon) rebuilt this into somatotype theory, rating each body on three dimensions:

  • Endomorphy → soft, round build; the temperament he called viscerotonia, relaxed, sociable, food-loving.
  • Mesomorphy → hard, muscular build; somatotonia, energetic, assertive, adventurous.
  • Ectomorphy → thin, fragile build; cerebrotonia, restrained, private, intellectual.
Trap. Sheldon's and Kretschmer's body-type claims sound scientific but are largely discredited; the correlations were weak and the method biased. Know them as historically important type theories, not as established facts.

Carl Jung (Carl Gustav Jung) contributed the single most durable type distinction: introversion versus extraversion. The introvert's psychic energy flows inward toward thoughts and feelings; the extravert's flows outward toward people and things. Jung meant these as tendencies, not rigid boxes, and this pair later became a cornerstone of the trait theories in section 13.3.

Finally, the American cardiologists Meyer Friedman and Ray Rosenman described the Type A and Type B behaviour patterns. Type A people are competitive, impatient, time-pressured, and easily angered; Type B people are relaxed and easy-going. Their notable finding, from observing heart patients, was that Type A behaviour is linked to higher risk of coronary heart disease, making this a rare type theory with real medical weight (the hostility component especially).

Who → what. Meyer Friedman & Ray Rosenman → the Type A / Type B behaviour patterns; Type A (hurried, hostile, competitive) linked to heart disease.

13.3 Trait theories: measuring the dimensions

Trait theorists abandoned discrete boxes for continuous dimensions. A trait is a relatively stable disposition to behave in a certain way across situations; everyone has some amount of every trait, so we differ in degree, not in kind. The question became: how many traits are there, and which are basic?

Gordon Allport's traits

Allport distinguished three levels. A cardinal trait is a single ruling passion so dominant it organises a whole life, so strong the person's name becomes an adjective (a "scrooge," a "narcissist"); most people have none. Central traits are the five to ten characteristic qualities you would list to describe a friend (honest, anxious, generous). Secondary traits are narrow preferences that surface only in particular contexts (nervous in exams, fond of spicy food).

Key. Allport's three levels: cardinal (one all-dominating trait, rare) > central (the handful that define you) > secondary (situation-specific quirks).

Raymond Cattell and factor analysis

The British-American psychologist Raymond Cattell (Raymond Bernard Cattell) brought statistics to the problem. He used factor analysis, a technique that scans a large table of correlations and finds the smaller number of underlying dimensions that account for how the measured variables cluster together. Cattell distinguished surface traits, clusters of behaviour that visibly go together, from source traits, the deeper underlying factors that cause those clusters. Factor-analysing thousands of trait words, he arrived at 16 source traits and built the 16 Personality Factor Questionnaire (16PF) to measure them (for example, reserved vs outgoing, trusting vs suspicious, relaxed vs tense).

Who → what. Raymond Cattell → used factor analysis to reduce personality to 16 source traits; built the 16PF; distinguished surface traits (visible clusters) from source traits (underlying causes).

Hans Eysenck and the PEN model

The German-British psychologist Hans Eysenck (Hans Jurgen Eysenck) argued Cattell's sixteen factors were too many and themselves correlated; a smaller set of broad, biologically grounded super-factors would do. His PEN model has three:

  • Extraversion vs Introversion (E) → sociable and stimulation-seeking vs quiet and reserved. Eysenck rooted this in cortical arousal: introverts have naturally higher baseline brain arousal, so they seek less external stimulation to feel comfortable, while extraverts are chronically under-aroused and hunt for excitement.
  • Neuroticism vs Stability (N) → anxious, moody, and reactive vs calm and even. He tied this to reactivity of the autonomic nervous system.
  • Psychoticism (P) → a later-added dimension of tough-mindedness, aggression, and cold non-conformity, linked tentatively to testosterone.

Eysenck's insistence on a biological basis and on a very few broad dimensions is his signature. His E and N dimensions have held up extremely well in later research.

Who → what. Hans Eysenck → the PEN model (Psychoticism, Extraversion, Neuroticism); argued personality has a biological, inherited basis (introverts = higher cortical arousal).

The Big Five (Five Factor Model)

Decades of factor-analytic work by many researchers, brought to prominence by Paul Costa (Paul T. Costa Jr.) and Robert McCrae (Robert R. McCrae), converged on five broad factors, remembered by the acronym OCEAN:

  • Openness to experience → imaginative, curious, unconventional vs practical and conventional.
  • Conscientiousness → organised, disciplined, dependable vs careless and impulsive.
  • Extraversion → sociable, energetic, assertive vs reserved.
  • Agreeableness → kind, trusting, cooperative vs hostile and suspicious.
  • Neuroticism → anxious, emotionally unstable vs calm (its opposite pole is emotional stability).

The model rests on the lexical hypothesis: the idea that the most important individual differences have, over history, become encoded as single words in ordinary language, so that analysing the trait-adjectives of a language will reveal the true structure of personality. The Big Five is today the most widely accepted framework, measured by the NEO-PI (NEO Personality Inventory). It sits neatly between Cattell's sixteen and Eysenck's three: Eysenck's E and N reappear, while his single P roughly splits into low Conscientiousness and low Agreeableness, and Openness is added.

Trap. Do not confuse the counts. Cattell = 16 factors, Eysenck (PEN) = 3, Big Five / Costa & McCrae = 5. All three used factor analysis; they disagree only on how many factors best summarise the data.
Why. The numbers differ because factor analysis lets the researcher choose how broadly to group. Slice finely and you get Cattell's 16; group into the broadest possible super-factors and you get Eysenck's 3; the Big Five is the middle level most researchers find replicates best across cultures and languages.

13.4 Psychodynamic theories: the hidden engine

Where trait theory describes the surface, psychodynamic theory hunts for the hidden forces beneath. It begins with Sigmund Freud (Sigmund Freud, Austrian neurologist, founder of psychoanalysis).

Freud's structure of the mind

Freud pictured the mind at two angles. The topographic model divides awareness into the conscious (what you are aware of right now), the preconscious (what you could easily call to mind, like your phone number), and the unconscious (wishes, fears, and memories kept out of awareness by force, yet still driving behaviour). The structural model divides the personality into three agencies:

  • Id → present from birth, entirely unconscious, the reservoir of instinctual drives; it runs on the pleasure principle, demanding immediate gratification.
  • Ego → the executive that develops to deal with reality; it runs on the reality principle, delaying gratification until it is safe and appropriate, mediating between id, superego, and world.
  • Superego → the internalised moral authority, the conscience and the ego-ideal, absorbed from parents and society; it judges and punishes with guilt.

When the ego is overwhelmed by conflict between these agencies it feels anxiety, and it protects itself with defence mechanisms, unconscious strategies that distort reality to reduce distress: repression (pushing threats out of awareness), projection (attributing one's own unacceptable impulses to others), denial, displacement (redirecting an impulse to a safer target), rationalisation (inventing acceptable reasons), reaction formation (acting the opposite of a forbidden urge), regression, and sublimation (channelling impulses into socially valued work). Freud's psychosexual stages (oral, anal, phallic, latency, genital) are treated in the developmental chapter and only noted here.

Who → what. Sigmund Freud → founded psychoanalysis; id / ego / superego; conscious / preconscious / unconscious; defence mechanisms; personality driven mostly by unconscious sexual and aggressive instincts.

Carl Jung's analytical psychology

Carl Jung broke from Freud and founded analytical psychology. He accepted a personal unconscious but added a deeper collective unconscious, a layer inherited by all humans containing archetypes, universal images and themes that recur across myths and cultures. Key archetypes include the persona (the social mask we present), the shadow (the dark, denied side of the self), the anima (the feminine element in a man) and the animus (the masculine element in a woman), and the self (the archetype of wholeness and integration). Jung saw motivation as more than sexual; he described a general life energy and the lifelong process of individuation, becoming a whole, integrated person.

Who → what. Carl Jung → analytical psychology; the collective unconscious and its archetypes (persona, shadow, anima, animus, self); introversion/extraversion; broke with Freud over the sexual emphasis.

Alfred Adler's individual psychology

Alfred Adler (Austrian physician) founded individual psychology. For him the master motive is not sex but the drive to overcome a universal childhood inferiority. Every child begins small and helpless; this inferiority feeling spurs a lifelong striving for superiority, meaning striving for competence, mastery, and completion. When this goes wrong it hardens into an inferiority complex (paralysing self-doubt) or a compensating superiority complex (arrogant over-striving). Each person develops a unique style of life, an overall strategy for pursuing their goals, shaped early on. Adler stressed birth order (first-borns dethroned by a sibling, pampered youngest children, and so on) as one influence on that style, and made social interest (Gemeinschaftsgefuhl), a feeling of connection with and concern for humanity, his mark of psychological health.

Who → what. Alfred Adler → individual psychology; inferiority and the striving for superiority; style of life; birth order; social interest as the sign of health.
Trap. The favourite exam question contrasts the three depth theorists on motivation. Freud → unconscious sexual/aggressive drives (pleasure); Jung → the collective unconscious and individuation (broader spiritual energy); Adler → overcoming inferiority and striving for superiority (conscious goals and social interest, NOT sex). Freud's unconscious is a seething cauldron; Adler barely uses the unconscious at all.

Karen Horney and the neo-Freudians

Karen Horney (German-American psychoanalyst, pronounced HORN-eye) argued personality is shaped less by instinct than by the child's social relationships. Her central concept is basic anxiety: the feeling of being isolated and helpless in a potentially hostile world, arising when parents fail to meet a child's need for safety. To cope, people develop ten neurotic needs (for affection, for a partner, for power, for prestige, and so on), which she grouped into three broad interpersonal styles:

  • Moving toward people → the compliant, self-effacing solution: winning safety through love and approval.
  • Moving against people → the aggressive, expansive solution: winning safety through power and control.
  • Moving away from people → the detached solution: winning safety through withdrawal and self-sufficiency.

The healthy person uses all three flexibly; the neurotic is locked rigidly into one. Horney also delivered a famous feminist critique of Freud: she rejected his idea of penis envy as the core of feminine psychology, arguing that women's frustrations come from real social and cultural disadvantage, not anatomy, and she countered with the notion of male "womb envy" of women's power to bear children.

Who → what. Karen Horney → basic anxiety; ten neurotic needs grouped as moving toward / against / away from people; challenged Freud's penis-envy account of women, blaming culture not anatomy.

Erich Fromm (German social psychologist) extended this social emphasis, arguing that modern freedom brings isolation, so people often "escape from freedom" into conformity, submission, or destructiveness rather than face the burden of choosing for themselves. Alongside these thinkers, the object-relations school (associated with Melanie Klein, W.R.D. Fairbairn, and Donald Winnicott) shifted the focus from drives to the infant's earliest relationships with caregivers, the internalised "objects" that become templates for all later relationships. These are noted briefly; the thread they share is the move away from Freud's biology toward the social and relational.

13.5 Humanistic theories: growth and the self

Humanistic psychology, the "third force" after psychoanalysis and behaviourism, rejected the view of humans as either bundles of dark instincts or passive products of conditioning. It sees people as basically good, free, and driven toward growth.

Carl Rogers (Carl Ransom Rogers) built his theory around the self-concept, the organised set of beliefs a person holds about who they are. He distinguished the real self (who you actually are) from the ideal self (who you wish to be); a wide gap between them, called incongruence, breeds distress, while a close match, congruence, marks health. Children develop conditions of worth when love is made conditional on behaving as others demand ("I will love you only if..."), and they then distort their experience to keep that approval. The antidote is unconditional positive regard, being accepted and valued without conditions, which frees a person to grow into the fully functioning person: open to experience, living in the present, trusting themselves, and free.

Who → what. Carl Rogers → the self-concept; real vs ideal self; conditions of worth; unconditional positive regard; the fully functioning person; founder of client-centred therapy.

Abraham Maslow (Abraham Harold Maslow) is best known for his hierarchy of needs (treated in the motivation chapter) topped by self-actualization, the drive to fulfil one's unique potential and become all one is capable of becoming. He studied psychologically healthy, high-achieving people and found they shared traits like realism, acceptance, autonomy, and creativity, and that they reported peak experiences, moments of intense joy, wonder, and self-transcendence in which they felt whole and at one with the world.

Who → what. Abraham Maslow → self-actualization at the top of the needs hierarchy; studied healthy people; peak experiences.

13.6 Behavioural and social-cognitive theories

This family locates personality not inside the person but in learning and situations. For B.F. Skinner (Burrhus Frederic Skinner) and radical behaviourism, "personality" is not a real inner thing at all; it is simply the collection of learned behaviour patterns a person's reinforcement history has shaped. There is no need to postulate an inner self, traits, or unconscious: change the reinforcement contingencies and you change the person. What we call a "trait" is just a habit that has been rewarded consistently.

Who → what. B.F. Skinner → radical behaviourism; personality = learned behaviour shaped by reinforcement history; no inner self needed.

Later theorists softened this by putting thought back in. Albert Bandura (Canadian-American psychologist) proposed reciprocal determinism: the person (their thoughts and beliefs), their behaviour, and the environment all influence one another in a continuous three-way loop, rather than the environment simply stamping the person. His most influential concept is self-efficacy, a person's belief in their own capability to succeed at a specific task; high self-efficacy leads people to attempt more, persist longer, and recover from failure faster.

Who → what. Albert Bandura → social-cognitive theory; reciprocal determinism (person, behaviour, environment loop); self-efficacy (belief in one's own capability).

Julian Rotter (Julian Bernard Rotter) added locus of control, a person's generalised belief about what controls the outcomes in their life. Those with an internal locus believe outcomes flow from their own effort and choices; those with an external locus believe outcomes are ruled by luck, fate, or powerful others. Rotter also stressed expectancy: whether we perform a behaviour depends on how much we expect it to bring a reward we value. Walter Mischel (Austrian-American psychologist) lit the fuse of the person-situation debate by showing, in 1968, that broad traits often predict behaviour poorly because behaviour varies so much across situations; a person "honest" on a test may cheat at cards. He argued we must weigh the situation, not just the trait. Mischel is also famous for the delay of gratification studies, the marshmallow test, in which young children who could resist eating one marshmallow now to earn two later tended to show better outcomes years afterward, a demonstration of self-regulation as a stable and consequential personal quality.

Who → what. Julian Rotter → locus of control (internal vs external) and expectancy. Walter Mischel → the person-situation debate; the marshmallow test of delayed gratification.

13.7 Indian perspectives on personality

Indian thought approaches personality through the triguna, the theory of three fundamental qualities (gunas) that constitute all of nature (prakriti) and combine in different proportions in every person:

  • Sattva → the quality of purity, clarity, balance, calm, wisdom, and goodness.
  • Rajas → the quality of activity, passion, restlessness, ambition, and desire.
  • Tamas → the quality of inertia, dullness, ignorance, and lethargy.

Every personality is a shifting blend of the three, and the balance can be cultivated, for example toward greater sattva, through disciplined living. Unlike Western trait models, the triguna is embedded in a moral and spiritual framework aimed at growth. The Indian concept of self also differs sharply from the Western: where much Western theory centres a bounded individual ego, Indian thought distinguishes the everyday, socially embedded, changeable self (ahankara, the "I-maker") from a deeper, unchanging true self (atman), and treats the good life as gradually loosening identification with the surface ego. This gives Indian personality psychology an idiographic, developmental, and transcendent flavour that complements the nomothetic Western tests.

Key. Triguna: Sattva (calm clarity) · Rajas (restless activity) · Tamas (dull inertia). Everyone is a blend; the aim is to cultivate sattva.

13.8 The four paradigms compared

Step back and the whole chapter sorts into four great paradigms. This table answers the three opening questions for each.

ParadigmView of human natureSource of personalityKey figuresHow assessed
PsychodynamicDriven by hidden, often irrational unconscious forces; conflict-riddenEarly childhood; unconscious drives and conflictsFreud, Jung, Adler, Horney, FrommProjective tests (Rorschach, TAT), free association, dream analysis
TraitDescribable by stable, measurable dimensions; consistent across situationsLargely inherited biology plus environment; enduring dispositionsAllport, Cattell, Eysenck, Costa & McCraeSelf-report questionnaires (16PF, EPQ, NEO-PI); factor analysis
HumanisticBasically good, free, and growth-seeking; conscious and self-awareThe self-concept and the drive toward self-actualizationRogers, MaslowQ-sort, self-concept scales, interviews, personal experience
Behavioural / social-cognitiveShaped by learning and situations; malleable, not fixedReinforcement history; learning; beliefs and the environmentSkinner, Bandura, Rotter, MischelDirect behavioural observation; situation-specific measures; self-efficacy scales
Why. No single paradigm is simply right. Each captures something real: the trait view predicts and compares well, the psychodynamic view illuminates inner conflict, the humanistic view honours growth and freedom, and the behavioural view explains change. A complete picture of a person borrows from all four.

13.9 How personality is measured

Two broad testing traditions match the paradigms. Objective (self-report) tests present clear statements the person rates about themselves; they are easy to score and standardised. Examples include Cattell's 16PF, Eysenck's EPQ (Eysenck Personality Questionnaire), the Big Five NEO-PI, and the widely used clinical inventory the MMPI (Minnesota Multiphasic Personality Inventory). Projective tests, favoured by the psychodynamic school, present ambiguous stimuli on the assumption that people "project" their hidden inner world onto them: the Rorschach inkblot test (Hermann Rorschach) and the Thematic Apperception Test (TAT) (Henry Murray and Christiana Morgan), in which the person makes up stories about pictures. Objective tests are reliable but can be faked; projective tests are rich but hard to score reliably.

Trap. Match the test to the school. Projective tests (Rorschach, TAT) = psychodynamic. Self-report inventories (16PF, EPQ, NEO-PI, MMPI) = trait/objective. Mixing these up is a classic exam slip.
Recap. Personality is the enduring pattern that makes a person recognisably themselves (Allport), studied nomothetically (universal dimensions) or idiographically (the unique individual). Type theories sort people into kinds (four humours of Hippocrates and Galen; Kretschmer's and Sheldon's body types; Jung's introvert/extravert; Friedman and Rosenman's Type A/B). Trait theories place everyone on continuous dimensions and disagree only on the count: Allport's cardinal/central/secondary, Cattell's 16 source traits, Eysenck's 3 (PEN, biologically based), and the Big Five/OCEAN of Costa and McCrae built on the lexical hypothesis. Psychodynamic theories seek hidden engines: Freud's id/ego/superego and unconscious drives, Jung's collective unconscious and archetypes, Adler's striving to overcome inferiority, Horney's basic anxiety and moving toward/against/away. Humanistic theories stress growth: Rogers's self-concept, conditions of worth, and unconditional positive regard, and Maslow's self-actualization and peak experiences. Behavioural and social-cognitive theories stress learning and situation: Skinner's reinforcement, Bandura's reciprocal determinism and self-efficacy, Rotter's locus of control, Mischel's person-situation debate and marshmallow test. Indian thought adds the triguna (sattva, rajas, tamas) and a layered self. Four paradigms, four answers, one person.

13.10 The complete map: every theory of personality and its theorist

Personality items in an aptitude paper are almost always match-the-theory-to-the-theorist. This roster gathers, in one place, the full set of personality theories you could be asked to name, grouped into four families and numbered continuously. Skim the core-idea column until each name and its one-line signature stick.

Key. Read every row as theorist plus signature idea, not as a definition to memorise word for word. Names in bold are the label examiners use; the person in bold beside it is who to write on the line. Full names are given first so a first-initial-only option cannot trip you.

Family A: Psychodynamic & psychoanalytic

#Theory / modelTheorist(s)Core idea in one line
1Psychoanalysis (structural & psychosexual)Sigmund FreudPersonality is id, ego and superego in conflict, shaped by oral to genital psychosexual stages and driven by the unconscious.
2Analytical psychologyCarl JungThe psyche holds a personal and a collective unconscious of archetypes; growth is individuation toward the Self.

18Reinforcement sensitivity (BIS/BAS)Jeffrey GrayBehaviour rests on a behavioural inhibition system and a behavioural activation system sensitive to punishment and reward.

#Theory / modelTheorist(s)Core idea in one line
20Self-actualization & hierarchy of needsAbraham MaslowNeeds ascend from physiological to self-actualization; the healthy person fulfils their potential.
21Person-centred / self theoryCarl RogersThe fully functioning person emerges when the self-concept meets unconditional positive regard and low incongruence.
22Personal construct theoryGeorge KellyEach person is a scientist who anticipates events through bipolar personal constructs.
23Needs / press (personology)Henry MurrayBehaviour springs from psychogenic needs interacting with environmental press; assessed via the TAT.

Family D: Social-cognitive, behavioural & biological

Trap. Watch the near-neighbours: Freud is psychosexual while Erikson is psychosocial; Jung's collective unconscious is not Adler's inferiority; Cattell's 16 factors, Eysenck's 3 and the Big Five are three different counts, so match the number to the name. Sheldon gives somatotypes but Kretschmer gives constitutional types, and Rotter's locus of control is often mis-tagged to Bandura.
Recap. Four families cover the field: psychodynamic (Freud, Jung, Adler, Horney, Sullivan, Fromm, Erikson, object relations, self psychology); trait and type (Allport, Cattell, Eysenck, Big Five, HEXACO, Sheldon, Kretschmer, Gray, Cloninger); humanistic (Maslow, Rogers, Kelly, Murray, May); and social-cognitive and behavioural (Bandura, Mischel, Rotter, Skinner, McAdams). Fix the theorist to the one-line signature and the match items answer themselves.

14 Intelligence

Intelligence is one of the oldest and most argued-over ideas in psychology. Everyone recognises it in daily life: the friend who grasps a new game in minutes, the child who reasons her way out of a problem no one taught her, the elder whose store of knowledge seems bottomless. Yet turning that everyday recognition into a precise scientific definition, and then into a number, has kept psychologists busy for more than a century. This chapter builds the concept from the ground up. It asks what intelligence is, surveys the major theories that describe its structure, explains where the familiar IQ score comes from, looks at the two extremes of the distribution, weighs the heredity-versus-environment debate, and closes with newer ideas such as emotional intelligence and creativity. The actual test batteries used to measure intelligence (the Wechsler scales, the Binet-Kamat, the Bhatia battery, and so on) are treated in the assessment chapter; here the focus stays on theory and concept.

14.1 What is intelligence?

There is no single agreed definition. Different psychologists have emphasised different things, which is itself a clue that intelligence is not one simple property but a bundle of related capacities.

Who → what. Alfred Binet (French psychologist, 1857–1911) treated intelligence practically, as good judgement, comprehension and reasoning, the ability to adapt one's thinking to a goal and to criticise one's own conclusions.
Who → what. David Wechsler (Romanian-American psychologist, 1896–1981) gave the most quoted textbook definition: intelligence is "the global capacity of the individual to act purposefully, to think rationally, and to deal effectively with his environment." The word global matters: he saw intelligence as an overall capacity built from many specific abilities plus non-intellectual factors such as drive and persistence.

A famous half-joke, often attributed to Edwin Boring, defines intelligence as "whatever intelligence tests measure." It sounds circular, but it flags a real point: for a long time the concept was pinned to the tests rather than the other way round. Most modern definitions converge on a few recurring elements: the ability to learn from experience, to reason and solve problems, to think abstractly, and to adapt to new situations.

Key. Definitions of intelligence cluster around four themes: learning from experience, abstract reasoning, problem solving, and adaptation to the environment. No one definition is "correct"; each theory below picks which of these to foreground.

14.2 Psychometric and factor theories

The psychometric approach studies intelligence by measuring people on many tasks and then using factor analysis (a statistical method that finds clusters of test items which rise and fall together) to infer the underlying abilities. This is where the debate over "one intelligence versus many" was first fought with numbers.

14.2.1 Spearman's two-factor theory

Who → what. Charles Spearman (English psychologist, 1863–1945) noticed that people who do well on one mental test tend to do well on others. This tendency for all cognitive tests to correlate positively he called the positive manifold.

To explain it, Spearman proposed that every test performance draws on two factors. The first is g, a general factor of mental energy or power that runs through all intellectual tasks. The second is s, a specific factor unique to each particular task (verbal, numerical, spatial and so on). Doing well on an arithmetic test, for example, uses your general g plus the s specific to arithmetic. Spearman is remembered as the founder of the idea of general intelligence, and g remains one of the most robust findings in the field.

Key. In Spearman's model, every ability = g (shared across all tasks) + s (unique to that one task). The positive manifold is the evidence for g.

14.2.2 Thurstone's primary mental abilities

Who → what. Louis Leon Thurstone (American psychologist, 1887–1955) challenged Spearman. Using a different factor-analytic method, he argued intelligence is not one general factor but a set of independent primary mental abilities (PMAs).

Thurstone described seven such abilities: verbal comprehension, word fluency, number (arithmetic), spatial visualisation, associative memory, perceptual speed, and inductive reasoning. In his view, describing a person by a single IQ number wastes information; a profile across the seven PMAs is more faithful. Later analyses showed the PMAs themselves correlate, which actually points back towards a higher-order g, and Thurstone eventually accepted a place for it. The Spearman–Thurstone disagreement (one factor versus several) set the template for a debate that still runs.

14.2.3 Cattell's fluid and crystallized intelligence

Who → what. Raymond Bernard Cattell (British-American psychologist, 1905–1998), a student of Spearman, split g into two broad factors.
  • Fluid intelligence (Gf) is the capacity to reason, see patterns and solve novel problems independent of learned knowledge. It is what you use on a puzzle you have never seen. It peaks in early adulthood and gradually declines with age, and is sensitive to biological factors.
  • Crystallized intelligence (Gc) is the store of knowledge, vocabulary and skills acquired through education and experience. It is culture-loaded and, importantly, it holds steady or even grows into old age.

The distinction elegantly explains why an older adult can be slower on an unfamiliar reasoning puzzle (declining Gf) yet richer in general knowledge and judgement (intact Gc). Cattell's collaborator John Leonard Horn extended the model to many more broad factors.

Trap. Fluid versus crystallized is easy to reverse. Fluid = on-the-spot reasoning with novel material, declines with age. Crystallized = accumulated knowledge, stays or grows with age. A memory hook: crystals build up slowly over time, like your knowledge.

14.2.4 Vernon's hierarchical model

Who → what. Philip Ewart Vernon (English psychologist, 1905–1987) reconciled Spearman and Thurstone with a hierarchy.

At the top sits g. Below it, g splits into two major group factors: v:ed (verbal-educational) and k:m (spatial-mechanical-practical). Each of these divides into minor group factors, and those into specific factors at the bottom. This arrangement showed that "one g" and "many abilities" are not rivals; abilities can be layered, general at the top and increasingly specific toward the base.

14.2.5 Guilford's Structure of Intellect

Who → what. Joy Paul Guilford (American psychologist, 1897–1987) rejected g entirely and proposed a three-dimensional model, the Structure of Intellect (SOI).

Guilford argued that any intellectual act can be classified along three axes:

  • Operations (what the mind does): cognition, memory, divergent production, convergent production, and evaluation.
  • Contents (the kind of material): figural, symbolic, semantic, and behavioural.
  • Products (the form of the result): units, classes, relations, systems, transformations, and implications.

Multiplying the categories (originally 5 × 4 × 6) yielded 120 distinct abilities, later expanded by Guilford to 150 and beyond. Critics found the model unwieldy and hard to validate, but it was hugely influential in one respect: its divergent production operation put creativity firmly on the map (see 14.9).

14.2.6 The Cattell-Horn-Carroll synthesis

Modern psychometrics has largely converged on the Cattell-Horn-Carroll (CHC) theory, which merges Cattell and Horn's Gf-Gc work with John Bissell Carroll's three-stratum model. CHC pictures intelligence in three layers: a general g at the top (stratum III), around ten broad abilities in the middle (stratum II, such as Gf, Gc, memory, processing speed), and many narrow abilities at the base (stratum I). CHC now underlies the design of most contemporary intelligence tests.

14.3 Systems and modern theories

Dissatisfied with a single number, several theorists in the late twentieth century argued intelligence is broader than what school-style tests capture.

14.3.1 Gardner's multiple intelligences

Who → what. Howard Earl Gardner (American psychologist, born 1943) proposed the theory of multiple intelligences in 1983, arguing there are several relatively independent intelligences, each with its own brain basis, rather than one general ability.

His original list contained seven, later expanded:

  • Linguistic (words and language)
  • Logical-mathematical (reasoning, numbers, logic)
  • Spatial (visualising, navigating)
  • Musical (rhythm, pitch, tone)
  • Bodily-kinesthetic (body control, athletics, dance)
  • Interpersonal (understanding others)
  • Intrapersonal (understanding oneself)
  • Naturalistic (recognising patterns in nature), added later

Gardner also floated a possible existential intelligence. The theory is popular among educators because it validates non-academic talents, but critics note it lacks strong psychometric support and that Gardner's "intelligences" may be better described as talents or aptitudes.

14.3.2 Sternberg's triarchic theory

Who → what. Robert Jeffrey Sternberg (American psychologist, born 1949) proposed the triarchic theory, dividing intelligence into three interacting aspects.
  • Analytical (componential) intelligence: the reasoning and evaluating abilities that IQ tests already measure.
  • Creative (experiential) intelligence: dealing with novelty and automatising familiar tasks; insight and invention.
  • Practical (contextual) intelligence: "street smarts", the ability to adapt to, shape or select real-world environments, drawing on tacit knowledge that is rarely taught explicitly.

Sternberg later folded these into the idea of successful intelligence: the ability to set and reach personally meaningful goals in one's cultural context by capitalising on strengths and correcting weaknesses, balancing analytical, creative and practical skills.

14.3.3 The PASS theory

Who → what. Jagannath Prasad Das (Indian-Canadian psychologist), with J.A. Naglieri and J.R. Kirby, built the PASS theory of intelligence on the neuropsychology of Alexander Romanovich Luria (Russian neuropsychologist, 1902–1977).

PASS names four cognitive processes rooted in Luria's functional units of the brain:

  • Planning: setting goals, choosing and monitoring strategies (frontal lobes).
  • Attention: arousal and focused, selective attention (brainstem and lower structures).
  • Simultaneous processing: integrating separate pieces into a whole, as in spatial reasoning (occipital-parietal region).
  • Successive processing: handling information in serial order, as in language and sequencing (frontotemporal region).

PASS is notable for grounding intelligence in brain function and for underlying the CAS (Cognitive Assessment System). It carries special weight in Indian syllabi because of Das's central role.

14.4 Comparison of the major theories

TheoristStructure proposedOne-line idea
Charles Spearmang + specific factors (s)One general factor runs through all mental tasks.
Louis ThurstoneSeven primary mental abilitiesIntelligence is several independent abilities, not one.
Raymond CattellFluid (Gf) and crystallized (Gc)Split g into on-the-spot reasoning versus learned knowledge.
Philip VernonHierarchy: g → v:ed & k:m → specificsGeneral and specific abilities are layered, not rivals.
J.P. GuilfordStructure of Intellect (120+ cells)Operations × contents × products; no g.
Cattell-Horn-CarrollThree strata: g / broad / narrowModern synthesis behind most current tests.
Howard GardnerEight (or more) multiple intelligencesMany independent, domain-specific intelligences.
Robert SternbergTriarchic: analytical, creative, practicalSuccessful intelligence balances three real-world aspects.
J.P. Das (after Luria)PASS: planning, attention, simultaneous, successiveIntelligence as four brain-based cognitive processes.
Trap. g versus multiple intelligences is a classic exam contrast. Spearman (and later CHC) keep a unifying g; Thurstone, Guilford and Gardner emphasise many distinct abilities and downplay or reject a single general factor. Don't attribute "multiple intelligences" to Sternberg (his is the triarchic theory) or to Thurstone (his are "primary mental abilities"); the phrase belongs to Gardner.

14.5 The IQ concept

The intelligence quotient (IQ) is a score that expresses how a person's test performance compares with others. Its history explains why the modern number no longer literally involves a "quotient".

14.5.1 Mental age

Who → what. Alfred Binet and Theodore Simon (French physician, 1873–1961) built the first practical intelligence scale in 1905 to identify Parisian schoolchildren who needed extra help. They introduced mental age (MA): a child who solves the items that an average eight-year-old solves has a mental age of 8, whatever their actual age.

14.5.2 The ratio IQ

Who → what. William Stern (German psychologist, 1871–1938) turned mental age into a single ratio, later multiplied by 100 by Lewis Terman: the ratio IQ.

The formula is: IQ = (mental age ÷ chronological age) × 100. A ten-year-old (chronological age 10) with a mental age of 12 has an IQ of (12 ÷ 10) × 100 = 120. If mental age equals chronological age, IQ is exactly 100, the average by definition.

Why. The ratio IQ works well for children but breaks down for adults. Mental growth flattens after adolescence while chronological age keeps rising, so the ratio would make every adult look as though their IQ is falling year on year. That flaw is exactly what the deviation IQ was invented to fix.

14.5.3 The deviation IQ

Who → what. David Wechsler replaced the ratio with the deviation IQ: a score based on how far a person deviates from the average of their own age group, expressed in standard-deviation units.

The deviation IQ is set so the mean is 100 and (on the Wechsler scales) the standard deviation is 15. Your IQ now says where you stand relative to same-age peers, not a ratio of mental to chronological age. Nearly all modern tests use deviation IQs.

Trap. Ratio IQ (Stern/Terman, MA ÷ CA × 100) is not the same as deviation IQ (Wechsler, standard scores against an age group). If a question gives you mental age and chronological age and asks you to compute, it wants the ratio formula. If it talks about mean 100 and SD 15, it means the deviation IQ.

14.5.4 The normal distribution and classification

IQ scores in the population follow, closely, the normal distribution (the symmetric bell curve). With a mean of 100 and SD of 15, about 68% of people score between 85 and 115, and about 95% between 70 and 130. Scores are grouped into descriptive bands.

Approximate IQ rangeClassification
130 and aboveVery superior / gifted
120–129Superior
110–119High average
90–109Average
80–89Low average
70–79Borderline
Below 70Intellectual disability range
Key. Modern IQ: mean 100, SD 15, normally distributed. About two-thirds of people fall between 85 and 115. The exact band labels vary a little across manuals, so learn the structure rather than memorising every boundary.

14.6 The two extremes

14.6.1 Intellectual disability

Intellectual disability (formerly "mental retardation") is diagnosed when there are significant limitations in both intellectual functioning (reasoning, problem solving, roughly IQ below 70) and adaptive behaviour (everyday conceptual, social and practical skills), with onset during the developmental period. Both criteria must be met; a low IQ alone is not enough. Traditional severity levels, keyed to IQ, are:

LevelApprox. IQ range
Mild50–69
Moderate35–49
Severe20–34
ProfoundBelow 20

Current diagnostic systems (DSM-5, ICD-11) now define severity mainly by the level of adaptive functioning support the person needs rather than by IQ alone, but the IQ-based levels are still widely taught.

14.6.2 Giftedness

At the upper extreme, giftedness refers to markedly above-average ability, conventionally an IQ around 130 or higher, though modern conceptions (for example that of Joseph Renzulli) add creativity and task commitment.

Who → what. Lewis Madison Terman (American psychologist, 1877–1956), who created the Stanford-Binet, launched in 1921 a famous longitudinal study of over 1,500 highly gifted Californian children (nicknamed the "Termites"). Followed for decades, they generally grew into healthy, successful, well-adjusted adults, puncturing the myth that gifted children are frail or socially awkward. The study also showed that very high IQ alone does not guarantee eminence; motivation and opportunity matter.

14.7 Heredity versus environment

How much of the variation in intelligence is due to genes and how much to environment is one of psychology's most sensitive debates. The evidence comes chiefly from kinship studies.

  • Twin studies compare identical (monozygotic) twins, who share all their genes, with fraternal (dizygotic) twins, who share about half. Identical twins, even those reared apart, show markedly more similar IQs, pointing to a substantial genetic contribution.
  • Adoption studies compare adopted children with both their biological and their adoptive parents. Correlations with biological parents support heredity; the rise in adopted children's scores when raised in enriched homes supports environment.

The consensus is that both matter and that they interact: heritability of IQ is often estimated around 0.5 in adults, meaning roughly half the variation within a studied population is associated with genetic differences. Crucially, heritability is a population statistic and does not say how much of any one person's intelligence is "fixed", nor does it explain differences between groups.

Who → what. James Robert Flynn (1934–2020) documented the Flynn effect: average IQ scores rose steadily across the twentieth century, roughly three points per decade in many countries. Because genes cannot change that fast, the rise must be largely environmental (better nutrition, education, health, and familiarity with abstract, test-like thinking). The effect is powerful evidence that environment shapes measured intelligence.

Trap. High heritability does not mean "unchangeable" or "genetic and therefore fixed". The Flynn effect proves scores can climb rapidly through environment even if IQ is highly heritable within a generation. Heritability and malleability are separate ideas.

14.8 Emotional intelligence

Intelligence about emotions became a major topic in the 1990s. Two versions must be kept apart.

Who → what. Peter Salovey (American psychologist) and John D. Mayer (American psychologist) coined emotional intelligence (EI) in 1990 and defined the scientific ability model: EI is a set of mental abilities to perceive, use (facilitate thinking with), understand, and manage emotions in oneself and others. Being an ability, it can in principle be tested with right-and-wrong performance items.
Who → what. Daniel Goleman (American psychologist and journalist) popularised EI in his 1995 bestseller, using a broader mixed model that blends emotional abilities with personality traits and competencies such as self-awareness, self-regulation, motivation, empathy and social skill, and argued EI can matter more than IQ for life success.
Trap. Do not credit Goleman with coining emotional intelligence. Salovey and Mayer defined it (the ability model); Goleman popularised it (the broader mixed model). Exam items love this distinction.

14.9 Creativity

Creativity, the ability to produce work that is both novel and useful/appropriate, overlaps with but is not identical to intelligence. Beyond a modest IQ threshold (around 120), the two show little further correlation, the threshold hypothesis.

Who → what. J.P. Guilford distinguished convergent thinking (narrowing many possibilities to one correct answer, what IQ tests reward) from divergent thinking (generating many varied answers to an open problem, the engine of creativity). Divergent thinking is judged by fluency, flexibility, originality and elaboration.
Who → what. Ellis Paul Torrance (American psychologist, 1915–2003), often called the "father of creativity", built on Guilford's ideas to create the widely used Torrance Tests of Creative Thinking (TTCT), scoring verbal and figural tasks on fluency, flexibility, originality and elaboration.

14.10 Culture fairness and Indian adaptations

Intelligence tests can be unfair when items assume a particular language, schooling or cultural knowledge, which mainly taps crystallized ability. Culture-fair or culture-reduced tests try to minimise this, usually by relying on non-verbal, figural reasoning (for example Raven's Progressive Matrices and Cattell's Culture Fair Intelligence Test), which lean on fluid ability. In India, Western tests have been adapted and re-standardised for local languages and norms (for instance the Binet-Kamat Test by C.H. Rice, the Malin's Intelligence Scale for Indian Children, and the non-verbal Bhatia Battery of Performance Tests by Chandra Mohan Bhatia). The construction, administration and scoring of these batteries are covered in the assessment chapter; the point here is conceptual: no test is entirely culture-free, and fair measurement requires appropriate norms for the population being tested.

Why. A test standardised on urban English-speaking children will underestimate a rural child who is equally able but unfamiliar with the material. This is why fluid, non-verbal, locally normed tests exist and why cross-cultural comparison of raw IQ scores is treated with caution.

14.11 The complete map: all 30 theories of intelligence

Sections 14.2 to 14.10 gave the deep treatment to the theories a general paper hits most often. This map is the full set: every theory or model of intelligence you might see named in an aptitude paper, each tied to the person who proposed it, in one place. If a question drops a name you did not deep-read above, find it here. The theories fall into four families: factor / structural (how many abilities are there and how do they stack?), systems & cognitive-process (what does the mind actually do?), definitional & developmental (what is intelligence?), and emotional / social (intelligence beyond IQ).

Key. The single most examined fact about any theory is who proposed it and its one headline idea. Learn every row of these four tables as a "name → idea" pair and you own the whole topic, not one tenth of it.

Family A: Factor-analytic / structural theories (how many abilities, and how do they stack?)

#Theory / modelTheorist(s)Core idea in one line
1Two-factor theory (g + s)Charles SpearmanEvery task draws on one general factor g plus a task-specific factor s; positive manifold is the evidence for g.
2Sampling / "bonds" theoryGodfrey ThomsonThere is no single g; each test samples many overlapping neural bonds, and the overlap only looks like g.
3Primary Mental Abilities (7 PMAs)Louis L. ThurstoneIntelligence is several independent group factors (verbal, number, space, memory, word fluency, perceptual speed, reasoning), not one g.
4Group-factor hierarchyCyril BurtAn early hierarchy: g at the top, broad group factors beneath, specific factors at the bottom.
5Hierarchical modelPhilip E. Vernong → two major group factors v:ed (verbal-educational) and k:m (spatial-mechanical) → minor factors → specifics.
6Fluid & Crystallized (Gf-Gc)Raymond B. Cattellg splits into fluid (Gf, on-the-spot reasoning, declines with age) and crystallized (Gc, stored knowledge, holds with age).
7Investment theoryRaymond B. CattellCrystallized ability grows by investing fluid ability into learning over a lifetime.
8Extended Gf-GcJohn L. HornExpanded Cattell's two factors into 9–10 broad abilities (adding visual, auditory, short-term memory, processing speed, and more).
9Three-Stratum theoryJohn B. CarrollFrom re-analysing 460+ datasets: g (stratum III) over 8 broad (II) over ~70 narrow abilities (I).
10Cattell-Horn-Carroll (CHC)Cattell, Horn & Carroll (integrated by Kevin McGrew)The merged, dominant modern taxonomy of cognitive abilities; underlies the Woodcock-Johnson and KABC batteries.
11Structure of Intellect (SOI)J.P. GuilfordNo g; ability = operations × contents × products, yielding up to 180 distinct abilities; introduced divergent thinking.
12Radex modelLouis GuttmanAbilities arranged geometrically by complexity (simplex) and content (circumplex), with g-loaded tests at the centre.

Family B: Systems & cognitive-process theories (what does the mind actually do?)

#Theory / modelTheorist(s)Core idea in one line
13Multiple IntelligencesHoward GardnerEight (+1) autonomous intelligences (linguistic, logical-mathematical, spatial, musical, bodily-kinaesthetic, interpersonal, intrapersonal, naturalistic, ?existential), each with its own neural base.
14Triarchic theoryRobert J. SternbergThree parts: analytical (componential), creative (experiential), practical (contextual) intelligence.
15Successful Intelligence / WICSRobert J. SternbergLater reframing: balance analytical, creative and practical abilities (plus wisdom) to reach your own goals in your own culture.
16PASS theoryJ.P. Das, Jack Naglieri & J.R. KirbyIntelligence = four processes: Planning, Attention, Simultaneous, Successive; operationalised by the CAS.
17Three functional units (neuropsychological)Alexander R. LuriaThe brain works in three blocks (arousal, coding, planning); the foundation PASS was built on.
18Bioecological modelStephen J. CeciMultiple cognitive potentials are realised only through context and knowledge; there is no single context-free g.
19Minimal cognitive architectureMike AndersonIntelligence = a basic processing-speed mechanism plus two specific processors and dedicated modules.
20Two-level theoryArthur R. JensenLevel I (associative: rote memory) versus Level II (conceptual: abstract reasoning) abilities.

Family C: Definitional & developmental theories (what is intelligence?)

#Theory / modelTheorist(s)Core idea in one line
21Cognitive-correlates / information-processingEarl HuntLinks elementary cognitive tasks (reaction time, working-memory speed) to measured verbal ability.
22Practical-judgement definitionAlfred BinetIntelligence = judgement, comprehension, reasoning and the ability to criticise one's own conclusions.
23Global-capacity definitionDavid WechslerThe global/aggregate capacity to act purposefully, think rationally and deal effectively with the environment.
24Adaptation / developmental viewJean PiagetIntelligence is biological adaptation to the environment through assimilation and accommodation, maturing in stages.
25Intelligence A / B (/ C)Donald O. Hebb (C added by Philip Vernon)A = innate genetic potential, B = intelligence actually shown in behaviour, C = the sample a test score captures.
26Social intelligenceEdward L. ThorndikeSplit intelligence into abstract, mechanical and social (understanding and managing people).

Family D: Emotional & social intelligence (beyond IQ)

#Theory / modelTheorist(s)Core idea in one line
27Ability model of EIPeter Salovey & John D. MayerEI is a real ability to perceive, use, understand and manage emotions; testable with right/wrong items (MSCEIT).
28Mixed / competency model of EIDaniel GolemanEI blends emotional abilities with personality competencies: self-awareness, self-regulation, motivation, empathy, social skill.
29Emotional-Social Intelligence (EQ-i)Reuven Bar-OnCoined "EQ"; a mixed model of intrapersonal, interpersonal, adaptability, stress-management and general-mood competencies.
30Trait EI (emotional self-efficacy)K.V. PetridesEI treated as a cluster of personality traits measured by self-report, distinct from the ability model.
Trap. Do not merge people who share a lineage. Cattell made the fluid/crystallized split, Horn broadened it, Carroll stacked it into three strata, and only the three together make the CHC model. Likewise Salovey and Mayer built the scientific ability model of EI while Goleman popularised the looser mixed model and Bar-On coined "EQ": the name in the question tells you which model is meant.
Recap. Intelligence has no single agreed definition; Wechsler's "global capacity to act purposefully, think rationally and deal effectively with the environment" is the standard textbook line. Psychometric theories range from Spearman's single g, through Thurstone's seven primary mental abilities, Cattell's fluid/crystallized split, Vernon's hierarchy and Guilford's Structure of Intellect, to the modern CHC synthesis. Systems theories broaden the idea: Gardner's multiple intelligences, Sternberg's triarchic (analytical, creative, practical) successful intelligence, and Das's brain-based PASS theory after Luria. IQ evolved from Binet and Simon's mental age, through Stern and Terman's ratio IQ (MA ÷ CA × 100), to Wechsler's deviation IQ (mean 100, SD 15) on a normal curve. The extremes are intellectual disability (defined by intellectual and adaptive deficits) and giftedness (Terman's Termites). Heredity and environment both matter and interact, with twin and adoption studies showing substantial heritability while the Flynn effect proves environmental power. Emotional intelligence splits into Salovey and Mayer's ability model and Goleman's popular mixed model, and creativity rests on Guilford's divergent thinking and Torrance's tests. Remember the three classic traps: g versus multiple intelligences, fluid versus crystallized, and ratio versus deviation IQ. And 14.11 is the safety net: all 30 theories of intelligence, each tied to the person who proposed it, in four families, so no named theory in the paper can catch you out.

15 The attack plan & the interview

You have now been through the seven core areas. This closer is how you turn the reading into marks, plus the second stage most candidates under-prepare: the interview.

The written-test attack order

A general aptitude paper is won on breadth and speed of recognition, not on any single deep topic. Spend your remaining prep like this:

  1. Lock the founder pairings. Across Sections 5, 6 and 8 there are perhaps sixty "who made this" facts (Wilhelm Wundt / the first lab, Alfred Binet / the first intelligence scale, Hermann Rorschach / the inkblot, Joseph Wolpe / systematic desensitization, Carl Rogers / person-centred therapy, Aaron Beck / cognitive therapy, Ivan Pavlov / classical conditioning). These are the cheapest, most frequent marks in a general paper.
  2. Own the classification landmarks. DSM-5-TR vs ICD-11, Jean Piaget's four stages with ages, Erik Erikson's eight stages, Abraham Maslow's hierarchy, the neurotransmitter-to-disorder links. High-frequency, high-certainty.
  3. Keep stats shallow but correct. Mean vs median under skew, reliability vs validity, Type I vs Type II error, when to use a t-test vs ANOVA vs chi-square. A general paper rarely asks you to compute; it asks you to pick the right tool.
  4. Answer everything. No SGT negative-marking rule is published, so a blank is a guaranteed zero while a considered guess is not. Never leave one.
Key. If you only had one evening, you would re-read every teal Who → what box in this book and nothing else, and you would still clear most of a general aptitude paper. That is where this format concentrates the marks on purpose.

The interview: the second gate

SGT's entrance is written test then personal interview. The interview is not a formality; it is a selection stage, and it is the part you can most reliably control because it rewards clarity, not recall. Panels for an RCI MA Clinical Psychology seat tend to probe the same things.

What they probeWhat a strong answer sounds like
Why clinical psychologyA specific, honest reason grounded in your own experience, not "I want to help people." Name what pulled you toward the clinical, assessment-and-therapy side over counselling or research.
Why an RCI courseShow you know that RCI licensure is what lets you practise clinical psychology in India, and that SGT's MA is the RCI-recognised route.
Core concept checksBe able to define, in one clean sentence each: what a clinical psychologist does vs a psychiatrist vs a counsellor; what CBT is; what reliability and validity mean; one ethical principle.
A named model or therapistHave one therapy you can talk about with some depth (pick from Section 6, e.g. Aaron Beck's cognitive therapy or Carl Rogers's person-centred approach) so a follow-up does not catch you flat.
Ethics & self-awarenessConfidentiality and its limits, informed consent, knowing your scope. Panels like a candidate who knows what they do not yet know.
Why this matters. Two candidates can score the same on the written test; the interview is where the seat is actually decided. Ten minutes of rehearsing your "why clinical psychology" answer out loud is worth more per mark than another pass over stats.
Who → what, the interview version. If they ask you to name people, the safe, high-value names to have ready are: Sigmund Freud (psychoanalysis), Carl Rogers (person-centred / unconditional positive regard), Aaron Beck (cognitive therapy), Albert Ellis (REBT), Ivan Pavlov (classical conditioning), B.F. Skinner (operant conditioning), Jean Piaget (cognitive development), and for India, the Rehabilitation Council of India as the licensing body.
Recap. SGT is a two-stage, breadth-first general-psychology entrance with no published syllabus, so this book prepped the seven areas it samples rather than a topic list. Win the written test on the founder pairings and the classification landmarks, keep stats shallow but correct, answer every question because no negative marking is published, and treat the interview as a real selection gate you can rehearse. You are already prepped for the content; this book just gathered it in one place. Good luck.
